At times this created within her moods of introspection which veered dangerously near the morbid. At other times it inculcated a deep and passionate scorn of those who were her own and her race’s oppressors. She chuckled when she read or heard of or saw their imbecilities, their shortcomings. She looked with scorn on their provincialism, their stupidity, their ignorance. Conversely, she found herself magnifying the virtues, the excellencies of her own people and, at the same time, she tried to explain away through a process of subtle sophistry all their faults.

In time the continuation of these practices began to work a decided and noticeable change in her outlook on life in general. She found herself in time thinking of practically all things, it mattered not what their nature nor how remotely connected with race or colour problems they were, in terms of race or colour. This distorted vision seriously handicapped her but she was unaware of it for it coincided so completely with the viewpoints of her friends. “Poor white trash” became the ultimate of scorn, of contumely, of disparagement. In marked contrast with her former jovial and friendly manner she became almost malicious in little cruelties to tradesmen, to hucksters, to clerks in stores who happened to be white. In these episodes she took acute delight in the fact that her cream-coloured skin, her Gallic name and her French accent gave her immunities she might not have possessed had she been more distinctly Negro.

When first she had come to Atlanta she had been captiously critical of the foibles and petty vices of her new friends. She had recoiled from their bearing of tales, their tearing down of reputations on scanty and imperfect reports of alleged shortcomings. She had hated the obsession of the men on the making of money, the vying of woman with woman in dress, in grandeur of entertainment and of homes. But with the passing of years and particularly after the scenes she had witnessed in the rioting, she began to take these as a matter of course and she found they no longer shocked nor annoyed her. She did not know if she were becoming inured to the new order, if she were succumbing to the new point of view.

===Chinese===

米米此后便把自己“身为有色人”的自觉，定在1906年9月。在她看来，旧秩序已经过去，她如今确定无疑地属于一个被分隔开来的种族。这有时在她心里激起种种内省的情绪，危险地近于病态。有时又灌输给她一种深深的、炽烈的蔑视——对那些压迫她本人、压迫她种族的人。读到或听说到、亲眼见到他们的愚蠢和短处，她便暗自发笑。她鄙夷他们的偏狭、愚蠢、无知。反过来，她发觉自己把本族人的美德、优胜越拔越高，同时又绞尽一套巧妙的诡辩，把他们的一切过失统统开脱掉。

久而久之，这些习气的持续，开始给她的人生观带来一种显著可见的变化。到后来她发觉，自己思考几乎一切事情——不管事情性质如何、与种族肤色问题相隔多远——都用种族和肤色来想。这副畸形的目光严重地拖累了她，她自己却浑然不觉，因为它与她的朋友们契合得太完全了。“穷白佬”（poor white trash）成了蔑视、羞辱、贬损的极点。同她往日随和友好的做派形成鲜明对照的是，她对碰巧是白人的掌柜、小贩、店员，几乎恶毒地耍些小小的刻薄。在这些插曲里，她满心得意于这样一点：她那奶油色的皮肤、高卢人的姓氏和法国口音，给了她一层豁免权——倘若她的黑人特征更明显些，这豁免是未必有的。

刚到亚特兰大那阵，她对新鲜友们的怪癖和小毛病是挑剔的。她们搬弄是非，凭着道听途说的所谓短处拆人名誉，她曾为此退缩；男人们对捞钱的痴迷、女人与女人之间在衣饰、排场和宅子上的攀比，她曾为之厌恶。可随着岁月流逝，尤其在那场暴乱中亲眼见过那些场面之后，她开始把这些看作理所当然，发觉它们再也惊不动她、恼不着她了。她不知道，自己是在对新秩序渐渐硬了心肠，还是在新观点面前缴了械。

===Original===

Her association with Jean and the lack of contact in her youth with children of her own age had given her a maturity of mind far in advance of her years. She was pained now when she realized that her talks with Jean were becoming more and more infrequent and when they did come they were somewhat less understanding than they had been before. Her sense of loyalty kept her from thinking consciously that Jean was being rapidly left behind by the procession of events, that she who once had seen life eye to eye with him was far beyond him. But though she would not admit it, nevertheless it was true and therein lay the reason why both he and she sought less and less for the old comradeship which had meant so much to both.

Jean, however, saw with clear eye the changes being wrought in Mimi. Her new attitude towards race grieved him though he fully understood the reasons for that change. He knew that a blind race obsession would materially arrest her development and he deplored its rapid growth. He knew that argument and direct attack would be of little avail﻿—Mimi’s face on that memorable and terrible night had revealed to him the bitter travail of soul which was going on underneath. Nor would he have argued against her feeling of hatred towards the whites with whom she came into infrequent contact, had he felt that good would have come of it.

===Chinese===

她与让的来往，加上幼年缺少同龄伙伴，给了她一颗远远超前于年龄的成熟心智。如今她痛苦地意识到，她同让的交谈越来越稀少，即便有，也不如从前那样彼此会心了。她的忠心使她不肯有意识地想：让正被事变的行列迅速甩在身后，她这个曾与他见解一致的人已远远越过了他。可尽管不肯承认，事实终究是事实，这也就说明了，为什么两人都越来越不去寻回那份对他们意味着一切的旧日情谊。

然而，让用清晰的眼睛看着米米身上正在发生的变化。她对种族的新态度使他痛心，尽管他完全明白这变化的缘由。他知道，一种盲目的种族偏执会实实在在地阻碍她的成长，他为它的急速滋长而痛惜。他也知道，争辩和正面进攻都无济于事——那个难忘而可怕的夜晚，米米的脸已经向他揭示出底下进行着怎样痛苦的灵魂产痛。对她在罕有接触的那些白人身上生出的恨意，他也绝不会去争辩阻挡——倘若争辩能有什么好处的话。

===Original===

He sought instead to counterbalance it through carefully casual remarks regarding the genuine worth of this man or that one, to place in Mimi’s hands, or where she would surely come upon them, articles, newspapers, books which would fill her mind with beauty and truth. Once it was an excellent translation of the short stories of Balzac. Another time it was *The Way of All Flesh*. Or a Thomas Hardy novel, though he kept from her *Tess*﻿—that, he felt, was too﻿—well, a little too advanced for one of Mimi’s tender years. He did not know that before they had left New Orleans Mimi had spent many hours locked in her own room while she surreptitiously devoured (almost literally, her nose was so close to the pages) the tragedy of Tess. Nor did he know she had read every one of the morocco-bound volumes of Maupassant he had so carefully placed on the topmost shelf. Now Mimi read (or reread) the volumes he gave her, she listened to his long talks and she answered the questions he at the most unlikely moments would ask her about her reading, for she knew it would have given him pain had she not done so.

And Jean’s strategy was not wholly a failure. It gave Mimi a sense of almost malicious delight when she found in classrooms that she was more or less familiar with a sonnet of Shakespeare, that she knew who Chaucer was, that Tennyson was something more than the shadowy figure of a man who had “written poetry or painted pictures or something.” Only with Hilda did she refrain from stressing, ever so gently, the advantages she possessed in having chosen a father like Jean. With Hilda she shared the stories that gave her pleasure, with Jean’s permission she loaned Hilda books and therein was built another link between them.﻿ ﻿…

To the trumpet-heralded tune of the martial “God of our fathers, whose almighty hand﻿ ﻿… ,” Mimi and Hilda, clad in white, filmy dresses identical of material and design with thirty others, marched forth one sunshiny June morning when the new century had reached its eleventh year. In their hands were clutched rolls of paper, each tied with a bow of ribbon. The required tasks for completion of the normal school course had been done, the seal of official approval was bestowed. There were presents and congratulations, parties and sighs of relief that it was all over.

===Chinese===

他转而设法用另一头去压秤：貌似随意地谈几句这个人或那个人真正的好处；把文章、报纸、书籍放进米米手里，或放在她定会撞见的地方，好把美与真灌进她的头脑。有一次是一部上佳的巴尔扎克短篇小说译本。又一次是《众生之路》（*The Way of All Flesh*）。或者一部托马斯·哈代的小说——只是《苔丝》他扣着不给——那部，他觉得，对米米这般稚龄的人未免太——嗯，稍嫌太超前。他不知道，还在新奥尔良的时候，米米就曾多少个钟头反锁在自己房里，偷偷吞读（真算得上吞，鼻尖几乎贴上纸页）苔丝的悲剧。他也不知道，他小心安放在最高一层书架上的那几卷摩洛哥革精装的莫泊桑，她一本不落地全读过了。如今米米读（或者重读）他给她的书，听他长长的谈话，回答他在最想不到的时刻向她提起的关于阅读的问题——因为她知道，她若不做，会叫他伤心。

让的策略并非全然失败。在课堂上发现自己对莎士比亚的某首十四行诗多少并不陌生、知道自己晓得乔叟是何许人、知道丁尼生不只是“写过诗还是画过画反正是那么个人影”的时候，米米心里泛起一种近乎恶意的得意。只有对希尔达，她才克制着不去轻轻渲染自己有幸选了让这样的父亲的好处。对希尔达，她分享带给她快乐的故事，经让允许，还借书给她——这又成了两人之间的一条纽带。…

在一支军乐嘹亮的号声中——《我们祖辈的上帝，你全能的手……》——新世纪的第十一个年头，一个阳光明媚的六月早晨，米米和希尔达随三十来个一模一样装束的姑娘，穿着白纱似的衣裙列队而出。她们手里各攒着一卷纸，系着缎带打的结。师范课程规定的学业均已修完，官方嘉许的印信已然颁下。有礼物，有道贺，有聚会，也有一场总算结束的如释重负的叹息。

===Original===

Jean sat in the throng of parents and relatives in the seats reserved for them, his eyes moist as he watched his tiny Mimi pass another milestone between that day when he had anxiously hovered outside Margot’s door and womanhood. As the music rolled its sonorous way upward, growing more and more faint as the huge chorus followed the graduates from the chapel, he lived once more the twenty short years that had so miraculously sped by on winged feet.

It was as though it were yesterday he had stood waiting eagerly for some word from Margot’s room. As he had heard her agony he had hated this newcomer who was causing his Margot to descend into the shadows. When finally he had been admitted to the room he had rushed to the bed, refusing even to look at the intruder, and had fallen on his knees weeping bitter tears of suffering and relief. He had taken a solemn oath Margot would never go through the ordeal again, an oath which later the doctor had confirmed as necessary﻿—another child would have killed Margot.﻿ ﻿… The years of happiness the three of them had spent together came back to him. The first step, the first word, the first tooth. Long walks wandering through the old winding streets of New Orleans, little fat hands clutching his and Margot’s fingers. The first days of school. Margot’s illness and death. A sharp pain went through him that shook him like a convulsion.

“What’s the matter? Sick?” inquired Mrs. Daquin. “No﻿—I’m all right,” he whispered, resenting the intrusion into his reverie of her who had come after Margot.

The years of indecision, days followed by lonely nights when he had sought to achieve oblivion in the mottled green wineglass. Mary. The coming to Atlanta. Years of discontent. Years of unhappiness. He was better off, materially, of course. He had made money, it’s true, but he wondered if he would have even held his job had it not been backed by Mary’s father with his money and his influence. Good Lord, he had been slowly achieving the respectable status of solid citizenry under the combined urging and guidance of Mary and Mr. Robertson. And what was it all worth? Was any of this petty striving and scrambling worth half the things it took out of you?

===Chinese===

让坐在专设的家长亲友席的人群中，眼睛湿润了，望着他的小米米又跨过一道里程碑——从当年他在玛戈门外焦灼徘徊的那一天，到成年。乐声轰隆隆向上滚去，大合唱随着毕业生们走出礼拜堂，越来越远，越来越轻，他又把这奇迹般振翼疾飞而过的短短二十年重新活了一遍。

仿佛就在昨天，他站在那里急切地等玛戈房里的消息。听见她的痛楚，他恨透了那个把他的玛戈拖进幽暗里去的新来者。等终于被让进房去，他扑到床边，连看也不肯看那闯入者一眼，双膝跪倒，流出又苦又觉解脱的泪。他郑重起过誓：玛戈绝不再受这一遭——这誓言后来医生也确认是必要的——再生一个孩子会要了玛戈的命。…他们三人共度的那些幸福年月回到他眼前。第一步，第一个字，第一颗牙。在新奥尔良弯弯曲曲的老街里没完没了的散步，胖乎乎的小手攒住他和玛戈的手指。上学的头几天。玛戈的病和死。一阵尖锐的痛穿过他，抽搐似的摇了他一摇。

“怎么了？不舒服？”达坎太太问。“没事——我好好的，”他低声说，恨她闯进了他的追忆——她，这个玛戈之后来的人。

那些举棋不定的年月，一个又一个孤寂的夜，他曾在斑驳的绿酒杯里寻求忘却。玛丽。来亚特兰大。失意的岁月。不快的岁月。当然，物质上他好过了。钱是挣下了，这是真的，可他怀疑，若不是玛丽父亲拿钱和势力在背后撑着，他这份差事还保不保得住。老天爷，在玛丽和罗伯逊先生联手敦促与引路之下，他正慢慢熬成体面市民里扎实的一分子。可这一切又值几个钱？这等蝇营狗苟，抵得上它从你身上抽走的那一半吗？

===Original===

And Mimi﻿—she had grown away from him, too. There was between them of course the old affection﻿—he never doubted that for a minute. But the little ways that revealed the bond beneath, they were becoming fewer each passing year. Oh, well, she is young, he thought, while I’m old and away behind the new generation. He was proud of her, nevertheless. As she marched from the chapel and passed successively the long, narrow windows, the sun streaming in had made her beautiful hair burst into a wild, exotic, flaming red beauty. It stood out against the blacks and browns of the heads of the other girls like a radiant setting sun in a sky of rain clouds. He was happy she was so pretty﻿—he hoped she’d be married before he joined Margot. He had watched the little ways, the growth of tiny revealments of the warm blood which coursed beneath Mimi’s laughing exterior. He was afraid, terribly afraid, of leaving her alone. Alone? Yes, he concluded, that’s exactly what would be the case if anything happened to me. Mary and Mimi would never agree were he not there.﻿ ﻿…

“Come on, Jean,” Mary roused him. “It’s all over.﻿ ﻿… Oh, how do you do, Mrs. Lewis? Yes, it’s sad, in a way, to see our little Mimi become a young woman right before your very eyes. Yes, she did look nice﻿ ﻿… and she’s a dear﻿—Mr. Daquin and I adore her.﻿—Oh, thank you so much, Mr. Thompson.﻿—Where’s Mrs. Thompson?﻿—There she is! My dear, that was a beautiful gift you sent. I was just thanking your husband for it but I want to thank you both.﻿ ﻿…”

Here, there, everywhere Mrs. Daquin nodded or flung a cheerful, religiously proper phrase. Jean and Mimi owe all this to me, she was thinking. It was I who brought them into a civilized community.﻿ ﻿…

It was that summer that Carl Hunter came home. Mr. Hunter told Jean why.

“That boy’s been there at school all these years, spending money and having a good time. I told him if he flunked any more of his studies he would have to come home and work. I guess he thought I was fooling but he’ll see whether I am or not. Either he’s going to come back here and work or else I’m through with him.”

“Aren’t you a little hard on the boy?” Jean ventured to inquire. “He’s young yet﻿—hasn’t found himself﻿—he’ll come around in time.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

还有米米——她也离他远了。两人之间自然还有旧日的亲情——这一点他一刻也不曾怀疑。可那些显露底下情分的小小姿态，却一年比一年少了。唉，也难怪，她年轻，他想，我老了，落在新一代人后头了。但他仍为她骄傲。她从礼拜堂里列队而出，依次经过那一扇扇窄长的窗，涌进的阳光让她那头美发轰然燃起一片野性的、异域的、火焰般的红。在其他姑娘黑黢黢、棕褐色的发间，它鹤立鸡群，像雨云满天里一轮辉煌的落日。他为她这样标致而欢喜——他盼着在自己去陪玛戈之前，能看她嫁出去。他注视过米米笑语盈盈的外表底下那股热血的流动，注视过它一点点细小的泄露。他害怕，怕极了，把她孤零零留下。孤零零？是的，他断定，我要是有个三长两短，情形就正是这样。玛丽和米米没有我在中间，是永世合不来的。…

“走吧，让，”玛丽唤醒了他。“都完了。…哦，您好哇，刘易斯太太！是啊，眼看着咱们的小米米长成大姑娘了，从某个方面说，真叫人感伤。是啊，她今天真俊…她是个乖孩子——达坎先生和我疼她。—哦，多谢多谢，汤普森先生。—汤普森太太没来？—那不是她嘛！亲爱的，你们送的那份礼真漂亮。我刚谢过您先生，可我要谢就谢你们二位。…”

到处点头，东一句西一句抛着欢快的、虔敬而得体的话。让和米米的这一切都是我的功劳，她心里想。是我把他们带进了文明社会。…

就在那年夏天，卡尔·亨特回了家。亨特先生告诉了让缘故。

“那小子这些年赖在学校里，光花钱，光快活。我警告过他，功课再不及格，就回家来干活。他大概当我说着玩——他倒要看看我是不是说着玩。要么回来干活，要么我不管他。”

“对孩子是不是太狠了点儿？”让试着问。“他还年轻——还没找准自己——早晚会的。…”

===Original===

“Come around?” Mr. Hunter snorted. “He’s twenty-three now, and when I was his age I had been working for six years. No, I’m through coddling him and he’s got to strike out for himself or go under.﻿ ﻿…”

It was at a picnic late in June that Mimi saw Carl again. She remembered the curt manner in which he had greeted her at the party years before and she found on seeing him again that she still resented the cavalier way in which he had acted. She determined to treat him with the same coolness and lack of interest. As he spied her and Hilda a three-piece orchestra began to play in the covered pavilion.

“Hello,” he casually greeted them both. Mimi stiffened, it was the same word he had used five years before.

“How do you do?” she questioned but in a tone that said plainly she did not expect nor did she want an answer to her inquiry.

Carl grinned.

“My! You’re upstagy. Will you dance with me?”

“Thanks, no. You’d better dance with Hilda,” was Mimi’s answer as she turned away.

Hilda had stood there, saying nothing, but fighting to keep her eyes free from tears. She felt the electrical current that flashed between the two of her friends heavily charged with antagonism﻿—but Hilda was too wise to let that ill feeling reassure her. His five years’ absence had not changed Carl﻿—she knew he cared no more for her now than he did then. And here was Mimi, the only girl with whom she had ever felt completely happy, the girl she loved passionately, catching and holding Carl’s interest and attention. Hilda knew Mimi was not doing so deliberately. She knew Mimi too well to believe that. She knew, too, Mimi would rather have done almost anything than hurt her. Despite these reassurances of herself Hilda felt the first bitterness in her heart against her friend, the first twinges of jealousy. Her pride was hurt, it was true, but beneath that and far deeper was the pain caused by the sudden realization that Carl had never been, was not now, and never would be interested in her. She wanted to cry, to dash madly through the woods nearby, to get away from everybody and everything and cry until no more tears would come.

All this raced through her mind at Mimi’s words. She smiled bravely.

“No﻿—no. I don’t want to dance,” she lied cheerfully. “You two go ahead.”

===Chinese===

“会的？”亨特先生嗤了一声。“他今年二十三了，我在他这个年纪，已经干了六年活。不，我惯够他了，他要么自己闯出去，要么沉底。…”

六月底的一次野餐会上，米米又见到了卡尔。她记得几年前那次聚会上他招呼她的粗率样子，再见之下，发觉自己还记恨着他那副满不在乎的做派。她打定主意，也用同样的冷淡和不層回敬他。他一眼望见她和希尔达时，带顶棚的音乐台里一支三件头的乐队奏了起来。

“哈罗，”他随随便便向她们俩打招呼。米米绷紧了——五年前他用的正是这一个词。

“你好？”她回问，语气却明明白白：她并不指望、也不想要这句问话的答复。

卡尔咧嘴一笑。

“嚄！你好大的架子。肯跟我跳个舞吗？”

“谢谢，不跳。你最好跟希尔达跳。”米米答着，转过身去。

希尔达一直站在那儿，一言不发，只拼命忍着不让眼里涌出泪来。她感到两位朋友之间闪过的那股电流充着满满的敌意——可希尔达太明白了，这点恶感安慰不了她。离别五年没有改变卡尔——她知道他如今待她，同当年一样没那份心思。而这里站着米米，唯一一个她同她在一起感到完全快乐的姑娘，她挚爱的姑娘，却一下子攫住并占住了卡尔的兴味和注意。希尔达知道米米不是故意的。她太了解米米，不会这么想。她也知道，米米几乎什么都肯做，就是不肯伤她。尽管这样一遍遍宽自己的心，希尔达心里还是生出了对朋友的第一缕怨，第一阵嫉妒的刺痛。她的自尊受了伤，这是真的；可在那底下更深的地方，是突然认清卡尔从来不曾、现在不曾、永远也不会对她有兴趣而引起的疼。她想哭，想疯了一样冲进近旁的树林，躲开所有的人所有的事，哭到再也流不出眼泪。

这一切，在米米说话的当口从她心里疾驰而过。她勇敢地笑了。

“不——不，我不想跳，”她快活地撒了个谎。“你们俩去吧。”

===Original===

She watched them leave trying hard to keep from herself the certainty that her best and dearest friend had created in an instant, with two or three meaningless words, an interest which she had hoped and dreamed she would arouse. It was hard﻿—terribly hard. Consciously, she held Mimi blameless. But underneath it was a bitterness, a feeling almost of nausea, a pain that made her think of the Spartan youth smiling while his abdomen was being gnawed underneath his tunic or shirt or whatever they wore in those days. The figure gave her a bit of cold comfort. She toyed with the idea, turning it over and over in her mind as she went and sat silent with her mother.﻿ ﻿…

Mimi was wholly unaware of the emotions she and Carl had roused in Hilda. She knew of Hilda’s carefully shielded affection for him, but she had been so absorbed in her own reactions towards him she had neither noticed the expression on Hilda’s face during the brief episode nor had she caught the too elaborately casual way in which Hilda had urged her to dance with Carl. As she walked with him towards the pavilion all her old resentment against him surged through her. She detested his cool and aloof superiority, his calm assumption that she would gladly dance with him. Carl too was silent. His thoughts were of this girl who was Mimi, yet was not the Mimi he had only casually noticed before. Her flaming hair intrigued him, he had always loved that torch-like shade of gold and red. And she had a temper, too. That was fine﻿—he liked folks to show some spirit.

They danced. Mimi loved to dance. She found herself forgetting her resentment, she had to keep reminding herself that she was angry. And Carl did dance well. No stepping awkwardly all over her feet as did most of the other boys. The big bass fiddle moaned. The violin chirped. The mandolin pertly strummed. Every few minutes the players joined in the song. From near at hand came the smells of meat being barbecued. With it mingled the fragrancy of pines and the shout of children. It was going to be hard to remain aloof.

The music stopped. Its spell broken, she found her resentment rising. But Carl only stood and grinned happily at her.

“That’s the best dance I ever had,” he announced.

“How many times and to how many different girls have you said that?” she countered.

“Many, many times and to countless girls! But I meant it each time I said it.”

===Chinese===

她望着他们走开，拼命想把那个确信从心里摁下去：她最亲最好的朋友，只用两三句无意义的话，就在一瞬之间唤起了她原本盼望着、梦想着自己来唤起的那种兴味。这很难受——难受极了。有意识的那一面，她判定米米无罪。可底下是一股苦味，一种近乎恶心的心绪，一种疼痛，使她想起那个斯巴达少年——肚腹在束腰外衣底下（或者衬衫，反正他们那时穿什么）被啃噬着，脸上还挂着笑。这幅形象给了她一点冷冷的安慰。她摆弄着这个念头，翻来覆去，然后走过去，默默坐在母亲身边。…

米米全然不觉她和卡尔在希尔达心里搅起了什么。她知道希尔达那份小心护着的爱，可她自己对他的反应把她吸得太牢，那一幕短短的经过里希尔达脸上的神色她没留意，希尔达劝她同卡尔跳舞时那副过分做作的随意，她也没听出来。同他走向音乐台的一路上，她对他的旧怨全涌了上来。她憎恶他那冷淡孤高的优越，憎恶他那种笃定她乐意同他跳舞的从容假定。卡尔也沉默。他想的是这个姑娘——是米米，却又不是他从前只漫不经心瞟过几眼的那个米米。她那头火焰般的头发勾住了他，他一向爱那种火把似的金红颜色。她还有脾气。那更好——他就喜欢人有点血性。

他们跳了舞。米米爱跳舞。她发觉自己在忘掉怨气，只得不断提醒自己：我在生气。卡尔也确实跳得好。不像别的多数男孩，把她的脚踩了个遍。大低音提琴呜呜咽咽。小提琴啁啾。曼陀林泼辣辣地弹。乐师们隔几分钟就齐声加入歌唱。近处飘来烤肉的香气，松林的芬芳和孩子们的呼喊混在其中。要保持疏远，可真难。

乐声停了。魔力一散，她觉出怨气又抬了头。可卡尔只是站在那儿，快活地朝她咧嘴笑。

“这是我跳过的最好的一支舞，”他宣布。

“这话你说过多少回、对多少个姑娘说过了？”她顶了一句。

“多少回，对数不清的姑娘！可我每一回说的都是真心话。”

===Original===

At any rate he didn’t protest he had never said such a thing before.

“Shall we sit over there and talk? I’d like to,” he asked, simply.

They sat down and watched the crowd. Over near the pavilion sat a little cluster of middle-aged women, heads close together but not so close that they missed anything going on around them.

“The anvil chorus commences,” Carl commented, nodding his head at the group. “The old tabbies will now proceed to dissect every soul around here.”

“Oh, they mean no real harm. Why bother about them? That’s about all they care about,” Mimi easily remarked.

“Why? The hit dog yelps. They’ve begun again on me right where they left off when I went away to school nearly six years ago. I am discovering that I’m an extremely worthless fellow﻿—I smoke cigarettes, I don’t see much use in going to church and listening to a lot of platitudes about religion mixed with bum philosophy and worse science﻿—the direct opposite of most of the things I learned in school. I haven’t sense enough to listen to it and keep my mouth shut﻿—I go around mouthing my own disagreement with a lot of ignorant preachers!”

“Why go to the trouble of expressing your own opinions? Why not ignore the things that annoy you by their falseness? It would be much easier﻿—”

“Ignore them? Lord, I’ve tried a thousand times and just when I think I’ve tied my tongue, out pops an objection and I’m in hot water again. My dad’s disgusted with me﻿—he says I’m a conceited, useless young fool and I’m more than halfway inclined to believe he’s right. He’s yanked me out of college ’cause I flunked math and physics again even though I did fairly well in languages and literature and one or two other things. He says I’ve got to go to work in the insurance business or he’s going to wash his hands of me.”

“What makes you so restless﻿—makes you kick against the traces all the time?” Mimi found herself taking delight in siding with Mr. Hunter against Carl.

===Chinese===

至少他没有辩白说自己从没说过这种话。

“咱们坐那边聊聊好吗？我想聊聊，”他直截了当地问。

他们坐下看人群。音乐台近旁坐着一小簇中年妇人，脑袋凑在一处，可又没凑得太拢，四周的风吹草动一样也逃不过她们。

“铁砧大合唱开始了，”卡尔朝那堆人点点头评论道。“这班老长舌妇这就动手，把这儿每个人的灵魂都解剖一遍。”

“哦，她们也没什么真恶意。理她们干嘛？她们操心的也就这么点事了，”米米轻巧地说。

“为什么？挨了棍子的狗才叫唤。她们又拿我开刀了，正好从我六年前出门上学时掠下的地方接着来。我正在领教：我是个一钱不值的家伙——我抽纸烟，我看不大上教堂，不爱听人把宗教的陈词滥调掺上蹩脚哲学和更蹩脚的科学讲上一大套——同我在学校学的大多数东西正好相反。我又没那份涵养听完还闭紧嘴巴——我偏要四处嚷嚷，跟一帮无知的传道人唱反调！”

“何必费劲发表自己的意见呢？那些叫你恼火的东西，不理睬不就完了？那容易得多——”

“不理？天哪，我试过一千回了，每回刚觉得舌头拴住了，一句反驳就蹦出来，我又成了热锅上的蚂蚁。我爸嫌死我了——他说我是个自负、无用的年轻蠢货，我倒有一大半倾向于信他说得对。他把我从大学里拽了出来，就因为我数学、物理又不及格，虽然我语文、文学还有一两门学得还不错。他说我必须进保险公司干活，不然他就跟我一刀两断。”

“你干嘛这么躁动不安——老是轭里踢腾？”米米发觉自己竟站到了亨特先生一边，跟卡尔作对，且以此为乐。

===Original===

“I don’t know,” Carl answered, a faint note of bafflement, almost of despair, creeping into his voice. “All my life I’ve been just what you called me a kicker. I’ve tried to conform﻿—but all the time I find myself saying: ‘Is *that* so?’ when some greybeard starts to lecture me. And most of the things that everybody accepts as axiomatic, as truths never to be questioned, seem to me false and absolutely without value. In college I ran across a passage one day from Spinoza that fits it exactly where he says that ‘truths are the falsehoods grown hoary with age.’ ”

“You’re just pessimistic﻿—you’ll settle down in time,” counselled Mimi, finding herself interested in spite of herself.

Carl picked up her words.

“Pessimistic! Settle down! I’ve heard those words with variations a thousand times! Why should I settle down? If I’d only work like a slave for ten or fifteen years, make some money, marry some respectable but dull girl who’d have a lot of babies and get fat in a few years, and then drop into a rut from which I’d never get out, then I’d meet the approval of all those tabbies over there as a ‘fine, upright, Christian young man’!” His last five words were filled with distaste for the respectability the community wished for him.

“I don’t want a lot of money,” he went on vehemently. “I want to see things and live things and﻿—and﻿—” He peered at Mimi, somewhat doubtfully and hesitantly, then blurted it out as though he expected to be spanked: “write.” It was almost a shout of defiance. To Mimi there was much of the childish in his gesture of defiance, but there was also something fine about it. The burden of the conversation of the other boys of her acquaintance usually was: “When I’m making such-and-such a number of dollars” or “When I’m a doctor and have established a big practice.” Carl interested her much more than she had believed he would. Indeed, she had not thought about his interesting her. There was some, thing of her own spirit in his defiance of local mores, his words made her realize just how deeply she herself had sunk into the rut of conventional standards.

“But why can’t you write or do whatever you want to do and at the same time work in your father’s insurance business?” she asked sympathetically. “We’re living in a material world﻿—money *is* necessary﻿—and you don’t have to sell your soul simply to earn a living, do you?”

===Chinese===

“我说不上来，”卡尔回答，嗓音里渗进一丝困惑，近乎绝望。“我这辈子就是你说的那种轭里踢腾的马。我试过随大流——可每逢哪个老先生开腔训我，我总发现自己在心里说：‘*真的*吗？’而大家公认为公理、认为天经地义不容置疑的大多数东西，我看都是假的，一钱不值。在大学里有一天我碰到斯宾诺莎的一段话，正说中了这一点，他说：‘真理不过是年老发白的谬误。’”

“你这是悲观——过阵子就安分了，”米米劝道，发觉自己不由得起了兴致。

卡尔抓住她的话头。

“悲观！安分！这些话连着各色变体我听过一千遍了！我凭什么要安分？只消我像奴隶似的苦干十年十五年，挣下点钱，娶个本分而乏味的姑娘，生一窝孩子，几年就吃胖，然后跌进一道再也爬不出来的车辙，那时候，那边那群老长舌妇就会夸我了，夸我是个‘品行端正的好基督徒青年’！”最后五个字里满含着他对此地要他有的那份体面的厌恶。

“我不要大钱，”他激烈地接着说。“我要看事情，活出事情，还要——还要——”他觑了米米一眼，有点迟疑，有点踌躇，随即脱口而出，像是等着挨一顿巴掌：“写作。”那几乎是一声挑战的呐喊。在米米看来，他这副挑战的姿态里有不少孩子气，但也有一种好的东西。她相识的其他男孩，谈话的内容无非是：“等我挣下多少多少块钱的时候”，或者“等我当了大夫、挂牌生意做大了的时候”。卡尔让她感兴趣的程度，远超她自己事先的料想。真的，她原本压根没想过他会使她感兴趣。他这份对本地俗见的蔑视里，有几分她自己的精神；他的话让她明白，她自己陷进习俗标准的辙已有多深。

“可你为什么不能一边写作、干你想干的事，一边在你父亲的保险公司做事呢？”她同情地问。“我们活在一个物质的世界里——钱*是*少不了的——可挣口饭吃也不至于就得出卖灵魂，是不是？”

===Original===

“The whole thing gives me a pain and I hate it﻿—I hate it!” was his answer. “All I’ve heard since I’ve been back is a lot of men telling dirty stories and confessing the loss of their sexual powers by boasting of their amorous adventures. If it isn’t that, it’s these old women spitting out lies about every woman who isn’t present. I’ve gotten to the place I wince every time I hear something nice about a person﻿—I know it’s liable to be only the preface to a whopping big story tearing that same person to pieces.”

“You’re exaggerating﻿—you’re letting a handful give you an obsession that puts the same stamp on all of them,” she protested. “There are lots of women and men who’d fit into neither of the classes you’ve given.”

“I know it﻿—there are lots of folks like Mrs. Adams, for example, and Hilda, but the trouble is they’re the kind who attend to their own affairs. It’s this other kind that sets the example and they’re the ones who do the talking you’ve got to listen to.﻿ ﻿… Say, I guess we’d better not sit here too long or they’ll begin talking about you.”

“Let ’em,” retorted Mimi, and she was rewarded by his smile of thanks.

“Nope. You can’t afford it. But you’re the only person I know I could have talked to like this and who’d have understood.﻿ ﻿…”

Hilda sat beside her mother, silent for a long time. Seldom did her eyes leave Mimi and Carl. Though she could not hear what they were saying, she knew from the absorbed, eager way they were talking they had found some subject of deep mutual interest. To her their blissful ignoring of others was as excruciating as though red-hot irons were being applied to her own flesh. At first she furiously rejected the thought that Mimi was deliberately seeking to attract Carl. As the distant conversation continued she began to toy idly with the notion, turning it over and over in her mind, though it pained her, much as one will constantly probe a sore tooth with one’s tongue.

===Chinese===

“这一摊子事叫我头疼，我恨它——恨它！”这就是他的回答。“我回来以后听到的，净是一帮男人讲荤段子，吹自己的风流艳遇，招认自己房里的本事不行。再不就是这些老太婆，把不在场的每个女人挨个咬一遍。我现在落下了毛病，一听见夸谁，我就哆嗦——我知道那多半只是个大故事的引子，转眼就要把同一个人斯成碎片。”

“你太夸张了——你让一小撮人灌了迷魂汤，把他们打成了一路货，”她抗议道。“有许多女人男人，都套不进你说的那两类里。”

“我知道——这样的人很多，比如亚当斯太太，比如希尔达。可麻烦就在于，这种人只管自家的营生。立样板的是另外那一种，扯着嗓门非要你听个够的也正是他们。…我说，咱们别在这儿坐太久，不然该轮到她们嚼你舌头了。”

“嚼去呗，”米米回敬，得了他感激的一笑。

“不行。你担不起。不过，我认识的人里头，能这样谈话、又谈得懂的，也就你一个。…”

希尔达坐在母亲身旁，久久无言。她的目光很少离开米米和卡尔。虽然听不见他们在说什么，可从他们谈得那么专注、那么起劲的样子，她知道他们找到了彼此深感兴趣的话题。他们那份把旁人忘得一干二净的陶醉，在她就像烧红的烙铁往自己肉上烙。起初她愤然拒绝那个念头——米米是存心勾卡尔。可那场远处的谈话继续下去，她开始有一下没一下地拨弄起这个念头，在脑子里翻来覆去，尽管它疼——就像人忍不住总拿舌尖去顶那颗坏牙。

===Original===

Then she hugged the belief closely to her, firm in the conviction Mimi was maliciously trying to take Carl﻿—her, Hilda’s, Carl﻿—away from her. She knew this wasn’t true, she hated herself for her distrust of her friend. But back she went each time to that same distrust which bordered dangerously near hatred. It was like meeting a person afflicted with some facial or bodily affliction, a harelip or a glass eye, determining not to notice the deformity, yet fascinatedly unable to keep one’s gaze from returning to the very thing one determined not to watch.

Hilda was all the more ashamed of her feeling toward Mimi because, so far as Carl knew, there was no reason why Hilda should have any claim upon him. There had never been any word of love between them, he had always been rather fond of her in an offhand way, had called her, as long as she could remember, “Little Sister.” She hated the term not alone because it seemed to her banal but more because of the mild affection it implied, vastly inferior in intensity to her own emotion. Reason, however, plays but small part when one’s love is concerned, thought Hilda, and she returned to her brooding.﻿ ﻿…

“It seems to me you’re making a mistake,” Mimi meanwhile was saying to Carl. “You keep harping on the way the women here gossip﻿—that’s no practice peculiar only to Atlanta and the folks you and I know. Women do that everywhere, it seems to me, and I often think men do more gossiping than women. Does it ever occur to you there are a lot of things on the other side of the shield?”

“That’s the only reason I came back here,” Carl eagerly replied, the look of morbid unrest changing quickly to one of alert cheerfulness. “When I get most pessimistic about our own folks all I need to cheer me up is to look at unhappiness of white people. You ever notice that no matter how hard the luck he may be in, the Negro can laugh and sing and forget all his worries? They call it shiftlessness and laziness but, Lord, when I see the terribly unhappy way these white people live, so busy making money and keeping ‘the nigger in his place,’ I think we’re mighty well off. Say, would you like to go with me sometime to a camp-meeting or some of these other places where you’ll see the thing that’s kept the Negro from being wiped out﻿—this thing they call ‘spirit’ or ‘soul’ of the race?”

===Chinese===

随后她把这信念紧紧搂在怀里：米米是恶意的，存心要把卡尔——她的、希尔达的卡尔——从她手里夺走。她知道这不是真的，她为自己的不信任朋友而恨自己。可她一次次退回到那同一种不信任里，它已危险地逼近仇恨。这就像遇见一个脸面或身体上有残缺的人——兔唇，或者玻璃眼——打定主意不去注意那残缺，目光却像中了魔，偏一次又一次落回那决意不看的地方。

希尔达尤其为她对米米的这种心思羞愧，因为就卡尔所知，希尔达对他没有任何可以主张的权利。两人之间从没有过一个爱字；他对她一向只是随随便便有点喜欢，打她记事起就叫她“小妹妹”。她恨这个称呼，不单因为它听着俗气，更因为它暗含的那点温吞的情分，跟她自己的感情相比差得太远。可是，理智在爱情面前，又能占几分分量呢，希尔达想，于是又回到她的愁思里去。…

“我看你是在犯一个错误，”这当口米米正对卡尔说。“你老揪着这边的女人爱嚼舌不放——这可不是亚特兰大、不是你我熟人独有的毛病。依我看，女人到处都这样，我还常想，男人嚼得比女人更凶呢。你可曾想过，盾还有另一面？”

“这正是我肯回来的唯一缘故，”卡尔急切地应道，那副病态的躁郁神色一变而为机敏的明快。“每逢我对咱们自己人悲观到极点，我只消看看白人的不幸福，就又提得起劲来。你可注意到，黑人身处多倒霉的运道都还笑得出来、唱得出来，把烦恼忘个精光？人家管这叫没出息、叫懒，可是天哪，我一见这些白人过得那样愁苦——忙挣钱，忙着‘把黑鬼摁在他的位子上’——我就想，我们的日子好过多了。哎，哪天跟我去看看露营布道会，或者别的那些地方？在那儿你能见到那样东西——就是它使黑人没被斩尽杀绝——他们管它叫这个种族的‘精神’，或者‘灵魂’。”

===Original===

“I’d love to,” answered Mimi as they walked back towards where Hilda was sitting. Hilda was able to manage a smile but Mimi noticed all during the day that she was strangely silent and aloof. The first coolness of consequence during their friendship had come between them. Mimi was at first puzzled and wondered what might have caused it. Then she remembered Hilda’s shy admissions of her interest from childhood in Carl. Mimi knew then, or rather she sensed, the reason for Hilda’s attitude. Mimi said nothing to her, knowing the unwisdom of mentioning the subject and fearing that the breach might be widened if she spoke of it. And, being human, Hilda’s pouting irritated her.

“She’s silly,” thought Mimi, “getting angry with me simply because Carl and I talked for a few minutes. If that’s the kind of friend she is﻿—” Mimi did not complete the thought but she felt vaguely amused and annoyed at Hilda. At the same time she looked forward to talking with Carl again. He was different.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“我很愿意，”米米答。两人朝希尔达坐的地方走回去。希尔达勉强挤出一丝笑，可那一整天，米米都注意到她出奇地沉默、疏远。她们友谊里头一场真正的冷淡，头一回落在了两人中间。米米先是纳闷，想不出缘故。后来她记起希尔达红着脸招认过、她从小就惦着卡尔。米米于是明白了——不如说，感觉到了——希尔达这种态度的缘故。米米什么也没说，知道提起这个话题不聪明，怕一开口裂缝反倒更大。而且，人到底是人，希尔达的怄气也叫她着恼。

“她真傻，”米米想，“就因为卡尔同我聊了几分钟，就跟我生气。要是她做朋友是这个做法——”这个念头没想完，可她隐隐觉得又好笑又好气。与此同时，她盼着再同卡尔谈谈。他不一样。…

## VII / 七
===Original===

To Mimi there was no more fascinating thing in the life she saw around her than the song, the laughter, the deep religious faith and the spontaneous humanity of the people of whom she was now a part. In New Orleans she had been stirred by the music which had a distinctive Negro note but which had been influenced to a definite extent by French songs that made it a sort of Africanized French. Here she felt much more vividly the rhythmic surge and sweep of the Negro music untouched by other influences the ecstatic pouring forth of melodies that often were not melodies but a wild and intoxicating thing that made little chills run up and down her spine and filled her eyes with tears. With Carl she came upon new phases of this life in out-of-the-way places which had hitherto been unknown to her. She began to see and understand the deep spirituality which lay back of this people of hers, to comprehend the gifts which had enabled them to withstand oppression which would long since have crushed a weaker race.

The day when a convict gang began to repair the street in front of their home was one Mimi never forgot. She awoke hearing a wild, plaintive, poignantly simple melody so strange she thought herself yet asleep. Drawn by the music, she dressed hastily and hurried to see from whence it came. In the street a strange sight met her eyes. A crowd of Negro convicts, clad in the broad-striped and ill-fitting garb of rough material, huge balls of iron attached to their ankles by heavy chains, were tearing up the street with synchronized strokes of their pickaxes. Up and down the sidewalks strode the guards, sawed-off shotguns on their shoulders. One of these was a stalwart fellow with stooped shoulders, unshaven, his mouth stained by the tobacco he constantly chewed. Another was of shorter and stockier build, equally unshaven and tobacco-stained, one eye missing, the empty socket shrunken until it gave his face a curiously unbalanced and evil look.

===Chinese===

对米米来说，在她如今置身其间、朝夕得见的生活里，再没有什么比歌声、笑声、深沉的宗教信仰和率真天然的人情味更使她心醉的了。在新奥尔良，那种音乐曾打动过她——它带着独特的黑人音调，却又分明受了法国歌曲的影响，成了一种非洲化了的法语歌。而在这里，她更加真切地感觉到黑人音乐那未经他种影响沾染的节奏的奔涌与回荡，感觉到那一阵阵心醉神迷的倾泻——那常常算不上旋律，而是一种狂野醉人的东西，激得她脊背上一阵阵细细的寒栗，又叫她双眼噙满泪水。卡尔带她走进一些偏僻去处，接触到这种生活里她以往无从知晓的种种面貌。她开始看见并理解她这个民族底蕴里那深沉的灵性，开始懂得是什么样的禀赋，使他们经受住了足以早早压垮一个较弱民族的压迫。

一队苦役犯来修他们家门口那条街的那一天，米米终生难忘。她是被一支狂放、哀婉、质朴得动人的调子唤醒的，那调子古怪得很，她只当自己还在梦中。被乐声牵引，她匆匆穿衣，赶出去看它来自何方。街上，一幅奇异的景象映入眼帘。一群黑人苦役犯，穿着粗布做的宽条纹囚衣，不合身，脚踝上用沉重的铁链拴着大铁球，正随着整齐划一的节奏抡镐刨开路面。人行道上，看守们扛着锯短的猎枪来回踱步。其中一个是个肩膀歪塌的壮汉，胡子拉碴，嘴里被不停咀嚼的烟草染得发黑。另一个矮些、敦实些，同样胡子拉碴、烟渍斑斑，瞎了一只眼，那空了的眼窝凹陷紧缩，使他那张脸透着一种古怪的不协调的凶相。

===Original===

Up and down the guards strode, keeping ever-watchful eyes upon their charges, who worked and sang, apparently in blissful ignorance of guards or toil or any external thing. Occasionally one would stop for a drink of water brought by a remarkably ragged young Negro who nearly all the time was playing when he was most needed. The thirsty one would dawdle, invent all sorts of ingenious methods of delaying, take as long as he could in assuaging his thirst. If the operation consumed too much time there would come a warning from one of the guards, ominous, threatening, but shaken off as lightly as the water from the proverbial duck.

The convicts were of all shades and sizes and shapes. In the awkward garb they were a ludicrous, ill-assorted lot. Some there were with the shifty, roving eyes of tricksters. Others wore on their faces the hallmark of the criminal of dangerous type. The faces of a few of these bore livid scars of varying lengths gained by knifings in desperate fights. But to Mimi most of the men were kindly of expression, were obviously those arrested and convicted on petty charges because the city needed them or men like them to work its streets.

There was one item only in which this motley crowd was as one. A stalwart Negro with a ringing baritone led them in the song which had awakened Mimi. On and on he sang, verse after verse of a wildly sweet and simple song, joined in the chorus by the others. Like the beat of a giant metronome there came a grunting “Hunh!” as the shining points of the pickaxes were plunged into the red clay. There was little rhyme and little melody in the song, but rhythmically it was without flaw.

“Oh, she ast me﻿—hunh!﻿—in de parlour﻿—hunh!

An’ she cool me﻿—hunh!﻿—wid her fan﻿—hunh!

An’ she whispered﻿—hunh!﻿—to her mammy﻿—hunh!

Mammy, I love dat﻿—hunh!﻿—dark-eyed man﻿—hunh!

Well, I ast her﻿—hunh!﻿—mammy for her﻿—hunh!

An’ she said she﻿—hunh!﻿—was too young﻿—hunh!

Lawd, I wish’d I’d﻿—hunh!﻿—never seen her﻿—hunh!

An’ I wish’d she’d﻿—hunh!﻿—never been bawn﻿—hunh!

Well, I led her﻿—hunh!﻿—to de altar﻿—hunh!

An’ de preacher﻿—hunh!﻿—made us one﻿—hunh!

An’ she swore by﻿—hunh!﻿—God that’d made her﻿—hunh!

She’d never love hunh!﻿—another man﻿—hunh!﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

看守们走来走去，眼睛时刻不离他们看管的人；那些人一边干活一边唱歌，对看守、对劳苦、对外界的一切仿佛浑然不觉，快活得很。偶尔有人停下来喝水——水由一个衣衫褴褛得出奇的黑人青年端来，偏偏最需要他的时候他多半在玩耍。那口渴的便磨磨蹭蹭，变着法儿巧施拖延，把解渴的工夫拖得越长越好。若是这过程耗时太久，看守中便会有一声警告抛过来，凶险、威吓，却被轻轻一抖，像谚语里的鸭子抖落背上的水珠一样不当回事。

苦役犯们高矮胖瘦、深浅颜色，各色各样。套在那身笨拙的囚衣里，他们是一群滑稽的、七拼八凑的乌合之众。有的长着骗子才有的那种贼溜溜、乱转的眼珠。有的脸上带着危险型罪犯的印记。这一类里有几张脸带着青紫的疤，长短不一，是殊死斗殴中刀子留下的。但在米米看来，这些人多半面相和善，明摆着是因为城里需要他们、或是需要他们这样的人来修路，才以些微罪名抓来定罪的。

这杂七杂八的一群人只有一点是齐一的。一个身材魁伟的黑人领着他们唱那支唤醒米米的歌，嗓子是洪亮的男中音。他一段接一段地唱下去，唱一支狂放又甜美的质朴歌谣，其余的人在副歌处应和。闪亮的镐尖插进红土，便像一支巨大的节拍器一样，响起一声沉闷的“哼！”。那歌几乎不押韵，也谈不上什么旋律，可是节奏上无懈可击。

“哦，她问我——哼！——在客厅里——哼！
拿扇子——哼！——给我凉快——哼！
她悄悄对——哼！——她妈说——哼！
妈呀，我爱那——哼！——黑眼睛的汉子——哼！
好，我求她妈——哼！——把她给我——哼！
她说她——哼！——年纪还轻——哼！
主啊，我但愿——哼！——从没见过她——哼！
但愿她呀——哼！——压根没生下来——哼！
后来我领她——哼！——到圣坛前——哼！
牧师他——哼！——把我们配成双——哼！
她指着她——哼！——造她的主起誓——哼！
这辈子决不爱——哼！——别的男人——哼！…”

===Original===

Mimi’s heart beat faster and faster as she watched and listened. She sensed that the song carried the toilers far above their miserable lot. For them the toil and sweat, the louring guards who shouted staccato commands or flung crisp oaths when one of the convicts slackened or appeared to slacken in his labour, did not exist. She began to comprehend the thing Carl had said to her of the “over-soul” the Negro possessed.﻿ ﻿…

“Look at some of these coloured folks around you,” he had said to her, “most of them poor, having to work like dogs for a meagre living, deprived of practically every ordinary outlet in the way of amusement. Are they depressed, morbid, bitter? Not on your life! They can find amusement where nobody else can. But look at the white folks who are just about as bad off financially﻿—they’re grouchy and morose, hating everybody on earth and themselves most of all though they don’t know it.”

Mimi could see, though as yet with some difficulty, just what Carl had meant. She wondered if she were in such a plight as these convicts if she could have had the courage to sing. She was afraid she would not have been that optimistic﻿—she doubted that she could have used song even as an opiate to forget hard circumstance as these men were doing. She marvelled at their toughness of fibre which seemed to be a racial characteristic, which made them able to live in the midst of a highly mechanized civilization, enjoy its undoubted advantages, and yet keep free that individual and racial distinctiveness which did not permit the surrender of individuality to the machine.

In slavery it had kept them from being crushed and exterminated as oppression had done to the Indian. In freedom it had kept them from becoming mere cogs in an elaborately organized machine. Some people called it shiftlessness, laziness, inherent racial inferiority. Mimi herself at times had heard these charges made and frequently she had believed them and had been ashamed of them, especially during the years she had lived in Atlanta and particularly since that terrible night during the riot. The forced growth of her race-conscious attitude had accelerated this shamedness of her people’s apparent lack of assimilation by industrialism﻿—now she began to feel glad that this was so.

===Chinese===

米米看着听着，心跳得越来越快。她感觉到，这歌声载着这些劳作的人，远远高过了他们悲惨的命运。对他们来说，苦役与汗水不存在，那些阴沉着脸、一见犯人偷懒或像是偷懒便厉声短喝、甩出干脆咒骂的看守也不存在。她开始领会卡尔同她说过的那个道理——黑人身上的“超灵”……

“你瞧瞧你周围这些有色人，”他那时对她说，“多半穷得很，得像狗一样苦干才挣得一口饭吃，寻欢作乐的寻常门路几乎一概没有。他们消沉吗？阴郁吗？怨毒吗？才不呢！别人找不到乐子的地方他们都能找到乐子。再瞧瞧那些家底差不多一样薄的白人——一个个怨气冲天、阴沉沉的，恨透了世上所有人，最恨的其实是他自己，尽管他自己不知道。”

米米能够明白——虽然还有些吃力——卡尔的意思了。她想，倘若自己落到这些苦役犯这般田地，可有勇气唱歌？她怕是做不到那样达观——她怀疑自己连拿歌当麻醉剂、像这些人一样借此忘却苦境的本事都没有。她惊叹他们那种仿佛与生俱来的坚韧——那是这个种族的特性，凭着它，他们才得以生活在一个高度机械化的文明之中，享受这文明无可置疑的种种好处，却仍然保全了那份个人的、种族的独特性，不让个性向机器投降。

在奴隶制下，这特性使他们免遭压迫碾碎、免遭印第安人那样的灭绝。在自由中，这特性又使他们免于沦为一部精密组织的机器上的小小齿轮。有些人管这叫懒惰散漫、叫天生的种族低劣。米米自己就多次听人这样指摘，而且常常信了，为他们害臊——尤其是住在亚特兰大的那些年，特别是暴乱那可怕的一夜之后。她这种种族意识的被迫滋长，加速了她对本族人面对工业文明显然无力同化的羞愧——如今她倒开始觉得，幸亏如此。

===Original===

She wished she could make up her mind just what she would have them be. When she listened to Mr. Hunter and the type of hustling, progressive, acquisitive men which he represented, she doubted seriously the wisdom of the Negro’s doing anything other than acquire wealth, forge ahead in business and commerce and manufacture as many of them were doing. But when she talked with Carl or, more strongly, when she heard Negroes sing or laugh, she wondered which road would lead to greater happiness.

On Communion Sundays she went through the same process of ratiocination. The passing of the bread and wine after the sermon, the bread hard and tasteless, the wine in a huge silver cup passed from hand to hand, was followed always by a silent prayer ending with the singing of a Spiritual, “Were You There When They Crucified My Lord?” She never forgot the emotions which the song created within her as plaintive, eerily sweet, stirring verse after verse winged its way through the small church and lost itself among the age-stained rafters. For years afterward the song made her imagine she had pains in her neck and back, for the song was always sung bent forward in prayer. It was with this weariness that she associated the terrifyingly real agony of Jesus which the words evoked. “Were you there when they nailed Him to the tree?” “When they pierced Him in the side?” “When He bowed His head and died?” “When they laid Him in the tomb?”

===Chinese===

她但愿自己能拿定主意，究竟要他们成为什么样的人。听亨特先生和同他一样那一类风风火火、锐意进取、精于聚敛的人说话时，她便深深怀疑，黑人除了积攒财富、在工商实业界奋力向前——正像他们中许多人做的那样——之外再做别的是否明智。可每当她同卡尔谈话，或者更强烈地，每听见黑人唱歌、大笑，她又不禁想：哪一条路通向更大的幸福呢？

每逢圣餐礼拜日，她都要经历同一番推想。讲道之后传递饼和酒——饼又干又无味，酒盛在一只巨大的银杯里传过一双手又一双手——随后总是默祷，末了唱一首灵歌：《当我的主被钉十字架，你在那里吗？》那歌在她心中激起的情感她终生难忘：哀婉的、甜美得近乎诡异的诗节，一节又一节，振翅飞过小小的教堂，消散在年深日久、斑驳发暗的椽木之间。此后许多年里，这支歌总叫她觉得脖颈和后背隐隐作痛，因为唱这歌时人总得俯身祷告。她就是把那种疲惫，同歌词唤起的耶稣那真实得可怕的苦难联在一起的。“当他们在树上钉祂的时候，你在那里吗？”“当他们刺祂的肋旁的时候呢？”“当祂垂下头死去的时候呢？”“当他们把祂安放进坟墓的时候呢？”

===Original===

Verse after verse told with terrible inevitableness the grim story of His agony and sacrifice and to Mimi she lived each time she heard the song the tortures of the Crucified One. Always she longed for them to come to the verses of hope and happiness, when they rolled away the stone, when He rose from the dead. The rising and falling “Oh﻿—oh﻿—oh﻿—oh﻿—” which preceded the wail, “Sometimes it causes me to tremble﻿—tremble﻿—tremble,” changed now from dolour and pity and pain to an exultant, happy paean of exuberant joy in the resurrection. Always Mimi was glad to reach the open air outside﻿—the song did too many things to her down inside. Afterwards she felt as though she had been the victim of some peculiar metempsychosis﻿—that during the minutes of the song her soul had taken flight from her own body to that of some other rarer, more sentient, more delicately strung being of infinite beauty and understanding, to return only when the spell had been broken.

The other spirituals worked spells upon her of varied kinds. Carl took her frequently to out-of-the-way churches, some of them housed in miserably poor, ramshackle edifices, others in more pretentious buildings. In them all she found that deep earnestness, that abiding faith and hope and patience which she found herself able more and more to understand. Humble, simple folk most of the worshippers were, with envy and malice and smallness like all other humans, but blessed with that rare gift of lifting themselves emotionally and spiritually far, far above their material lives and selves. She was filled with inarticulate rage when she sensed all too often that the preacher was far inferior to his flock, that he played upon their emotions and fears and hopes only to earn for himself a comfortable livelihood free from too much toil. These leeches, fattening on the sacrifice of washerwomen and other humble ones who worked terrifically hard to earn their small wage, stirred in Mimi and Carl the desire to wreak some sort of vengeance upon them. Their rage usually resulted in avoiding such churches and there were many to which they never went a second time for this reason. Mimi soon was convinced she had found an accurate measuring-rod of worth in Negro preachers﻿—the worse the preacher, the longer the coat he wore.

===Chinese===

一节又一节，以可怕的必然性，讲述祂受难与牺牲的严酷故事；每听一次，米米便亲历一次被钉十字架者的酷刑。她总是盼着唱到那些希望与欢欣的诗节——滚开石头、祂从死里复活的时候。那此起彼伏的“哦——哦——哦——哦——”，随后一声哀告“有时它叫我颤抖——颤抖——颤抖”，这时便从悲怆、怜悯与疼痛，转为欢庆复活的一支欢腾、喜庆、喜不自胜的凯歌。每次唱完，米米总是庆幸能走到外面的开阔天地里去——那歌在她心底搅动的东西太多了。过后她仿佛经历了一场奇异的灵魂转世——唱歌的那几分钟里，她的灵魂仿佛飞离了自己的躯壳，栖入另一个更高贵、更有知觉、更纤敏、美与悟性都无穷的生灵，直到符咒解除方才归来。

别的灵歌也在她身上施着各样的魔法。卡尔常带她去偏僻的教堂，有些挤在破烂不堪的贫陋屋舍里，有些房子还像点样子。在所有的教堂里她都发现那种深沉的诚挚，那种恒久的信仰、盼望与忍耐——如今她越来越能够理解了。做礼拜的多是谦卑淳朴的人，也和所有别的人一样有着嫉妒、恶意与褊狭，却得天独厚，具有那种把自己在情感上、性灵上高高提升到物质生活与物质自我之上的稀有禀赋。可她时常痛感牧师远配不上他的会众，只会拨弄他们的情感、恐惧与盼望，好为自己挣一份不必过分操劳的安逸生计——每逢此时，她胸中便涌起无言的怒火。这些吸血虫，靠洗衣妇和其他辛苦挣几个小钱的卑微者的牺牲养肥自己，激起米米和卡尔一种想惩治他们一下的欲望。这怒火通常的结果是避开这样的教堂，许多教堂他们因此再没有去过第二次。米米不久便确信自己找到了一条衡量黑人牧师价值高下的准确标尺——牧师越糟，身上那件长袍越长。

===Original===

But they did not confine their visits to churches. Carl took her to parties where usually to the music of a three or four-piece orchestra of strings they danced or watched others dance with a lissom grace and abandon that intoxicated them like old, rare wines. Among all sorts and classes of their own people they rambled, oblivious of the criticism that came to them occasionally. The storm was rising though they did not know it. It broke with disturbing fury when one night Carl suggested they go to what he termed a “honky-tonky” dance. To Mrs. Daquin’s rather sharp inquiry regarding her destination, Mimi answered shortly that that was her own affair, and she thought no more of the matter at the time.

The dance was held in a small, poorly lighted and ventilated hall. The floor was so crowded they did not attempt to dance but sat and watched the others as they somewhat noisily but gaily swung and swayed about the floor. Carl acted as guide, interpreter, expositor.

“That’s what they call the ‘eagle rock,’ ” as he pointed to a dusky couple whose shoulders swayed in a fascinatingly free, peculiar rhythm. Many years afterwards when Mimi saw blonde chorus girls on Broadway swaying their shoulders and bodies as these two were doing, she remembered always where she had first seen it done. “Those two over there are ‘ballin’ the jack,’ ” Carl explained as he pointed in the throng to another couple whose knees were in motion in contrast to the shoulders of the first pair. Here, there, all around the hall he pointed out to the fascinated Mimi the originality of the dancers, moving according to no set form other than their own impulses as stirred by the weird, feet-moving strains of the Negro orchestra.

And the orchestra was doing its share with the same joy as the dancers, as though it too had paid its way in for the pleasure of playing. Carl explained that practically none of its members had ever taken a music lesson, none of them knew anything about the theory of music.

“They’ve just got instinct and rhythm﻿—and, well, what else do they need?” he ended with a smile.

===Chinese===

不过他们的足迹并不限于教堂。卡尔带她去参加聚会，通常有三四件弦乐器的乐队奏着乐，他们或亲自起舞，或看别人跳，那腰身的柔曼轻盈、恣纵酣畅，像陈年的稀世美酒一样令他们陶然。他们在自己族人的各色人等、各个阶层间穿行来去，偶尔招来的非议全不理会。风暴正在酝酿，他们却浑然不知。一夜，卡尔提议去参加他所谓的“低级酒馆舞会”，风暴便以骇人的狂怒骤然爆发。达坎太太相当尖利地追问她上哪儿去，米米简短地回一句那是她自己的事，当时也就没再放在心上。

舞场设在一间狭小、灯光昏暗、通风不良的厅堂里。地板上人挤得满满的，他们便不跳舞，坐着看别人吵吵嚷嚷却又欢欢喜喜地满场摇摆旋转。卡尔充当向导、解说员、评点人。

“那就是他们叫作‘鹰摇滚’（eagle rock）的，”他指着一对肤色黝黑的舞伴，两人的肩膀正随着一种迷人的、自由自在的独特节奏起伏。多年以后，当米米看见百老汇上金发合唱队的女郎们也这样摇摆肩头和身躯时，她总会记起自己最初是在哪里见到这跳法的。“那边那两个是在‘跳杰克舞’（ballin’ the jack），”卡尔指着人群中另一对解释道，那一对动的是膝盖，同头一对动肩膀正成对照。大厅里四面八方，卡尔指给看得入迷的米米：这些舞者的独创，不依任何固定程式，只随黑人乐队那些古怪撩人、令人脚痒的乐声，任自己的冲动而动。

乐队也同舞者一样兴致勃勃，仿佛它也是买了票进来、专为享受奏乐之乐的。卡尔解释说，队员几乎没人上过一堂音乐课，谁也不懂一点乐理。

“他们有的只是本能和节奏——再说，他们还要别的什么呢？”末了他笑着说。

===Original===

Mimi had never known that anything like this existed before. Over it all there was a primitive note, a freedom from inhibitions that gave grace and ecstasy to the dancers and the musicians. It exhilarated one and made him forget the stuffiness and dimness of the hall and the sometimes fetid smells which arose as the dance went on. She knew that here was something original﻿—she could not explain it nor could she tell how or why she felt that here was a vital and living thing. She only knew it had a vibrant, warming quality, and that was enough.

“Remember Dunbar’s lines?” Carl asked. Without waiting for a reply he recited them softly:

“Cripple Joe, de ole rheumatic, danced that flo’ frum side to middle.

Throwed away his crutch an’ hopped it, what’s rheumatics ’gainst a fiddle?

Eldah Thompson got so tickled dat he lak to los’ his grace,

Had to take bofe feet an’ hold ’em, so’s to keep ’em in deir place,

An’ de Christuns an’ de sinnahs got so mixed up on dat flo’,

Dat I don’t see how dey’s pahted ef de trump had chonced to blow.”

She smiled appreciatively at the quotation.

“It does just that to you,” she remarked. “I’m seeing better every day just what you mean.”﻿ ﻿…

It was not chance that caused Mrs. Plummer and Mrs. King to see Mimi and Carl as they left the hall. Always they took a shortcut homewards from prayer meeting which carried them past the two-storied brick building which housed on its top floor the hall.

“Look there, Mis’ King!” whispered Mrs. Plummer. “Ain’t that that Day-quinn girl with that Carl Hunter coming outa that joint?”

“It’s her, sho’s you born,” Mrs. King assured her. “I told you she’d bear watching. Playing off respectable and going in such places﻿—”

“That’s just what I say,” eagerly put in Mrs. Plummer, determined that her friend should not get ahead of her in the discovery made by her own sharp eyes. “An’ her ma’s such a good woman﻿—though they tell me she ain’t her own ma, only her step-ma. But you remember I tol’ you befo’ they come here you couldn’t depen’ on these here Cath’lics﻿—I’m a good Methodist and I ain’t got much use for Baptists but they’re regular Christians, anyhow﻿—” Mrs. Plummer shook with quick breaths, the combined result of her eagerness and the speed at which Mrs. King was hurrying down the street so that she could keep the couple ahead within sight.

===Chinese===

米米从前从不知道世上竟有这样的东西。这一切之上贯穿着一种原始的音调，一种摆脱一切抑制的自由，赋予舞者和乐手们优雅与狂喜。它使人振奋，使人忘掉厅堂里的闷热昏暗，忘掉舞跳得热烈时升腾起来的有时臭不可闻的气味。她知道这里有某种原创的东西——她说不明白，也说不清自己何以、为何感觉到这是一个有生命力的活物。她只知道它有一种震颤人心的、暖融融的特质，这就够了。

“记得邓巴的诗句吗？”卡尔问。不等回答，他轻声背诵道：

“瘸腿乔，老风湿，从场边一路蹦到场心，
扔了拐杖单脚跳，风湿算个啥，碰上小提琴？
汤普森长老乐得险些失了体面，
只好两只手把双脚死死按住，免得它们乱颠，
基督徒和罪人在那地板上搅作了一团，
号角若真吹响，我看他们怎么分得开两边。”

她会心地笑了。

“这诗说的就是这滋味，”她说。“你话里的意思，我一天比一天看得明白了。”…

普拉默太太和金太太从厅里出来时正看见米米和卡尔，这并非出于偶然。她们从祷告会回家总走一条近路，恰好经过那座两层砖楼——舞厅就设在楼上的顶层。

“快瞧，金太太！”普拉默太太悄声道。“那不是那个达坎家丫头，跟卡尔·亨特一道，从那家乌七八糟的地方出来吗？”

“就是她，错不了，”金太太一口咬定。“我早说她得留神着点儿。装得规规矩矩，却往那种地方钻——”

“我正是这么说，”普拉默太太急急插嘴，一心不叫朋友抢了先，这可是她那双尖眼睛的发现。“她妈倒是顶好的一个女人——虽说人家讲那不是她亲妈，是后妈。可你记得不，她们还没搬来我就讲过，这些个天主教徒靠不住——我是个虔诚的卫理公会教徒，浸礼会我也没有多大用处，可他们到底还算是正经基督徒——”普拉默太太急促地喘着气直抖，一半是激动，一半是金太太走得太快——她得紧紧跟着，好把前头那对年轻人收在眼里。

===Original===

“That girl’s headed straight for trouble﻿—carrying on like that!” Mrs. King affirmed. “Hurry up, can’t you? I want to see where they’re going *now*!”

“Ain’t I hurryin’ as fast’s I can?” Mrs. Plummer grumbled. “And ain’t I as anxious as you to follow ’em? Oh! They’ve gone in her house,” she complained, a note that closely approximated disappointment in her tone. The chagrin was deepened a minute later when she saw Carl leave and turn down the street towards his own home.﻿ ﻿…

It was Hilda who first told Mimi of the talk that was being spread about her and Carl. Mrs. Daquin had heard it, too, and had spoken to Jean about it, demanding that he forbid Mimi from seeing Carl again. This Jean had hesitated about doing. He trusted Mimi implicitly and he was fond of Carl. He had liked him ever since the day on which Mr. Hunter had confided in Jean his disappointment at Carl’s failure to show a keener interest in the insurance business.

With Hilda it had been different. When the story put into circulation by the Mesdames Plummer and King had first come to her, she had undergone a variety of emotions. First was indignation against those who dared say anything against Mimi. She resented, too, any disparagement of Carl for whom her affection, unexpressed though it yet remained, had grown instead of decreased since he had begun to show so deep an interest in Mimi.

And, being human, she found herself just a little bit glad this had come to them. She had two reasons for this feeling, the first of them being summed up in the words, “It serves them right!” Had Hilda cared less for Mimi and Carl, her momentary elation on hearing that they were being talked about would have been far slighter, if indeed she had felt the emotion at all. The very intensity of her passionate love for them both made her capable of greater cruelty, a vindictiveness that bordered on gloating over their descent into disfavour. A second reason for her fleeting satisfaction was wrapped in the possibility that through some means, she knew not what, there might come a break in the friendship between Carl and Mimi. Then she would, if the fates were kind, gain the interest and perhaps later the deeper affection for herself which she was sure Carl was lavishing on Mimi. Who can tell? she reflected; stranger things than that have happened.

===Chinese===

“这丫头是直奔着祸事去的——竟这样胡来！”金太太断言。“快点行不行？我要瞧瞧他们*这会儿*上哪儿去！”

“我这不正尽快赶着吗？”普拉默太太嘟囔。“我跟你不一样急着跟？哎呀！他们进她自己家了，”她抱怨道，语气里分明带着失望。一分钟后她看见卡尔离开、拐上回自家的街，懊恼就更深了。…

是希尔达头一个告诉米米外面正传着她同卡尔的闲话。达坎太太也听见了，已经同让谈过，要他禁止米米再同卡尔来往。这件事让却迟迟不肯下手。他对米米是绝对信得过的，又喜欢卡尔。打从那天起他就喜欢他——那天亨特先生私下向让吐露，说卡尔对保险生意不肯多上心，叫他很失望。

对希尔达来说情形又不同。普拉默太太和金太太放出的这段闲话头一回传到她耳朵里时，她心里百感交集。首先是愤慨，愤慨那些竟敢说米米坏话的人。任何贬损卡尔的话她也一样气恼——她对卡尔的那份情意虽然始终没说出口，自打他显出对米米这么深的兴致以来，非但没有减退，反倒更炽了。

况且人到底是人，她发现自己心里竟还有一点点高兴。这感觉有两个来由，头一个可以归纳成一句话：“活该！”倘若希尔达少爱米米和卡尔一些，听到他们被人议论时那一阵快意便会轻得多，说不定根本无动于衷。正因为她对两人爱得那么炽烈，才使她下得了更狠的狠心——那近乎幸灾乐祸，眼看他们跌进是非里而暗自快意。第二个使她一闪而过的满足得以生出的缘由则是：说不定通过什么途径——她不知道是什么途径——卡尔同米米的友谊会出现裂痕。那时她若走运，便能为自己赢得卡尔的心思，也许进一步赢得他更深的情意——她认定他如今是把这一切都倾注在米米身上的。谁知道呢？她想；比这更离奇的事都有过。

===Original===

As soon as this emotion became a conscious one, Hilda furiously rejected it as treachery of the basest kind to her friends. Filled with remorse, she rushed to see Mimi, fearing a return of the unworthy feeling. Her task there was made less easy by the somewhat cool manner in which Mimi greeted her. A very decided chilliness had entered into their hitherto warm relations which they both very distinctly dated from that day of the picnic early in the summer. Hilda through an essential honesty that governed all she did or thought found herself unable to act towards Mimi with the old cordiality and, to save both of them embarrassment, had stayed away from Mimi as much as she could. Mimi noticed this change and wondered what had caused it. Vaguely she felt it was due to her own friendship with Carl but she could not see why this should affect Hilda﻿—it was only a friendship and nothing more. She had become vexed with Hilda and hurt that Hilda by her actions had shown there was a certain distrust of Mimi’s loyalty. Thus the breach had widened and the intense pride of the two had kept each from making any advance that might have closed it.

The two girls faced each other, praying for an opening while they talked of little and unimportant things which served, rather ineffectually, as a mask for their real feelings. Ineffectually, for both knew the other was eager for some chance phrase, some little thing, which might put them at ease. Five﻿—ten﻿—fifteen minutes passed. They still talked of inconsequentialities. The air was becoming more tense. They each longed, ached desperately, for some way out of the difficult situation between them. Hilda began to wish she had not come, to think of excuses for leaving, her mission unfulfilled.

It was Mimi who in desperation and uneasiness broke the spell.

“You’ve been acting queerly all summer, Hilda,” she challenged. “Why have you acted so?” She hated herself for the awkward way in which she had spoken, the almost belligerent note in her voice when she had intended to speak with utmost casualness.

“Why?” Hilda echoed. “Don’t you know why?”

“No﻿—I can’t see﻿—Has it anything to do with Carl?” she blurted out, knowing, as she spoke, it had wholly to do with Carl.

===Chinese===

这情绪一落到自觉的层面上，希尔达便怒斥它是对自己朋友最卑劣的背叛。她满怀悔恨，赶去看米米，生怕那不体面的情绪卷土重来。可是米米待她有些冷淡，她这趟差事便不好办了。两人原本亲密的关系里透进了十分明显的寒意——她们俩都清清楚楚地把这日子算在初夏野餐的那一天。希尔达生性诚实，这诚实主宰着她的一切言行，她发觉自己无法照旧亲亲热热地对待米米，为了免得两人尴尬，索性尽量躲着米米。米米注意到了这个变化，纳闷是什么缘故。她隐隐觉得这同自己和卡尔的友谊有关，却看不出这为什么会影响希尔达——不过是友谊而已，再没别的。她对希尔达生了气，又感到伤心：希尔达用行动表明，她对米米的忠心竟存着几分不信任。裂痕就这样越拉越宽，两人又都骄傲得厉害，谁也不肯先迈出也许能弥合它的一步。

两个姑娘面对面坐着，一边扯些无关紧要的小事——那面具遮掩真实的心绪，遮掩得并不高明——一边各自祈祷着出现一个转机。不高明，因为两人都知道对方也在巴望某句随口的话、某件小小的事，好让彼此松快下来。五分钟——十分钟——十五分钟过去了。她们还在谈那些鸡毛蒜皮。空气越来越紧张。两人都渴望着、煎熬着，巴望有一条出路，摆脱这夹在中间的尴尬处境。希尔达开始后悔不该来，开始琢磨告辞的借口，使命未成便想脱身。

是米米，在绝望与不安中打破了魔咒。

“你一夏天都怪怪的，希尔达，”她发难道。“为什么要这样？”话一出口她便恨自己笨嘴拙舌，明明打算说得再随便不过，话音里却带着几乎要吵架的火气。

“为什么？”希尔达反问。“你不知道为什么？”

“不知道——我看不出——同卡尔有关系吗？”她脱口而出，话一出口就知道，这同卡尔全然有关。

===Original===

Hilda flushed and twisted her hands. Now that it was out, she felt a sense of nakedness, of being stripped bare and forced to walk through crowded streets, as she had often dreamed﻿—a dream that always awoke her with fright. Even to her mother, close as she was to her, and to Mimi she had never dared reveal the extent of her love for Carl. A natural shyness and a fear of being made or thought ridiculous had forced her to keep her real feeling to herself. Now that Mimi had put it into words, she wished fervently she had kept to her former secretiveness. Not knowing the extent to which Carl’s and Mimi’s friendship had developed, she felt she had laid herself open to ridicule. Her perturbation was answer enough for Mimi.

“But why have you acted as you have?” she pursued the inquiry. “You of all people ought to know there’s nothing between Carl and me﻿—”

Hilda silenced her with a raising of her hand.

“Tell me truthfully﻿—Mimi﻿—is that true?”

Her tone was so earnest, so appealing and fraught with apprehension, Mimi was startled.

“Of course I am telling you the truth,” she answered, irritation creeping into her voice despite her efforts to keep it out. “Don’t be silly, Hilda. And you might at least have trusted me.”

“I have trusted you﻿—I have trusted you, Mimi, and I’ve hated myself forever doubting you, but you know how I’ve always liked Carl﻿—” she ended miserably.

Mimi’s heart filled with pity and her eyes with tears. She clasped Hilda in her arms and Hilda clung to her passionately, pleading through her tears for forgiveness. All the misunderstanding was wiped away and they smiled happily through their tears. It was some time before Hilda could tell her the thing which had brought her. She felt great relief when Mimi merely laughed at the slanderous tales which were going the rounds.

“Oh, I’m not paying any attention to these gossipy old women around here. Why should I? If I had done anything of which I felt ashamed, it might worry me, but why worry one’s head over something that doesn’t exist? Life’s too short for that.”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

希尔达红了脸，绞着双手。话既然说破了，她便有一种赤身裸体的感觉，仿佛被剥得精光，被迫穿过熙熙攘攘的大街——她常做这样的梦，每次都吓得惊醒。哪怕对母亲——她跟母亲何等亲近——和米米，她也从敢吐露自己对卡尔的情意有多深。天生的羞怯，加上怕被人取笑、怕被人当作笑柄的顾虑，逼得她把真情深藏。如今米米把话挑明了，她只恨不得仍旧守住从前的缄默。她不知道卡尔同米米的友谊已到了什么地步，只觉得自己这样无异于把笑柄送到人家手里。她那份惶乱不安，对米米来说已是再明白不过的回答。

“可你为什么要这样行事呢？”米米追问。“卡尔同我之间清清白白，你是谁不好说，偏偏你最该明白——”

希尔达抬手止住她。

“对我说实话——米米——是真的吗？”

那语气那么恳切，那么哀恳，满含忧惧，米米吃了一惊。

“我当然说的是真话，”她答道，尽管竭力克制，火气还是往嗓音里钻。“别犯傻了，希尔达。你也至少该信得过我。”

“我是信了你——我是信了你呀，米米，可我一边信一边恨自己疑你，可你知道我一向喜欢卡尔——”她凄凄惨惨地收了尾。

米米心中充满怜惜，眼里噙满泪水。她把希尔达搂进怀里，希尔达也紧紧抱住她，哭着哀求饶恕。一切误会冰释，两人含泪相视而笑。过了好一会儿，希尔达才能说出她此来的正事。听米米对那些满天飞的流言只一笑置之，她如释重负。

“噢，这一带那帮长舌头的老婆子，她们的话我才不理会呢。何必呢？我要真做了什么亏心的事，兴许还会烦心；可子虚乌有的事，何必去伤那个脑筋？人生苦短，来不及呢。”…

## VIII / 八
===Original===

Mimi went to her room after Hilda had gone, and lay across the bed for a long time in deep thought. Hilda was a silly little goose for her anxiety, she thought, but she must care a great deal more about Carl than she, Mimi, had ever dreamed of. She determined to see Carl, tell him as much as was necessary about Hilda’s affection for him, and then refuse to see or go out with Carl herself. It was little enough for her to do for Hilda who had been so loyal a friend. And it would be a service, too, for Hilda’s shyness would prevent her from ever indicating to Carl that she cared for him. She herself would hate to break off the companionship with Carl which had come to mean so much to them both and from which she had derived so much. His alert mind appealed to her, the reading he had done and the eager, lovable way in which he rushed to share with her anything he had come across in his reading which he thought would interest her, his keen interest in things other than those right under his nose, his quick and unforetellable changes of mood, at one minute happy and carefree and an excellent companion, at another changing abruptly to a serious-minded, at times morose and dissatisfied individual. With a woman’s love for the unusual, the unstable, the unforeseen, she was attracted by these various moods. She had grown tired of most of the other boys of her acquaintance, for she could tell in advance, almost to the exact words, what each of them would say under any given circumstance. She had never had this feeling with Carl and he seemed to be possessed of an unending and never uninteresting variety of humours, most of them of mercurial state.

But with all these varied characteristics there was among them nothing which even remotely resembled a tendency to become mushily sentimental. Here, too, he was different from the others. Mimi had learned on several unpleasant occasions the proneness of the youth she knew to wax tender often when there was not the slightest provocation or encouragement. Their friendship had never been marred in this fashion and that was the reason, Mimi was certain, it had grown so beautifully and naturally. It had been a frank, eager companionship based on mutual interests and mutual understanding.

===Chinese===

希尔达走后，米米回到自己房里，横躺在床上，久久地陷入沉思。她想，希尔达为这种事发愁真是傻气，可她对卡尔的心意，准是比她米米过去所能想到的深得多。她决意去见卡尔，把希尔达对他的情意必要地透露一些给他，然后自己便不再与卡尔见面，也不再同他外出了。对希尔达这样一位忠心耿耿的朋友来说，她能做的实在微薄。而且这也算是一桩效劳，因为希尔达脸皮薄，是永远也不会向卡尔表示自己在意他的。她自己则实在不愿中断与卡尔的交往——这段交往对他们两人都意味深长，她也从中得益良多。他那灵敏的头脑吸引着她；他读过的书，他一读到什么自以为她会感兴趣的东西便热切可爱地跑来与她分享的那股劲儿，他对鼻子底下那些事以外的事情的浓厚兴趣，他那急速而不可捉摸的情绪变化——这一分钟还快活无忧、是个再好不过的伴侣，下一分钟便骤然变成一个心事重重、有时阴郁不满的人。怀着女人对不寻常、不安定、不可预料之物的偏爱，她被这些多样的情绪吸引住了。她早已厌倦了她相识的大多数别的男孩子，因为无论什么场合，他们每个人会说什么话，她几乎能一字不差地预先料到。她对卡尔从来没有这种感觉，他身上似乎有着无穷无尽、从不乏味的种种心绪，大半如水银一般变幻不定。

然而在这些多样的品性之中，却没有一样哪怕稍稍像是沾上了腻腻歪歪、多愁善感的苗头。在这一点上，他也与旁人不同。米米已在好几个不愉快的场合领教过，她认识的那些青年动辄便在没有丝毫挑逗或鼓励的情况下温情起来。他们的友谊从未因此蒙上污点，米米确信，这正是它长得如此美好、如此自然的缘故。那是一段建立在共同兴趣与相互理解之上的、坦率而热切的情谊。

===Original===

No, it would not be easy to give up this friendship, but she owed it to Hilda, who, in her way and as one of her own sex, had been equally if not a better friend than Carl. Certainly their companionship had been of longer duration and therefore made greater demands upon the parties to it. She was glad now there had been no word of love nor even any thought of love between her and Carl. He had never entered her mind in this guise and she could soon forget him.

But could she? The thought made her pause in the smooth course of her reasoning. Now that Hilda had brought the fact tangibly to her consciousness, was it true that her feeling towards Carl was so wholly Platonic? She began to have her doubts. It wouldn’t be so easy to give him up as she at first so glibly promised herself and Hilda. Who would there be who could take his place﻿—who could interest her as Carl had done? Knowing the futility of attempting to answer a question when there was in her own mind no question existing, nevertheless she went slowly, painstakingly through the list of all the young men she knew. One by one she discarded them until she came to the point she knew she would reach﻿—only Carl remained.

Rousing herself from the lethargy into which the circle of thought had plunged her, she went back to her original determination to end the companionship at once. She felt like a squirrel who had been madly racing in one of the barbarous, treadmill cages, rushing, but always remaining at the same point. She would give Carl to Hilda﻿—she smiled irrelevantly at the conceit of physically handing over to another one who outweighed her by many pounds and end the stupid misunderstandings at one time. She smiled again at the gratification of those who had been gossiping about her and Carl﻿—they would smugly congratulate themselves that they had caused her to break off her friendship with him, little knowing how far from the truth they were. Well, let them smile, she concluded.

Mimi went down to supper filled with a warming sense of having done something noble, sacrificial. It pleased her to have this feeling, she almost felt for herself a little of the pity of martyrdom. It was this mood which enabled her to answer Mrs. Daquin’s elaborately casual bringing up of the scandal.

===Chinese===

不，舍弃这段友谊不会是件容易事，但她欠希尔达这份情——希尔达以她自己的方式、以同性的身份，做朋友不比卡尔逊色，说不定还强过他。当然，她们的交往为时更久，因此对双方的要求也就更多。她如今庆幸自己和卡尔之间不曾有过爱的话语，甚至连爱的念头也不曾有过。他从未以这种面目进入过她的心里，她很快就能把他忘掉。

可是她能吗？这个念头使她那顺畅的思路停顿了下来。如今希尔达已把事实明明白白地带进了她的意识，她对卡尔的感情当真是全然柏拉图式的吗？她开始怀疑了。放弃他，不会像她起初对自己和希尔达轻飘飘许诺的那样容易。有谁能取代他——有谁能像卡尔那样使她感兴趣？明知自己心里其实并无这个问题、试图作答也是徒劳，她还是慢慢地、费劲地把她认识的年轻人挨个数了一遍。她一个个把他们排除，直到她知道自己必将到达的那一点——只剩下卡尔。

她从这圈思绪令她陷入的委顿中振作起来，回到原先的决心：立刻了断这段交往。她觉得自己像只在那野蛮的踏车笼里疯狂打转的松鼠，跑个不停，却始终停在原地。她要把卡尔让给希尔达——想到要把一个体重比她重出许多磅的人亲手交付给另一个人，她被这个怪念头逗得离题地笑了——并把这场愚蠢的误会一总了结。想到那帮一直拿她和卡尔嚼舌头的人将要称心快意，她又笑了——他们会沾沾自喜，以为是自己促成了她与他的决裂，却全然不知自己离真相有多远。好吧，让他们笑去，她这样收梢。

米米下楼吃晚饭，心里充满一股做了件高尚的、自我牺牲之事的暖意。有这种感觉她很受用，几乎为自己感到了一点殉道者的哀怜。正是这种心绪，使她能够从容应对达坎太太煞费苦心装作不经意地提起的那桩丑闻。

===Original===

“Oh, by the way, Mimi, I’ve been hearing something about you and Carl Hunter that doesn’t sound so nice﻿—” Mrs. Daquin began.

Mimi smiled easily at her, even encouragingly, and went on eating.

This attitude was not at all what Mrs. Daquin had expected. She had looked for tears, violent storms, protestations. In truth, she had feared what might come and her fear had kept her from mentioning for several days the stories she had heard. She had laid her plans for a gentle, casual opening and had steeled herself for an outburst. Mimi’s placidity made her feel uncertain, rather foolish.

“Yes?” Mimi inquired sweetly, too sweetly Mrs. Daquin thought, when the opening was not followed up. “Just what did you hear?”

Mrs. Daquin looked appealingly at Jean but he to all outward signs was intently examining the plate of salad before him. No help obviously to be expected from that quarter, Mrs. Daquin braced herself and proceeded.

“They’re saying you and Carl were seen coming out of a disreputable dance hall one night last week﻿—”

“It’s true. What of it?” broke in Mimi.

“What of it? What of it?” Mrs. Daquin’s second interrogation was almost a thin scream. “Jean, do you hear what your daughter is saying? She brazenly admits she and that Hunter boy went to a disreputable, vile place and then she asks: ‘What of it?’ ”

Mrs. Daquin’s anger was fast mastering her, despite her previously determined plan to retain mastery of the situation through calmness and scorn. Mimi’s willing admission of her guilt, as Mrs. Daquin considered it, had taken some of the wind out of her sails. Or, better, instead of a strong head wind Mrs. Daquin’s craft had been suddenly assailed by a veering wind from port. Like an inexperienced sailor she sought to bring her helm around and steer her boat into waters where there was less danger of squalls.

Jean looked up from his plate and stared hard but in not unfriendly fashion at Mimi. He answered his wife’s question without looking at her.

“Yes, Mary, I heard her. What about it, Mimi?”

“Carl did take me to the dance, papa. I wanted to see some of these new dances, hear the music, see a side of life among coloured people I had never seen before. We went there, we stayed awhile, and then Carl brought me home. Do you see anything wrong in that, papa Jean?” Mimi ended calmly.

===Chinese===

“哦，顺便说一句，米米，我一直听到些关于你和卡尔·亨特的闲话，听着可不怎么体面——”达坎太太开了口。

米米朝她从容地微笑，甚至带着鼓励的意味，继续吃着饭。

这副态度全然不是达坎太太所预料的。她原本料想会有眼泪、激烈的风波、连声的辩白。说实话，她一直害怕不知会闹出什么来，这份害怕使她把听来的传闻压了好几天没有提起。她原已拟好一个温和而不着痕迹的开场白，也预先打定主意要沉住气经住一场爆发。米米的平静使她没了把握，甚至觉得自己有点傻。

“是吗？”见对方没有接下去，米米甜甜地问道——达坎太太觉得甜得过了头。“您究竟听说了什么？”

达坎太太向让投去求援的目光，可他表面上正专心致志地端详着面前那盘沙拉。显然不能指望那一方伸出援手了，达坎太太鼓起勇气接着说了下去。

“她们说，上星期的一个晚上，有人看见你和卡尔从一家名声不好的舞厅里出来——”

“是真的。那又怎么样？”米米打断道。

“那又怎么样？那又怎么样？”达坎太太第二句问话几乎是一声尖细的尖叫。“让，你听见你女儿说的话了吗？她恬不知耻地承认她和那个亨特家的男孩去了那种下流的、乌七八糟的地方，还问：‘那又怎么样？’”

达坎太太的怒火迅速压倒了她，尽管她事先打定主意要以冷静与轻蔑来控制局面。米米痛痛快快地认了账——在达坎太太看来如此——这使她的气焰泄去了几分。或者不如说，达坎太太这条船迎着的本是一股强劲的顶头风，此刻却骤然换作一阵从左舷斜扑过来的风。她像个不熟练的水手，竭力转舵，想把船驶进风暴危险较小的水域。

让从盘子上抬起头，紧紧盯着米米，目光虽厉，却不含敌意。他回答妻子的问话时没有看她。

“是的，玛丽，我听见了。怎么样呢，米米？”

“卡尔确实带我去了那场舞会，爸爸。我想看看那些新式舞蹈，听听那儿的音乐，见识见识有色人中间我从未见过的一个生活侧面。我们去了，待了一会儿，然后卡尔送我回了家。您觉得这有什么不对吗，让爸爸？”米米平静地说完了。

===Original===

Before Jean could reply his wife burst in upon the conversation, able no longer to restrain her anger.

“Wrong in it? Mimi, haven’t you any sense of decency at all? Even if you didn’t do anything but what you said, think of what the neighbours will say! Think of what﻿—”

“I told my father, Mrs. Daquin, what took place, and I’m not in the habit of lying to him,” Mimi with cold fury, her eyes flaming with suppressed anger, said. “Furthermore, I resent your words ‘even if you didn’t do anything but what you said.’ ”

The two women glared at each other across the table. But Mrs. Daquin’s anger was beyond the heeding of the danger signs in Mimi’s eyes.

“Young woman, you can’t﻿—”

“Mary, keep quiet!” Jean half rose from the table, his hands pressed against it with such force all the blood had fled from his knuckles. “Mimi has told me what happened and that’s enough.”

Jean’s voice held a note which even Mary in her anger could not help but respect and fear.

“But, Jean dear,” she pleaded, her tone changing miraculously, “I’m only thinking of our good name﻿—”

“Damn your good name!” the unfamiliar Jean snapped. “I’m sick and tired of your petty bickering with these little folks you’re so mad about﻿—these busybodies so worried keeping track of other people and meddling into their affairs. As for this present matter, I want it stopped right here. Whether Mimi acted right or not, she’s told us what’s happened and that’s enough of it. Do you hear me?”

It was a long speech for Jean and it was not often that he permitted himself to become so angry: He did not know it but it gave him the opportunity of expressing his opinion of these aliens, so different to those of his own New Orleans whom he missed more and more. And his nostalgia was none the less potent and painful because he knew that even had he remained in New Orleans he would not have been happy. Just as when a man dies, his good traits or those good traits given him by popular belief are magnified after his death and his faults are minimized until they disappear altogether, so Jean remembered as the years passed only the beauties of New Orleans and he forgot the changes which had been so largely instrumental in persuading him to move to Atlanta.

===Chinese===

让还没来得及答话，他妻子便再也按捺不住怒火，闯进谈话中来。

“有什么不对？米米，你到底还有没有一点廉耻？就算你做的全像你说的那样，想想邻居们会怎么说！想想——”

“我把发生的事告诉了我的父亲，达坎太太，我没有对他说谎的习惯，”米米强压着怒火说，眼中燃着克制住的火焰。“而且，我很反感您那句‘就算你做的全像你说的那样’。”

两个女人隔着桌子怒目相视。但达坎太太的怒火已经顾不上理会米米眼中那些危险的信号了。

“小姑娘，你不能——”

“玛丽，闭嘴！”让半从桌边立起，双手撑在桌上，用力得指节上的血色都褪尽了。“米米已经把事情告诉了我，这就够了。”

让的声音里有一种连盛怒中的玛丽也不能不敬畏的调子。

“可是，亲爱的让，”她恳求道，语气奇迹般地一变，“我不过是想着我们的好名声——”

“去你的好名声！”这个变得陌生的让厉声道。“你跟那些小人物斗气斗嘴，我早就烦透了——那帮闲人，成天替别人记着行踪、插手人家的事务。至于眼下这件事，我要它就此打住。米米做得对也罢，不对也罢，她已经把事情告诉了我们，这就够了。听见我的话没有？”

对让来说，这已算是长篇大论，他也难得容许自己动这么大的气。他自己并不知道，这一下倒给了他一个机会，说出他对这些外乡人的看法——他们与他新奥尔良自家的乡亲何等不同，而他越来越想念那些乡亲。他的乡愁并没有因为一个自知而稍减其力量与苦楚：他明白，即使留在新奥尔良，他也不会快活。正如人一死，他的美德——或者说大众信以为真的那些美德——便会在死后被放大，而他的过失则被缩小，直到完全消失；随着年月流逝，让记住的也只是新奥尔良的美好，却忘掉了那些变化——正是那些变化在很大程度上促使他迁居亚特兰大。

===Original===

But, though calmed, the fire of Mrs. Daquin’s wrath, hotly burning because she feared any scandal might jeopardize her own social position, was too great to be quenched even by Jean’s outburst.

“All I’ve got to say then, Jean, if that’s all the thanks I get for trying to keep our name clean, is that Mimi had better not see Carl Hunter again, even if he’s the son of my best friend.”

Jean was about to speak again angrily, when Mimi stopped him.

“Neither of you need worry about that﻿—Carl and I won’t see each other again.”

Both Jean and his wife looked inquiringly at Mimi and waited for an explanation but none was forthcoming. But Mrs. Daquin concluded at this unexpected acquiescence that there was more to the incident than appeared on the surface. She privately concluded that Mimi had effectually hoodwinked Jean and that it was fear and guilt which made Mimi so readily consent to the breaking off of the friendship with Carl﻿ ﻿…

Her interview with Carl was not so easy as Mimi had supposed it would be. He came readily enough when she sent for him. He supposed she wanted to discuss with him the talk which had come to his ears, too. A look of amazement came over his face as she quietly told him of her resolve, followed by an expression that was almost dismay when she guardedly told him of Hilda’s affection for him.

“She likes you, Carl,” Mimi ended, “and she’s been thinking all summer that I, her best friend, have been trying to vamp you away from her. She knows now, though, there’s nothing between us. So for her sake and since this talk’s been started, we’d better see less of each other.” She added, almost as an afterthought: “I do hate, though, the satisfaction we’ll give these old tabbies.”

“But, Mimi,” Carl protested, his face eloquent of the pain within, “Hilda’s an awfully nice kid and I’m fond of her just as I’m fond of my own sister, Mildred. And I’d like to know who saw us at that dance so I could take one good punch at them!” he added with bitterness.

“It wouldn’t do any good﻿—they’d be sure then we’d done something terrible,” she counselled.

“Just now, Mimi, you said there’s nothing between us. I haven’t thought of you that way, Mimi, but﻿—but﻿—” he stammered, trying to take her hand.

===Chinese===

然而，尽管平息了下来，达坎太太胸中那团怒火——她之所以烧得如此炽烈，是因为她害怕任何丑闻都可能危及她自己的社会地位——毕竟太大，连让这一场爆发也扑不灭它。

“那么，让，我一心想保住我们名声的清白，得来的就是这样一番谢意。我只说一句：米米最好别再跟卡尔·亨特见面了，哪怕他是我最好朋友的儿子。”

让正要愤然再开口，米米止住了他。

“你们两位都不必为此操心——卡尔和我不会再见面了。”

让和他妻子都询问般地望着米米，等她解释，却没有等到。不过，达坎太太从这出乎意料的顺从中断定，这件事内情远比表面复杂。她私下认定，米米是把让蒙骗得死死的，正是恐惧与罪疚使米米这样爽快地答应与卡尔断交……

她与卡尔的谈话并不像米米预想的那样容易。她差人去叫，他倒爽爽快快地来了。他还以为她也想同他谈谈传到他耳朵里的那些闲话呢。待她平静地把自己的决定告诉他，他脸上先是一副惊讶之色，接着，当她小心翼翼地提起希尔达对他的情意时，他脸上的神情几乎近乎惊惶。

“她喜欢你，卡尔，”米米收尾道，“整个夏天她都在想，我这个她最好的朋友，一直在设法把她钟意的人从她身边勾走。不过她现在知道了，我们之间什么也没有。所以，为了她，也既然这阵闲话已经传开，我们还是少见面为好。”她又添了一句，几乎是随口想起的：“只是我真不甘心让那帮老长舌妇称心快意。”

“可是，米米，”卡尔抗议道，脸上的神情分明道出内心的痛苦，“希尔达是个顶好的姑娘，我喜欢她，就像喜欢我自己的妹妹米尔德丽德一样。我倒想知道舞会上是谁瞧见了我们，好狠狠揍他们一拳！”他又恨恨地加了一句。

“那没有用——那样他们反倒更拿准我们干了什么了不得的坏事，”她劝道。

“刚才，米米，你说我们之间什么也没有。我从来没有那样想过你，米米，可是——可是——”他结结巴巴地说着，伸手想去握她的手。

===Original===

“Don’t be silly, Carl,” Mimi laughed, moving away from him. She felt a sense of guilt, of disloyalty to Hilda that she had phrased it so crudely. As Carl repeated her words she was ashamed of them﻿—they sounded almost as though she had uttered them in the hope of a denial from Carl. “We’ve had a good time together and I’ve learned a lot and I’m grateful. You’ve given me a faith in my own people that I have never had before. Before I came to Atlanta I never thought much about ‘white people’ or ‘coloured people.’ I just thought of people as people. And then came that terrible night of the riot. After that I hated all white people and began to think every Negro was perfect even though my common sense told me I was foolish. Now I begin to see the good and the bad, in white people and coloured people﻿—and that’s something.”

But Carl heard none of the things she was saying. He sat with his face in his hands, his shoulders drooping dejectedly. He raised his head as she finished speaking. Mimi was touched with the look of pain upon it.

“Mimi,” he pleaded, “let’s forget all this silly talk and let things go on like they were before. I was wrong in taking you to that hall﻿—it wasn’t wrong in itself but wrong in the eyes of these people here﻿—so I shouldn’t have let you go. You’re the only person here I care about seeing. Oh, Mimi, don’t let’s break off here. I’m a weak and foolish creature and the only thing that’s held me up has been you.”

Desperately, despairingly, he pleaded, but Mimi, though she felt herself weakening, steeled herself against the almost overwhelming impulse to take Carl’s head into her lap and hold it to her breast as she would a child’s. The confident, rebellious man had become a child and the mother instinct, strong within her, almost overpowered her. She found herself swept by a flaming, consuming affection for Carl. Had he remained assertive, sure of himself, she would have mastered it easily. But his self-revilement, his dependency upon her touched her deeply. Love had come to her and now she must put it from her. Loyalty to Hilda and the promise she had made to her demanded it.

“No, Carl,” she firmly reiterated her resolution.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“别傻了，卡尔，”米米笑着躲开了他。她觉得有点负疚，觉得对不起希尔达，因为自己把话说得那样直白。卡尔重复着她的话时，她为这些话感到羞愧——听上去几乎像是她故意那样说，好等卡尔来否认似的。“我们在一起过得很开心，我学到了许多，我很感激。你让我对自己的族人有了信心，那是我从前从未有过的。来亚特兰大以前，我很少想到什么‘白人’或者‘有色人种’。我只是把人当人看。后来便是暴乱那个可怕的夜晚。那以后我恨所有的白人，开始觉得每一个黑人（Negro）都完美无缺，尽管常识告诉我这是犯傻。如今我开始看到白人和有色人各自的好与坏——这已经不简单了。”

可是卡尔一个字也没有听进去。他双手掩面坐着，肩膀颓然垂着。她说完了，他才抬起头来。米米被那张脸上的痛苦神情打动了。

“米米，”他恳求道，“让我们忘掉这些傻话，一切照旧吧。我带你去那家舞厅是我错了——那事情本身并没有错，可是在这帮人眼里是错的——所以我不该让你去。在这里，我唯一愿意相见的人就是你。哦，米米，我们别就在这里断了吧。我是个软弱愚蠢的家伙，一直支撑着我的，只有你。”

他绝望地、凄楚地恳求着，而米米虽然觉得自己在动摇，还是硬起心肠，抗拒着那几乎不可抗拒的冲动——想把卡尔的头揽到自己膝上，像搂孩子似的搂在怀里。那个自信而倔强的男子已经变成了一个孩子，她心中强烈的母性几乎把她压倒。她发觉自己被一股燃烧的、噬人的柔情席卷了。倘若他仍旧那样武断、那样自信，她本可以轻易克制。可他那样自责、那样依赖她，深深触动了她。爱情已经降临到她身上，而如今她必须把它抛开。对希尔达的忠义，以及她对希尔达许下的诺言，都要求她这样做。

“不，卡尔，”她坚定地重申了自己的决心……

## IX / 九
===Original===

The blazing suns of August and September cooled into the golden haze of Indian summer but its beauties brought little happiness to three young people of Atlanta. Hilda had listened with tearful gratitude to Mimi’s recital of her talk with Carl. Hopefully she had waited for some sign of interest, some word, some gesture from Carl, but day followed day and she yet was looking in vain. Languidly she prepared for the opening of school when she would begin her career as teacher, the customary interlude between student days and marriage for the girls to whom custom forbade pursuit of any other calling. Hilda passed through various periods of hope, of despair, of rage against herself, against Mimi, against Carl. At times she consoled herself that all affairs of this sort took time, that she need but wait and then her own excellencies would become so apparent, Carl would see how desirable it would be for him to follow up the opening she had made through Mimi’s intercession.

At other times she reviled herself as a hoyden, a brazen creature, a person without shame.
