Her talk with Mimi assumed gigantic proportions as a stripping bare of all her inner self, shamelessly flaunting her affection for a boy not worth the sacrifice. She castigated herself with all the cruelty of some zealot, crazed by religious fervour, who subjects himself to all manner of barbarities. Tartarus itself nor the Spanish inquisition contained such instruments of torture as those with which unrequited love can flagellate its victims, Hilda learned, and her unhappiness was not lessened by her lack of one in whom she could confide. She dared not risk further pain even from her mother, who might not understand, she thought, and whose lightest word would be as salt to a raw, quivering wound if she should speak lightly or with uncomplete understanding. Hilda’s conviction that her procedure had been wrong and very unwise, certain to drive Carl further from her than achieve any other end, only added to her unwillingness to confide even in her mother.

She thought several times of talking again with Mimi but each time, upon reflection, she rejected this procedure. As time passed with no sign from Carl other than he seemed to avoid her now almost obviously, she began to harbour a new suspicion of Mimi﻿—she wondered if Mimi had said to Carl exactly what she had told Hilda she had said.

===Chinese===

八月和九月的骄阳冷却成了小阳春金色的霭雾，可这美景却没给亚特兰大的三个年轻人带来多少快乐。希尔达含着眼泪感激地听完米米讲述她与卡尔的谈话。她满怀希望地等着卡尔表示出一点兴趣、一句话、一个姿态，可一天天过去，她仍是徒然守望。她无精打采地准备着开学的事，届时她将开始她的教师生涯——对那些习俗不许从事其他职业的女孩子来说，这是学生时代与婚姻之间惯常的一段过渡。希尔达在种种心境中辗转：希望、绝望、对自己恼恨、对米米恼恨、对卡尔恼恨。有时她安慰自己说，这一类事情都要费时间，她只需等待，到那时她自身的种种长处自会显露无遗，卡尔也会看到，顺着米米居间转圜打开的缺口再进一步，对他来说该是多么可取。

另一些时候她又痛骂自己是个野丫头、不要脸的货色、无耻之徒。她与米米的那次谈话，在她心里膨胀成了一场把内心自我剥得精光的示众，把她的深情无耻地炫示给一个不值得这份牺牲的男孩。她以某种被宗教狂热逼疯的苦行者的全部残酷来鞭笞自己，那种人会给自己施加种种野蛮的苦刑。希尔达体会到，无论是冥府还是西班牙宗教裁判所，都不曾备下单恋用来鞭笞其牺牲者的那些刑具；而她又没有一个可以倾诉的人，这份不幸也就愈发沉重。她连自己母亲那里也不敢再冒险去讨进一步的痛苦——她想，母亲也许不会理解，而母亲若随口说起、或理解得不到家，最轻的一句话也会像盐撒在鲜血淋漓、颤动不止的伤口上。希尔达确信自己的做法错了、极不明智，必定只会把卡尔推得更远而达不到任何别的结果，这更使她连母亲也不愿倾诉了。

她几次想再同米米谈谈，但每次细想之后又否定了这条路子。时间一天天过去，卡尔非但没有表示，如今几乎是明摆着在回避她，她开始对米米怀上了一种新的猜疑——她怀疑米米对卡尔说的话，是否真像她对希尔达转述的那样。

===Original===

She made for herself greater unhappiness by conjecturing phrases or sentences (or implications in phrases or sentences) that Mimi might have used in talking with him. Especially did such thoughts race through her mind after she had turned out the light at night and crept into her bed. Like a child frightened by ghosts and goblins and weird figments of the disordered brain, she shrank in terror from her own thoughts. She saw herself being laughed at by Carl and Mimi, being ridiculed as a brainless, silly little fool.

Daylight always brought relief from these tortures and regret for having entertained such thoughts. She would then rush as soon as she could to Mimi and try by unexplained little tendernesses to atone for her distrust. Mimi was often puzzled by these outbursts and she wondered what caused them. She was too wise, however, to ask directly﻿—she waited for some voluntary word from Hilda, which Hilda, in her shyness and because she was so thoroughly ashamed of her suspicions, never vouchsafed.

Nor was Mimi so free from her own worries that she could expend much thought on the problems of others. After her dismissal, Carl had resolutely avoided her, his pride too great to permit him to risk a second rebuff at her hands. Three times she had seen him approaching her on Auburn Avenue. Twice he had turned into a side street. Once he had entered a grocery store where she was certain he had no reason for going other than to avoid her. With a woman’s perversity of mind, she was annoyed that he should so explicitly obey her command that their friendship be kept on a mere disinterested plane. Mimi had never before been touched by the flaming torch of love nor even of affection despite her volatile and tender nature. The self-imposed abstention from companionship with Carl which had formed, she now discovered, so large a part of her thoughts, was resulting in the steady growth of an interest that before the rupture had been largely an impersonal one. As he continued to avoid her, Mimi determined to tell Carl how foolish she thought him to carry out her quixotic request too literally. Many days passed, though, between the making of the resolution and its execution.

===Chinese===

她靠猜想米米同他谈话时可能用过的词语或句子（或词语句子里的言外之意），给自己制造出更多的不快。夜里熄了灯、缩进被窝之后，这类念头尤其在她脑海里狂奞乱跑。像孩子被鬼怪和失调的头脑生出的怪影吓着了一样，她惊恐地畏缩着，不敢面对自己的念头。她看见自己被卡尔和米米嘲笑，被讥为没脑子的小蠢货。

天一亮，这些酷刑便解除了，她后悔竟让这些念头钻进了心里。于是她便赶紧跑到米米那儿，用种种说不清缘由的小殷勤来弥补自己的不信任。米米常常被这些突如其来的亲热弄得莫名其妙，不知是什么缘故。不过她很乖觉，不肯直问——她等希尔达自己开口，可希尔达出于羞怯，又因那些猜疑实在叫她无地自容，始终没有吐露。

米米自己也并非没有烦恼，顾不上多想别人的问题。遭她那番回绝之后，卡尔断然回避着她，他的自尊心太强，容不得再冒一次被她抢白的风险。有三次她看见他在奥本大道（Auburn Avenue）上朝她走来。两次他都拐进了横街。一次他钻进了一家杂货店，她敢肯定他进那家店毫无别的缘故，只是为了躲开她。怀着女人那种别扭的心思，她反倒恼恨他竟这样一丝不苟地遵从她的命令，把他们的友谊严格限制在纯粹无私的层面上。米米生性多情善感，却从未被爱情的烈焰乃至好感的烈焰点燃过。如今她发现，这强加于己的、不与卡尔来往的自我克制，原来在她思绪里占了那么大一块，而它正使一份情趣稳步生长——在决裂之前，那份情趣大体上是不带个人色彩的。眼见他继续回避自己，米米决意告诉卡尔，他把她的堂吉诃德式的请求执行得这样刻板，实在愚蠢。不过从下决心到付诸行动，又过去了许多天。

===Original===

Then one Sunday morning she saw him enter with his mother and sister and father the pew they always occupied near the front of the church. With all the eager impulsiveness of youth in love or youth imagining itself in love, Mimi felt a delightfully pleasant sensation in watching the back of Carl’s head, in being near him. She wanted to put in place the rebellious lock of hair which sprang in disarray from the top of Carl’s head like the feather from an Indian’s headdress. She wanted eagerly to talk with him again as they used to, she wanted to ramble from subject to subject in that comradely way which she missed now that the talks had been ended. The gossip which had partly led to that abrupt severance of their companionship had died down, though Mimi knew from experience that rumour, apparently dead and forgotten, was many lived and any misstep of the future could and would resurrect all previous reports. She knew they could arise again and always did, springing into life with new vigour like that mythical being whose name she could never remember who, thrown to earth, received new strength by contact with it, and entered the struggle again invigorated and more powerful than before.

To regain the friendship she had lost, however, she was willing to risk the wagging of tongues. She stood with bowed head watching Carl with irreverently slitted eyes as the services neared their close. “The Grace of our Lord and Saviour, Jesus Christ, the love of God the Father﻿ ﻿… now and forevermore, Amen!” droned the minister, his head tilted back, closed eyes raised to the low-hung ceiling.

The human impulse to talk, to move, to be saying or doing something, anything rather than endure introspection however slight, found gushing release as soon as the minister finished. Cloaks and wraps half donned as the benediction was being pronounced were adjusted and feminine hats given the final pat of arrangement, while little eddies of gusts of greeting and conversation flew back and forth. Comments, most of them admiring, were made on the sermon; inquiries, most of them perfunctory, regarding the health of those absent and those present; remarks on the weather, all the petty chitchat which serves as the mucilage that holds together everyday social relations.

===Chinese===

后来一个星期日的早晨，她看见他随母亲、妹妹和父亲走进教堂前排他们一贯坐的那排座位。怀着恋爱中的青年、或自以为恋爱的青年那股热切的冲动，米米望着卡尔的后脑勺，只觉得离他这样近，心里泛起一种说不出的愉快。她想把卡尔头顶上那撮乱蓬蓬、像印第安人头饰上的翎毛般翘起的头发抚平。她渴望再像从前那样同他聊天，渴望以那种同志式的方式从一个话题海阔天空地聊到另一个话题——如今谈话既已中止，她才发现自己多么怀念。那场部分导致他们突然断交的流言已经平息，不过米米凭经验知道，谣言表面上死了、被遗忘了，实则有许多条命，将来任何一个失足都可能而且必定会把先前的种种传闻统统复活。她知道它们还会卷土重来，而且总会卷土重来，带着新的活力蹦跳而起，就像那个她永远记不住名字的神话人物，被掷倒在地，一接触大地便获得新的力量，重整旗鼓再入战场，比先前更加威猛。

然而，为了赢回失去的友谊，她甘冒嚼舌之险。礼拜临近结束时，她垂着头，用不敬的眯缝眼望着卡尔。“愿我主救主耶稣基督的恩典、天父上帝的慈爱……从今日直到永远，阿们！”牧师拖长声调念着，头向后仰，闭着的眼睛抬向低垂的天花板。

说话、走动、总得说点什么做点什么的人类冲动——只要能逃避哪怕最轻微的自省——在牧师一念完便找到了宣泄的出口。还在念祝福词时就半披半挂的外衣斗篷这时穿戴停当，女人的帽子得到最后的整理一拍，而一阵阵寒暄和交谈的小旋涡来回飞卷。人们对讲道发表评论，多半是称赞；对缺席的和在场的人的健康致以问候，多半是客套；再谈谈天气——所有这些琐碎的闲聊，正是把日常社会关系黏合在一起的胶水。

===Original===

Mimi maneuvred her way from the pew into the aisle, timing it so exactly that she reached the end of the seat just as the Hunters were passing up the aisle. She greeted them inclusively but her eyes were on Carl. He sought to avoid her but as his parents passed on he was left standing face to face with Mimi. She smiled easily, he somewhat embarrassedly.

“I haven’t seen you lately﻿—that is, only at a distance,” she said. “I saw you enter Wright’s grocery rather hastily one day, though,” she added maliciously.

Carl flushed and seemed anxious to escape.

“Why should you see me?” he demanded, half angrily. “You practically told me to stay away from you just because of some silly old fools gossiping﻿—”

“You know that isn’t the truth,” she corrected him. “But I didn’t mean for you to avoid me as though I had some sort of contagious disease.”

Carl’s aggressiveness vanished, a tortured, hurt look in his eyes.

“It’s best things go along this way, Mimi,” he pleaded. “If we can’t be the kind of friends we were, it’s wisest I stay away from you altogether.”

His earnestness, for some reason she could not explain; created within her a vague uneasiness. He went on hurriedly before she could speak, his voice lowered to prevent those near overhearing.

“I’ve done a lot of thinking during the last few weeks and I think you were right in breaking off our friendship. No, I don’t mean on account of Hilda﻿—I wish I did care for her, it would make things easier. But I don’t and that’s all there is to it. I was happy with you, Mimi, because you made me content to go along and try to satisfy Dad by working. But now I see I was just fooling myself﻿—I never will be an insurance man﻿—”

“But, Carl, you’ve no right﻿—” Mimi interrupted him.

“Right? What do I care about right? And who’s to say what is right for me and what isn’t? I’m no fool﻿—I know pretty well what I’m doing and I know I’m a pretty rotten sort﻿—you’ll know what I mean by that before long﻿—as soon as the anvil chorus starts on me again.”

“Why don’t you come to see us any more, Carl? You know papa and I’ll always welcome, you.” Neither of them noticed that Mrs. Daquin had not been included.

“No﻿—I’ve caused enough talk about you already,” he answered cryptically as he lost himself in the crowd.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

米米从座位挤进过道，时机掐得那样准，亨特一家顺着过道走过来时，她恰好在排尾同他们相遇。她把他们一并招呼了，眼睛却落在卡尔身上。他想避开她，可他父母走过去之后，他就同米米面对面站着了。她从容地微笑，他则有几分尴尬。

“近来没怎么见你——也就是说，只远远望见过，”她说。“不过有一天，我可是瞧见你急匆匆地钻进了赖特家的杂货店，”她存心刀难地补了一句。

卡尔涨红了脸，一副急于脱身的样子。

“你为什么该见得着我？”他半带怒气地质问。“就因为几个嚼舌头的老蠢货，你就简直是叫我躲开你——”

“你知道那不是实话，”她纠正他。“可我也没叫你躲我躲得好像我害着什么传染病似的。”

卡尔的挑衅劲儿没了，眼里露出备受折磨的、受了伤的神情。

“事情就这样下去最好，米米，”他恳求道。“我们要是不能做从前那种朋友，那我干脆不见你，才是最明智的。”

他的恳切，不知怎的她说不清缘由，使她心里生出一种莫名的不安。不等她开口，他匆匆说了下去，压低了嗓音，怕近旁的人听见。

“这几个星期我想了很多，我觉得你断绝我们的友谊是对的。不，我不是因为希尔达——我倒真愿意自己喜欢她，那样事情会好办些。可我不喜欢她，就是这么回事。同你在一起我很快活，米米，因为你使我心甘情愿地照老样子走下去，干活来讨爸爸的欢心。可如今我明白我只是在骗自己——我永远当不成保险业的人——”

“可是，卡尔，你没有权利——”米米打断了他。

“权利？我管什么权利？再说谁有资格说什么对我是对的、什么是不对的？我不是傻子——我很清楚自己在干什么，我也知道自己是个相当糟糕的家伙——不久你就会明白这话什么意思——只等那帮人再冲着我敲打开腔。”

“你怎么不再来看我们了，卡尔？你知道爸爸和我永远是欢迎你的。”两人谁也没有留意，这话里没把达坎太太算上。

“不——我惹你的闲话已经够多了，”他莫测高深地答道，随即消失在人群里……

===Original===

For many days Mimi thought of the talk she had had with Carl. Naturally his mysterious statements of the talk he was certain to create piqued her interest. As discreetly as she could, she sought to find out what he referred to but with no success until Mrs. Hunter called at the Daquin home a week or two later and asked to speak to Mimi, *alone*. Mrs. Daquin, bursting with curiosity, regretfully complied with the suggestion that she absent herself.

As soon as the door closed, Mrs. Hunter explained her mission. She prefaced her story with a disconcerting question.

“Mimi,” she demanded, her eyes fixed on Mimi’s face, “do you love my Carl?”

“Oh, Mrs. Hunter, why do you ask me such a question?” Mimi, startled, parried the query. She was amazed﻿—she had never asked herself so directly the question Carl’s mother had flung at her.

“Don’t be frightened or worried﻿—I was so in hopes you did care for him. His father and I are terribly worried about him﻿—he has always been a restless, queer sort of a child but as long as you and he were friendly he seemed satisfied. Oh, yes, I know the silly talk that was going around,” she put in as Mimi started to speak, “but nobody who counted ever believed that rot.”

“That wasn’t the reason we did what we did, Mrs. Hunter,” Mimi quickly broke in. “It was on account of Hilda﻿—”

“Hilda? What did she have to do with it?”

Mimi told her the story while Mrs. Hunter showed on her face the amazement she felt.

“Silly little fool!” she pontifically announced when Mimi had finished.

“She isn’t a silly little fool and she’s far too good a girl for Carl or any other boy I’ve seen around here!” Mimi hotly declared, her anger flaming high at the disparagement of her friend. It never occurred to her that she had applied the same term to Hilda or at least terms suspiciously similar in meaning.

===Chinese===

一连许多天，米米都想着她同卡尔的那番谈话。他那些神秘的话，说自己必定会招来闲话，自然勾起了她的好奇心。她尽量小心地想打听他指的是什么，却始终没有结果，直到一两个星期后亨特太太到达坎家来拜访，要求同米米单独谈话。达坎太太好奇心涨得满满的，也只得遗憾地依从了请她避开的提议。

门一关上，亨特太太便说明了来意。她先提了一个叫人心慌的问题作为开场。

“米米，”她问道，眼睛紧盯着米米的脸，“你爱我的卡尔吗？”

“哦，亨特太太，您为什么问我这样的问题？”米米吃了一惊，避而不答。她惊愕——她从未这样直截了当地问过自己卡尔母亲劈头抛来的这个问题。

“别怕，也别担心——我正巴不得你真喜欢他呢。他父亲和我为他担心得要命——他一向是个坐不住的、古里古怪的孩子，可只要同你友好，他好像就安分了。哦，是的，外头传的那些混账话我都知道，”见米米要开口，她抢着说，“可有分量的人谁也不信那些鬼话。”

“我们那样做并不是为了那个缘故，亨特太太，”米米急忙插话。“是为了希尔达——”

“希尔达？她跟这事有什么相干？”

米米把事情讲了，亨特太太脸上露出她心中的惊讶。

“傻乎乎的小傻瓜！”米米讲完，她武断地宣布。

“她才不是傻乎乎的小傻瓜呢，而且对卡尔、对我在这儿见过的随便哪个男孩子来说，她都是个好得过分的好姑娘！”米米激愤地声明，为朋友遭贬损而怒火直冒。她根本没有想到，她给希尔达用过的正是同一个字眼，至少是意思近似得可疑的字眼。

===Original===

“Oh, don’t misunderstand me, dearie,” Mrs. Hunter hastened to cover her hasty statement. “Mind you, I didn’t mean she wasn’t good enough for my Carl but that she didn’t show very good judgment. But that isn’t what I came to talk about. It’s this. Carl, since you and he stopped seeing each other, has taken to staying out nights with all sorts of people and three times this week he’s come home drunk. He’s in such a nasty temper I’ve been afraid to say anything to him and I know there’ll be a terrible scene when his father finds out about his carryings-on.”

“I’m sorry to hear Carl’s acting this way, Mrs. Hunter, but what have I got to do with it?” Mimi, puzzled, asked.

“I was wondering if you’d let him see you occasionally﻿—and﻿—and I thought maybe you cared enough to be willing to do that and see if he would stop. Oh, Mimi, he’s my boy and I want you to help me to save him from himself.”

Mimi did not know what to say. She felt sorry, terribly sorry, for Mrs. Hunter﻿—knowing her, she knew the ordeal through which the older woman was going, imperiously proud as she was, in abasing herself before a younger woman, an outsider, and confessing her weakness, her impotence in saving her own son. On the other hand there was her promise to Hilda. True, Carl had said twice he did not care and would never care for Hilda, but that made her own promise none the less binding. She felt impatience with Carl coming over her. She was not puritanical by nature but Carl’s rapid succumbing to temptation vexed her, he was destroying the ideal she had created in her mind of which he was the embodiment.

===Chinese===

“哦，别误会，亲爱的，”亨特太太赶紧弥补自己失言。“你听着，我不是说她配不上我的卡尔，而是说她眼力不怎么样。可那不是我今天来要谈的。是这么回事：自从你和他不再见面，卡尔迷上了夜里在外头跟三教九流的人混，这一个星期就有三回醉醺醺地回家。他脾气坏得吓人，我连话都不敢跟他说，我知道他父亲一旦发现他这些荒唐事，家里准有一场大闹。”

“听到卡尔这样，我很难过，亨特太太，可这跟我有什么相干？”米米困惑地问。

“我想问问你，肯不肯偶尔让他见见你——还有——还有我想，也许你对他有心，肯这样帮一把，看看他会不会收心。哦，米米，他是我的儿子，我要你帮帮我，把他从他自己的手里救出来。”

米米不知道说什么好。她为亨特太太难过，难过极了——她了解这位太太，知道这位向来傲气凌人的妇人此刻经历着怎样的煎熬：向一个比她年轻的外人低声下气，承认自己的软弱，承认自己在拯救亲生儿子这件事上无能为力。另一方面，还有她对希尔达的诺言。不错，卡尔已两次说过他不喜欢希尔达、永远不会喜欢她，可这并没有使她自己的诺言减轻半分约束。她心里对卡尔涌起一阵不耐。她天性并非清教徒，可卡尔这样快就向诱惑屈服，使她恼火——他正在亲手毁掉她心目中那个以他为化身的理想。

===Original===

Her indecisiveness was due more than to any other cause to her own lack of understanding of the full importance of Mrs. Hunter’s words. This was not because of stupidity, it was because of Mimi’s amazing innocence of life. She had never fully realized why there should have been so much talk regarding the visit she and Carl had paid to the dance hall. The people she saw there she knew were not of her class nor were they of the kind she ever encountered. Margot and Jean had closely guarded Mimi from all influences which they felt might contaminate her mind. They had seen to it that her playmates were from families which observed identically the same customs and beliefs as did the Daquins. At school Mimi had heard phrases which would have shocked her had she understood their import but, knowing nothing of what they meant, she had given them no particular thought.

The same circumstance had existed during the years in Atlanta, where it was considered highly immoral to mention even the most rudimentary facts about oneself. Mimi had asked questions but these had been more or less skillfully evaded. She had asked only once about certain amazingly distressing things she noticed in her body and the answer to that query, implying as it did that she had said something she should not have mentioned, had sealed her lips. But in her own mind she had wondered at and been distressed by these things, by periods of intense depression when the most minor incident made her cry or laugh. Thus Mrs. Hunter’s dark suggestions regarding Carl’s deviations meant little to her and she wondered that his own mother and father could be so lacking in control over him that she had to appeal to her to help save him.

“I asked Carl at church two weeks ago,” she began, Mrs. Hunter meanwhile waiting anxiously for Mimi’s decision, “why he never came to see us anymore and he told me he didn’t want to.”

“Oh, please, Mimi,” the mother pleaded, begging almost piteously for her boy, “ask him again and again until he does come. You can save him﻿—if you will.”

“I’ve asked him once and if he doesn’t want to come to see me then I won’t ask him again,” Mimi said firmly.

Mrs. Hunter rose, her eyes wet with tears. She jabbed them jerkily with a tiny square of linen, ludicrously small for one of her bulk, Mimi thought irrelevantly even as she pitied the older woman’s plight.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她之所以拿不定主意，最主要的还不是别的原因，而是她未能充分领会亨特太太这番话的全部分量。这并非因为愚蠢，而是因为米米对世事惊人地无知。她从未完全明白，为什么她和卡尔去那家舞厅的事会引起那么大的闲话。她在那里见到的人，她知道不属于她的阶层，也不是她素来会遇到的那一类人。玛戈和让一直严严实实地护着米米，不让她接触一切他们觉得会玷污她心灵的影响。他们留心让她的玩伴都出自与达坎家习俗信仰完全相同的人家。在学校里，米米也听到过一些话，倘若懂得它们的含义，准会把她吓一跳，可她全然不解其意，也就没放在心上。

在亚特兰大的这些年，情况也一样——在这儿，哪怕提及自己身上最起码的事实，也被认为极不体面。米米问过一些问题，可都被或多或少巧妙地岔开了。她只问过一次，问的是她在自己身上注意到的某些令她极为不安的东西，而那一问所得到的回答，口气等于说她说了不该提的事，从此便封住了她的嘴。可是在心里，她一直为这些事纳闷、难过，为那些阵发性的情绪低落而难过——那时节，顶细小的一件事都会叫她哭或者笑。因此，亨特太太关于卡尔越轨的那些含糊的暗示，在她听来没多少分量，她反倒奇怪，他的亲生父母怎么竟这样管不住他，要来求她帮忙救他。

“两个星期前我在教堂里问过卡尔，”她开口道，亨特太太正焦急地等着米米的决定，“问他为什么再不来看我们，他说他不想来。”

“哦，求求你，米米，”做母亲的恳求道，几乎是在为她儿子苦苦哀告，“问了一遍再问一遍，直到他来为止。你能救他——只要你愿意。”

“我问过他一次了，他既然不想来看我，我就不再问了，”米米坚决地说。

亨特太太站起身，眼里喻着泪水。她用一小方亚麻布手帕一阵急促地按着眼睛——那手帕小得可笑，与她那样魁伟的身量不相称，米米在怜惜这位妇人的窘境时，不着边际地想……

===Original===

Mrs. Daquin sought skillfully to pry from Mimi the reason for Mrs. Hunter’s visit but Mimi evaded her questions and went to her own room. Her resentment against Carl increased. It was so silly of him to act as he was doing. Mrs. Hunter was an old frump, it was true, but she was a decent old sort after all. Mimi wondered just what he had been doing. Getting drunk was pretty bad for a young man who was yet in most respects a boy. Mimi remembered Jean and his demijohn of double port-wine after Margot died and before Mary Robertson came to New Orleans. Her loyalty to Jean and the contact with intoxication having removed some of its terrors made her maintain stoutly that getting drunk wasn’t the worst sin in the world. She wondered what sort of people Mrs. Hunter was referring to when she spoke of Carl’s new associates. There had been a note in Mrs. Hunter’s voice when she said it that brought little chills of apprehension to Mimi. Suppose Carl were killed by these shadowy and horrible creatures. Mimi felt an iciness gripping her that took her breath from her. The very indefiniteness of the danger that threatened Carl made it all the more terrifying and horrific.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

达坎太太千方百计想从米米嘴里套出亨特太太来访的缘由，米米却把她的问话一一岔开，回到自己房里去了。她对卡尔的怨气更重了。他这样行事实在太傻。亨特太太固然是个古板的老古董，可到底是个正派的老派人。米米纳闷他到底干了些什么。对一个在大多数方面还是个孩子的年轻人来说，喝醉酒是够糟的了。米米想起玛戈死后、玛丽·罗伯逊还没来新奥尔良那阵子，让和他那坛双料波特酒的细颈大坛（demijohn）。对让的忠心，加上同酗酒打过一回交道、消除了它的几分可怕，使她坚持认为，喝醉并不是天底下顶顶重的罪。她不知道亨特太太说起卡尔那伙新交时指的是什么样的人。亨特太太说这话时嗓音里有个调子，给米米带来一阵阵小小的寒噤。假如卡尔被这些影影绰绰、猔狞可怕的货色害死了呢。米米感到一股冰凉攫住了她，攫得她透不过气来。威胁着卡尔的那种危险恰恰因为无形无状，才愈发可怕、愈发猔狞……

## X / 十
===Original===

It was not long before Mimi knew in greater detail the reasons for Mrs. Hunter’s apprehension. Like a great orchestra beginning pianissimo upon a symphony, the tongues started clacking in soft and cryptic whisperings. Carl, with reckless disregard of the conventions, made no attempt to conceal his derelictions, his wandering from the ultra-strict codes which governed his family and the set in which it moved. He gave the prying eyes and clattering tongues an abundance of fuel, his flaunting of local standards serving much as the upward sweep of a conductor’s baton would draw forth a great swelling tone from his players. Carl was seen no more at church despite several acrimonious discussions on the subject with his father. Twice he was seen at a moving-picture show with a girl who was déclassée﻿—years before, her name had been linked in somewhat opprobrious fashion with that of a married man of the town. It was true no proof had been produced of any wrongdoing on their part and, as was the usual custom in such affairs, the man’s connection with the embryonic scandal had been long since forgotten. With the passage of years the blot on her name had grown through the very indefiniteness of the original rumour. For years she had been considered by the good Christians of the town almost as though her offence had been that of being “a fancy woman.”

Carl had known the girl for many years, as long, in fact, as he could remember. When Mimi had refused to see him, he had by chance met and talked with the girl one day and she had interested him. Even when he said to her one day: “Why haven’t we been friendly all these years?” she had made no defence of her reputation nor had she complained of the years of ostracism she had undergone. Had she done either of these, Carl’s interest in her would probably have died instantly. Knowing in a hazy sort of way of her story, he was attracted by her calm acceptance of it, and he found himself more and more drawn to her. Neither of them thought of their friendship save as friendship. But it would have been a feat of no mean proportions to convince those who saw them together that there was nothing questionable about their relationship. Her halting suggestion to Carl that he might bring unpleasant comment upon himself if he were seen too frequently with her had angered him﻿—he resolved to go with her wherever and whenever he chose.

===Chinese===

不久，米米就较为详细地知道了亨特太太忧虑的缘由。像一支大乐队以最弱音起奏交响曲那样，舌头们先是轻轻巧巧、隐隐约约地嗒嗒敲打起来。卡尔鲁莽地无视体统，毫不掩饰自己的失检，毫不掩饰自己偏离了管束着他家和他们那个圈子的一套严苛至极的规矩。他给窥探的眼睛和聒噪的舌头提供了大量的燃料，他对本地风纪标准的公然蔑视，其作用恰如指挥棒向上一扬，从乐师们那里引出一片高涨的音响。尽管同父亲为此有过好几场吵得凶巴巴的争论，卡尔再也不上教堂了。有人两次看见他同一个跌出了体面阶层的女人看电影——多年以前，她的名字曾以颇不光彩的方式同本城一个有妇之夫的名字连在一起。的确，并没有证据表明他们有什么不轨，而按这类事情的惯例，那个男人同这桩胎死腹中的丑闻的干系也早被忘了个干净。随着年月过去，她名声上的污点反而因为当年谣言的无凭无据而愈长愈大。多年来，本城的善良基督徒们看待她，几乎就像她的罪名是当“情妇”一般。

卡尔认识那姑娘已有许多年，其实，从他记事起就认识。米米拒不见他之后，有一天他偶然遇见了她，同她聊了聊，她引起了他的兴趣。甚至当有一天他对她说“这些年咱们怎么没有好上来着？”她也没有为自己的名声辩护，也没有抱怨这些年遭受的白眼。倘若她做了这两样中的任何一样，卡尔对她的兴趣多半会当即死掉。他对她的来历只有个模模糊糊的了解，正因她处之泰然，他被吸引住了，发觉自己越来越离不开她。他们俩谁也没把这段友谊当成别的什么。可是，要让那些看见他们同行的人相信他们的关系没有暖昧，那可是一件了不起的大工程。她吞吞吐吐地提醒卡尔，若是让人太经常看见同她在一起，怕要招来闲话，这话惹恼了他——他打定主意，想去哪儿、想什么时候去，就带她去哪儿。

===Original===

This affair, together with the known fact that Carl was drinking rather heavily, was all that was needed to consign him to the outermost circles. His family’s standing saved him from complete condemnation, though at the same time it brought more criticism upon him. He knew that had he come from a less respected family little would have been said regarding his derelictions. But the fact of his dissipation, which he took little pains to conceal, only furnished the groundwork for the reputation that grew with startling speed. Bandied about over teacups and back fences, across pews and shop-counters, through telephone and letter, a character of infinitely intricate pattern was woven around him.

All this meant little to Carl, however. He knew now that his feeling towards Mimi was not the disinterested, impersonal affair he had thought it. Women were queer, he concluded. At times they can be as ruthless and as without scruples as pirates or highwaymen and then turn right around and permit farfetched and ultra-chivalrous and foolish little consciences to destroy happiness for themselves and for others. He knew Mimi did not love him but he could not understand why she had insisted, all because she felt she owed some vague sort of duty to Hilda, that they sacrifice all the happiness there had been for them in their companionship.

And while Carl was rambling through the misty and unhappy realm of his thoughts, Mimi was treading much the same path. She heard the stories of his dissipation﻿—her friends saw to it that she should hear them with all current elaborations. These, instead of having the intended effect of driving the wedge between her and Carl deeper, appealed to her vanity﻿—she rather enjoyed the romantic glow which filled her on realizing that a man was throwing his life away for love of her. She found, too, that Carl occupied an increasing part in her thoughts. At night she lay awake for hours, seeing his face, hearing his voice. The separation which she herself imposed began to act as a boomerang upon her emotions﻿—instead of holding herself in check, as she had never experienced any difficulty in doing when she was seeing Carl regularly, she now found that she could no longer keep him from her thoughts. With rapidly growing intensity these moods came upon her until the mention of Carl’s name caused a queerly delightful sensation within her.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

这件事，加上卡尔喝酒喝得相当凶这一尽人皆知的事实，便足以把他打入最外圈了。他家的地位使他免遭全然的不齿，可同时也给他招来了更多的非难。他知道，倘若他出身于一个不那么受人敬重的人家，他那点失检本不会有多少人说。可他那份几乎不加遮掩的放浪，只为那以惊人速度生长起来的名声提供了底子。在茶杯边、后院栅栏边，在教堂座席间、柜台两头，通过电话和书信，一张花样无穷复杂的品格之网就这样绕着他织成了。

不过这一切对卡尔算不了什么。他如今明白了，他对米米的感情并不是他从前以为的那种超然、不带私心的情谊。女人真是奇怪，他断定。她们有时能凶得毫无顾忌，赛过海盗和路劫的强盗，可一转身，又会放任那些牵强附会、骑士气过了头的愚蠢小良心，毁掉自己和他人的幸福。他知道米米不爱他，可他不明白，她为什么只因为觉得自己欠希尔达某种说不清道不明的义务，就非要坚持断送他们交往中曾有过的全部幸福。

卡尔在他那雾蒙蒙、闷闷不乐的思绪王国里游荡的时候，米米走的也差不多是同一条路。她听到了关于他放浪的种种传闻——她的朋友们特意让她听到，还带着时下最新的种种渲染。这些传闻非但没有按本意把她和卡尔之间的楔子砸得更深，反倒撩动了她的虚荣——意识到有个男人正为爱她而糟蹋自己的性命，她心里充盈的那点浪漫的光焰，她相当受用。她还发觉，卡尔在她思绪里占的地盘越来越大。夜里她一连几个小时睡不着，看见他的脸，听见他的声音。她自己强加的分离，如今回过头来在她自己的感情上起了回旋镖的作用——从前同卡尔正常来往时，她克制自己从不觉得费力，如今她却发现，再也没法不让他待在自己的脑子里了。这样的心绪一阵紧似一阵地袭来，到后来，单是提一提卡尔的名字，都会在她心里引起一种奇异的愉快……

---

===Original===

Mrs. Adams took her turn entertaining the Fleur-de-Lis in October. Jean objected strenuously to going but Mrs. Daquin insisted.

“It’s only once a year husbands are allowed to attend and it’s little enough to ask you to go just this once. Besides, Mrs. Adams will feel badly if you don’t go,” she had pleaded, successfully.

After they had gone Mimi sat on the porch enjoying the crisp, chilly air. Tiring, she was entering the house when she saw a familiar figure enter and pass through the circle of light cast by the corner streetlamp. She waited until the shadowy form was passing the gate. It turned and looked up at the darkened house as it passed and she was happy that it did so.

“Carl,” she called softly.

The figure stopped suddenly.

“Won’t you come in awhile?” she urged.

Carl stood indecisively at the gate, then opened it and entered. As he greeted her a mantle of constraint like a pall fell upon them both.

“Come inside﻿—it’s rather chilly out here,” she remarked.

He followed her into the parlour and sat silent while she adjusted the shades and switched on the light.

“Well, you’re back again even if I did have to shanghai you,” she laughed. The forced pleasantry and the laugh both fell rather flat, she felt.

“Why did you make me come in?” he demanded, sullenly.

“Make you come in?” she laughed again.

“Don’t try to be coy, Mimi, or evade my question,” Carl almost angrily charged. “You told me to stay away from you﻿—”

She evaded the challenge.

“I’ve been hearing a lot of talk about you lately,” she sought to change the subject.

“What of it? And what’s the use of discussing it? These people here would talk about Jesus Christ himself.”

Mimi watched him closely. Here was a newer and more bitter mood than any in which she had hithertoo seen him. And he looked badly, too. His face was haggard. Under his eyes were dark circles, and the eyes themselves were sunken, reddened. Deep lines were in his face. He looked distrait, miserable. As she watched, his face became softer and he sank it in his hands. In muffled tones he began to speak again.

“Mimi, I’m a weak, vicious creature and no good to myself or anybody else. I used to fuss a lot about these people around here who talk about other people’s affairs﻿—I still talk about them and hate them. But, after all, they’re right﻿—I’m everything vile and low they’ve said I am﻿—”

===Chinese===

十月，轮到亚当斯太太做东款待百合花会（Fleur-de-Lis）了。让极力反对去，达坎太太却坚持。

“一年里只这么一回让丈夫们出席，就求你去这么一次，也算不得什么。再说你不去，亚当斯太太要心里难受的，”她这样恳求，成功了。

他们走后，米米坐在门廊上，享受那清冽的寒气。坐倦了，她正要进屋，看见一个熟悉的身影走进街角路灯洒下的光圈，又穿过去。她一直等到那影子般的身形走过院门。它转过身，一边走一边抬头望了望这幢黑着的房子，她为此觉得高兴。

“卡尔，”她轻轻唤道。

那身影猛地站住了。

“进来待一会儿不好吗？”她怂恿道。

卡尔在门口迟疑地站了一会儿，终于推门进来了。他向她问好时，一层拘束的幕布像棺罩似的罩住了他们两人。

“进屋吧——外头相当冷，”她说。

他跟着她进了客厅，在她拉窗帘、开灯的当儿，一言不发地坐着。

“好啊，我虽然是绑架你来的，你到底又来了，”她笑了。这勉强的玩笑和这笑声都相当没劲，她感觉得出。

“你为什么叫我进来？”他阴沉沉地质问。

“叫你进来？”她又笑了。

“别扭扭捏捏的，米米，也别回避我的问题，”卡尔几乎是怒气冲冲地指控。“是你叫我躲开你的——”

她避开了这一击。

“近来我听到不少关于你的闲话呢，”她想把话头岔开。

“那又怎么样？说这些又有什么用？这地方的人，就是耶稣基督本人来了，他们也要嚼舌头的。”

米米细细地端详他。这是一种她至今从未见他有过的心境，更苦、更冷。他的气色也坏了。脸庞消瘦，眼下一圈圈乌青，眼睛本身凹陷、发红。脸上刻着深深的纹路。他一副失魂落魄、愁苦不堪的样子。她望着望着，他的脸色柔和下来，把脸埋进了双手里。他用沉闷的声音又开了口。

“米米，我是个软弱的、恶毒的东西，对自己对谁都毫无用处。我从前老为这帮四处窥探人家私事的人发火——如今我还骂他们、恨他们。可是说到底，他们没说错——他们说我下流、卑劣，我样样都是——”

===Original===

“You mustn’t talk that way, Carl. You’re nothing of the sort.”

She tried to comfort him but he stopped her with a gesture of mingled impatience and denial. His abjectness stirred her deeply. She felt again the wave of tenderness coming over her that she had experienced the last time she had talked with him in this very room, the time she had sent him away. She was a little frightened﻿—her emotion was deeper, more moving than before. His unhappiness had subtly transferred itself to her and she found herself filled with an almost overwhelming desire to touch him, to hold him near her.

Carl raised his head and looked searchingly at her. His gaze disturbed her, moved her, made her uncomfortable. She remembered the day she first met him, the inclusive way in which he had surveyed her and the resentment his look had stirred in her. There was no resentment now. Instead there was a fear he might see way down beneath and know how she had missed him, how she had longed for him.

“Mimi, there’s one thing I want to tell you now that I’m here. I feel like a dog in telling you﻿—I have no business even thinking it. But I want you to know﻿—no matter what stories you hear﻿—that﻿—that﻿—I love you!” His tone was half defiant, half tender, as he nervously flung the last three words at her.

Mimi sat silent, her eyes in her lap. With Carl’s declaration a great peace came upon her. Doubt, fear, uncertainty left her.

“And I love you, too, Carl,” she said, simply.

With a bound he crossed the room and sat beside her.

“You﻿—love﻿—me﻿—too?” he asked, amazement, doubt that he had heard aright in his voice.

Mimi, her eyes filled with tears of happiness, could only nod.

“Oh, Mimi darling,” Carl half sobbed, and pillowed his face on her breast while she pressed him close to her, holding him with all the tenderness that had been pent up within her, holding him as though he would fly away never to return if she relaxed for an instant the tightness of her grasp.﻿ ﻿…

It seemed years later when Carl left.

“Mimi, I wonder if we have done right?” he asked as he stood at the door, his voice worried, uncertain.

Mimi only smiled as she kissed him.﻿ ﻿…

---

She had been asleep for a long time when Jean and his wife returned.

===Chinese===

“你不能这样说自己，卡尔。你根本不是那样的人。”

她想安慰他，他却用一个又急又否定的手势止住了她。他那副自暴自弃的凄惨样子深深搅动了她。她又一次感到那阵涌上心头的柔情，同上一回在这间屋子里、她打发他走的那次谈话时感到的一样。她有点害怕——这一回她的感情更深，更撼动她。他的愁苦悄悄地移到了她身上，她发觉自己心里涨满了几乎克制不住的欲望，想抚抚他，想把他搂近身边。

卡尔抬起头，探询地望着她。他的目光使她慌乱，使她感动，使她不安。她记起初见他那天，他把她从头到脚一齐打量的那副架势，那目光在她心里激起的反感。如今没有反感了。取而代之的是一种害怕——怕他一眼看进她心底，知道她多么想念他，多么渴望他。

“米米，有一件事，趁我在这儿我要告诉你。告诉你我觉得自己像条狗——连这么想都是不该的。可我要你知道——不管你听到什么传闻——我——我——我爱你！”他半是挑战、半是温柔，紧张不安地把最后三个字朝她甩了过去。

米米默默坐着，眼睛落在膝上。卡尔这一番表白，一种巨大的安宁降临到她身上。疑惧、惶恐、拿不定主意，都离开她了。

“我也爱你，卡尔，”她简单地说。

他一跃穿过房间，坐到她身边。

“你——也——爱——我？”他问，嗓音里满是惊讶，满是不敢相信自己听对了。

米米眼里噙着幸福的泪水，只能点头。

“哦，亲爱的米米，”卡尔半是呜咽，把脸枕在她胸前，她则把他紧紧搂住，以她胸中积压已久的全部温柔搂住他，搂得紧紧的，仿佛她稍一松手他就会飞走、再也不回来似的……

卡尔离去时，仿佛已隔了好几年。

“米米，你说咱们做得对吗？”他站在门口问，嗓音里带着忧愁和犹疑。

米米只是微笑着吻了他。……

---

她睡下很久之后，让和他妻子才回来。

===Original===

After she had undressed and turned out the light she lay in bed and thought of Carl. Life was funny, she mused. People came into one’s life, flickered like the figures in a movie thrown on an imperfect screen and passed out of one’s consciousness leaving no memories of importance behind them. Most people were like that but every so often there came those, always few in number, who stayed and, by staying, created all sorts of difficult and unpleasant or pleasant complications. She wondered why nearly everybody she knew or had read about made simple little problems into tremendous ones and harried themselves with things which, seen later, were of such slight worth. Conscience? Right? Honour? Justice? Truth? What were all these except little shibboleths which man had created in his own mind like little gods and before which he prostrated himself in abject groveling? Truth? She remembered the thing Carl had once quoted to her from Spinoza﻿—truth is made up of the lies grown hoary with age.

But after all there must be something in these things men had lived by and for which they had died. She wondered what it all could be. Were they worth all the agony and bloodshed and sacrifice they had brought?

Here I am, she mused, a woman, a Negro. Life for me if I were white would be hard enough, but it’s going to be doubly so when I have race problems added to my own difficulties as a woman. She toyed idly with the notion as to what her lot would have been if she had been born white﻿—if she were to cross over the line and forget the Negro blood in her body. The idea was not attractive. In New Orleans the women who attracted her most and whom she admired above all others were not white﻿—at least, they were not Anglo-Saxon. They all lived in the Creole quarter and Jean had pointed out many of them who had Negro blood, some of them knowing it and others in ignorance of it. She had not needed Jean’s telling her to pick them out﻿—there was something tangible yet intangible about them which indicated it to the observant eye﻿—a warmth, a delicate humanness, an attractiveness which did not belong to the women who lived north of Canal Street.

===Chinese===

先前卸了妆、熄了灯，她躺在床上想念卡尔。生活真有趣，她想。人们走进一个人的生命，像放映在不平整银幕上的电影人物那样闪闪烁烁，又从一个人的意识里走出去，不留下一桩要紧的回忆。大多数人都是这样，可是每隔一段时候总会来那么几个——数目永远寥寥——他们留了下来，而一留下来，就制造出种种为难的、不愉快的或者愉快的纠葛。她不明白，为什么她认识的、读到过的几乎所有人，都把简简单单的小事弄成天大的事，拿那些日后看来微不足道的东西折磨自己。良心？道义？荣誉？正义？真理？这些除了人自己在心里造出来的小口号、小神像——人还在它们面前五体投地、卑躬屈节——又算得了什么？真理？她记起卡尔有一次从斯宾诺莎那里引来对她讲的话——真理是由陈旧得发了白的谎言积成的。

可是话说回来，人赖以为生、又为之赴死的这些东西里，必定总还有些真东西。她想不明白那究竟是什么。它们值不值得人们为此经受的那一切痛苦、流血和牺牲呢？

“我这个人，”她默想，“是个女人，又是个黑人（Negro）。我若生为白人，人生已够艰难，可等我作为一个女人的难处之上再添上种族的难题，它就要加倍艰难了。”她闲闲地玩味着一个念头：她若生为白人会是什么命——她若越过分界线，忘掉身体里的黑人血液，会是什么命。这念头并不吸引她。在新奥尔良，最使她动心、她最敬佩的女人不是白人——至少，不是盎格鲁-撒克逊人。她们全住在克里奥尔人（Creole）区，让还给她指点过其中许多带有黑人血液的女人，有的自己知道，有的则蒙在鼓里。其实用不着让说，她也挑得出来——她们身上有种既实在又难以捉摸的东西，向留心观察的眼睛表明这一点：一种温暖，一种细腻的人情味，一种运河街以北的女人所不具备的动人之处。

===Original===

And here in Atlanta she had watched them, noting the subtle differences even in those like herself whose skins were fair. No wonder that in New Orleans in the old days, as she had read in the books of French travelers, the men often deserted the balls where their wives and daughters were, and slipped away to those more resplendent ones where quadroon women held sway. Mimi remembered a comparison one of them had made between these who had Negro blood with the American women﻿—“frank, warmhearted﻿ ﻿… with manners more interesting than the Americans﻿ ﻿… the roundness and beauty of shape in the women also contrasting with the straightness and angularity of American figures.”

She had asked Jean, in her innocence, to tell her about these balls but for some reason she could not explain he had evaded her question and seemed to be somewhat embarrassed by it. No, she concluded, with all its faults and petty unpleasant features, she would rather remain with her own people. They got, apparently, so much more out of the life they lived with all its barriers than those who had more but seemed infinitely less happy with it.

Her thoughts, she realized, revolved always in a circle of which the exact centre was Carl. She wondered why he and she had not ended their worries sooner by acceptance of the love which had come to them. It had all been so futile, so childish, and Mimi, with the reluctance of youth to think of itself as youthful, felt rather ashamed that they had acted in such callow and infantile manner. Now that she had stopped fighting against the love for Carl which she realized had been in her heart all the time, her fretful and worried air left her. She lay in her narrow bed and smiled tenderly as she felt again his lips from which his kisses at first had fallen upon hers like gentle, soft blows. She stirred happily as she felt once more the rough possessiveness to which his lips had changed﻿—she was not conscious of hurt until now when she realized her own lips were swollen slightly where he had pressed her close to him, while she had yielded happily. She was wondering where it would all end as she fell asleep.﻿ ﻿…

It seemed to her she had hardly closed her eyes when she was rudely awakened. Mrs. Daquin was shaking her roughly, a dressing-gown thrown hastily over her nightdress.

“Mimi, come quickly!” she was calling. “Something’s happened to your father.”

===Chinese===

而在亚特兰大这儿，她也一直观察着她们，注意到即便在像她这样皮肤白皙的人身上也有的细微差别。无怪乎当年在新奥尔良——她从法国旅行家的书里读到过——男人们常常丢下自己妻女所在的舞会，溜到那些更华丽的场子去，那里当家的是有黑人血统的混血女子。米米记得其中一位把这些人同美国女人作的比较——“坦率、热心……举止比美国人更有味道……女子身段的圆润丰美，也与美国人身板的平直瘦硬恰成对照。”

她曾天真地问让讲讲这些舞会，可不知怎的——她也说不清——让把话岔开了，还显得有点尴尬。不，她断定，纵有一身的毛病和种种琐碎的不愉快，她还是宁愿留在自己族人中间。他们显然从那处处设障的生活里得到的，比那些拥有更多的人还多得多，而后者看上去远不如他们快活。

她意识到，她的思念始终绕着一个圆圈转，圆心不多不少正是卡尔。她不明白，他和她为什么不早早接受降临到他们头上的爱情，省去这许多烦恼。这一切本来多么无谓、多么孩子气，而米米，怀着青年人不肯把自己当青年看待的那份别扭，倒有点害臊，他们竟表现得这样稚嫩、这样小儿科。如今她不再抗拒对卡尔的爱——她明白这爱其实一直在她心里——那副焦躁忧愁的神气便离开了她。她躺在窄床上，温柔地微笑着，重新感觉到了他的嘴唇——起初他的吻像轻柔的软击，一片片落在她唇上。当她再次体味他嘴唇变成的那种粗鲁的、占有的一切时，她幸福地动了动身子——直到此刻她才意识到疼痛，她发觉自己的嘴唇微微肿了，那是他把她紧紧按在怀里时留下的，而她曾那样快乐地顺从。睡着的时候，她还在想这一切会怎样收场。……

她觉得自己眼睛才合上，就被人粗暴地摇醒了。达坎太太在使劲摇晃她，睡衣外头胡乱罩了件晨衣。

“米米，快来！”她在喊。“你爸爸出事了。”

===Original===

“What’s the matter? Have you called the doctor?” asked Mimi as she hurried down the hall after Mrs. Daquin. Mimi was now thoroughly awake, a strange fear in her heart.

“Yes, he’s on the way here,” the older woman whispered as they entered Jean’s bedroom.

Jean was lying on the bed stretched at full length. His breath came with difficulty and his face was covered with a cold, clammy perspiration. Mimi with a little cry of distress and fear, quickly checked, flung herself on her knees beside the bed. She tried to feel Jean’s pulse but could find no trace. She pressed her head against Jean’s breast, trying to hear or feel his heart beating. An icy terror came over her when she could feel no throbbing. Jean groaned heavily as though in great pain. Mimi was happy to hear it﻿—at least, that was a sign that Jean was not dead.

The doctor found her there beside the bed while Mrs. Daquin stood at the foot, weeping and wringing her hands, all her efficiency and forthrighteousness fled in the face of this phenomenon. He gently led them from the room.

For hours, it seemed, they waited there. Once the door opened hastily and they heard the doctor, who had rushed past them without speaking, telephone another physician, speaking ominously of strychnine and nitroglycerine.

As he reentered the sickroom he muttered in answer to Mimi’s question something about Jean’s heart. “He’s a sick man﻿—a very sick man,” floated back to the two women as he closed the door.﻿ ﻿…

---

Morning found them waiting. For the first time Mimi felt close to Mrs. Daquin. Unconsciously they had clung together, brought close by the spectre which hovered over them. Mrs. Daquin brought a wrap and put it around Mimi and Mimi smiled her thanks. It seemed quite a natural thing that Mrs. Daquin should let her arm remain around the girl’s shoulders when she had placed the wrap there. Mimi snuggled near her and in her embrace Mimi felt a security from the dreadful thing in the room where Jean lay.

The door opened and the two doctors, haggard from their work, anxiety and loss of sleep, emerged, closed the door softly behind them. Dr. Adams led them to the lower floor before speaking.

===Chinese===

“怎么了？请医生了吗？”米米一面跟着达坎太太匆匆穿过走廊一面问。这时她完全醒了，心里怀着一种陌生的恐惧。

“请了，正在往这儿赶，”年长的妇人低声说，两人走进了让的卧室。

让直挺挺地躺在床上。他呼吸艰难，脸上覆着一层又冷又黏的汗。米米一声哀呼，又惊又怕，随即强自忍住，扑通跪倒在床边。她想试让的脉搏，却怎么也摸不到。她把头贴在让的胸口，想听见或感到他的心跳。感觉不到任何搏动时，一股冰冷的恐怖攝住了她。让沉重地呻吟了一声，像是在极大的痛苦中。米米听见这声呻吟反倒高兴——至少，这说明让还没有死。

医生发现她还跪在床边，达坎太太站在床脚，哭着绞着双手，她那副干练爽利在这场面面前飞得一干二净。医生轻轻把她们领出了房间。

她们在那儿等着，仿佛等了几个钟头。有一回门匆匆开了，她们听见医生一言不发地从身边冲过去，给另一位医生打电话，不祥地说着士的宁和硝酸甘油。

他重新走进病房时，咕哝了一句什么，算是回答米米的问话，说的是让的心脏。“他病得不轻——病得很不轻，”他关门时这句话飘回到两个女人耳边。……

---

清晨来临时，她们还守在那里。米米第一次觉得自己同达坎太太贴近了。不知不觉间，她们互相依偎着，被那盘桓在头顶的阴影拉到了一起。达坎太太取来一件外衣，披在米米身上，米米微笑着致谢。达坎太太放好外衣后，便让那条手臂留在姑娘肩上，这看起来完全是件自然而然的事。米米偎近她，在她的怀抱里，米米觉出一种保障，可以抵御那间躺着让的屋子里可怕的东西。

门开了，两位医生从里面走出来，因操劳、焦虑和缺觉而形容憔悴，随手轻轻带上了门。亚当斯医生领她们到楼下才开口。

===Original===

“Mr. Daquin has had a very serious attack. Heart trouble. Brought on by acute indigestion. We’ve given him a double injection of strychnine and nitroglycerine to speed up his heart action. At first I couldn’t get any heart action even with my stethoscope. Thought he was gone. He’s resting now. Keep him quiet. I’ll be back in an hour or so.”

Mimi felt, as Dr. Adams uttered his crisp, staccato sentences, as though she were a condemned criminal standing before a judge, hearing a terrible sentence pronounced upon her. Jean﻿—her Jean﻿—gentle, uncomplaining, always kind﻿—dangerously ill. It didn’t seem possible.

“I’ll tell Mrs. Adams and Hilda to come over and do what they can to help you,” Dr. Adams promised as he left the house.﻿ ﻿…

The news of Jean’s illness spread rapidly. Before Mimi and Mrs. Daquin could finish dressing they began to come with offers of help. Mrs. Adams and Hilda were the first. Hilda and Mimi held each other close, all differences melted away by the sadness which had come to Mimi. For a long time now Hilda had known from Carl’s attitude that her hopes were of no avail. She had freely unburdened her woes at last to her mother and in the telling of them and the ready sympathy which she received from Mrs. Adams had found the peace of resignation. She knew through some psychic means that Carl loved Mimi and her newfound calm enabled her to feel thoroughly happy for their sakes.﻿ ﻿…

All day they came. Mimi never knew there could be so much solicitude, so much genuine kindness. One came and prepared a meal, another tidied the house, another volunteered to do errands. Mimi felt ashamed of her dislike for some of them in the past. Affliction had shown her the real worth which lay beneath the petty malice, the ignominious bickerings and jealousies which to her had seemed the outstanding characteristics of many of these folks.

Jean slept the greater part of the day. Late in the afternoon Dr. Adams gave him another stimulant. Mimi had begun to show the strain through which she had been and he urged her to go for a walk. Returning, she found Mrs. Daquin waiting for her in the hall.

“Your father’s been asking for you. Dr. Adams said it would be all right for you to go in and see him but he must not be excited.”

He smiled as she entered the darkened room.

===Chinese===

“达坎先生犯了一次很重的病。心脏的毛病。由急性消化不良引发。我们给他打了双份的士的宁和硝酸甘油，提一提心脏的搏动。起初我连听诊器里都听不出心跳，还当他人已经去了。他现在休息了。让他安静。我一个钟头左右再来。”

亚当斯医生吐出这一句句干脆利落的短句时，米米觉得自己像个站在法官面前的死囚，听着一条可怕的判词宣判到自己头上。让——她的让——温存、从不抱怨、永远和善——病危了。这好像不可能。

“我这就告诉亚当斯太太和希尔达，叫她们过来，能帮什么帮什么，”亚当斯医生临出门时许诺道。……

让患病的消息飞快传开了。米米和达坎太太还没穿戴停当，来帮忙的人已经上了门。亚当斯太太和希尔达是头一批。希尔达和米米紧紧相拥，一切芥蒂都让降临到米米头上的悲伤融化了。希尔达早从卡尔的态度明白了自己的指望全都落空。她终于痛痛快快向母亲倾吐了苦楚，在倾诉中，在亚当斯太太给予的爽爽快快的同情里，她获得了听天由命的安宁。她凭着某种心灵感应知道卡尔爱着米米，而这新得的平静使她能够真心实意地为他们的幸福而快乐。……

一整天他们川流不息地来。米米从来不知道人间竟有这样多的关切、这样多的真心善意。这个来做了一顿饭，那个收拾了屋子，另一个自告奋勇去跑腿。米米为自己过去对其中某些人的嫌恶感到羞愧。灾病让她看见了那层琐屑的恶意、不光彩的龃龉和妒忌底下真正的价值——在她看来，那本是这帮人身上最扎眼的品性。

让睡了大半个白天。傍晚时分，亚当斯医生又给他打了一针强心剂。米米连日的操劳已现于形色，医生催她出去散散步。回来时，她发现达坎太太在门厅里等她。

“你父亲一直在问你。亚当斯医生说你进去看看他不妨事，只是千万别叫他激动。”

她走进那间光线幽暗的屋子，他微笑了。

===Original===

“I’m sorry I’ve caused you so much trouble,” he apologized as Mimi kissed him tenderly. As she bent over him she noticed for the first time how white his hair had become. His moustache and goatee, too, without their daily brushing and waxing, looked less magnificent than they had always seemed. Jean’s eyes were sunken, his face lined with the suffering he had been through. An ineffable pity filled her, she wished eagerly even as she knew it was futile that she could take his place.

“Do you remember that day in the St. Louis cemetery just before we came here?” Jean was asking.

“Do I remember it? I’ll never forget it,” she assured him.

“It seems a long, long time ago.”

A reminiscent, faraway look came into Jean’s eyes and he lay there, his thin hands resting on his chest, for several minutes without speaking. Mimi waited. At last he stirred, smiling again at her, this time apologetically.

“We were happy there, weren’t we, Mimi? I’ve never been satisfied here. Too much rushing about, no time for living﻿—real living. And you and I﻿—we haven’t had the time here for the long walks we used to take when we were home. No﻿—no﻿—I’m not blaming you,” he assured her as she started to speak. “We’ve both been too busy﻿—”

But Mimi felt a sting in the words. She knew there was none intended, it was her own conscience that lashed her. She wished fervently she had not been so selfish, that she had thought more of Jean. She knew now he had never been happy in Atlanta. His leisurely, reflective nature made him unhappy, he was wholly out of place anywhere save in some easygoing place like his beloved Louisiana.

“I suppose it’s been for the best, though. Young people like you haven’t much patience for the old ways and customs﻿—you, too, would in time have become unhappy there. But I didn’t intend to ramble off like that. It’s about you I wanted to talk.﻿ ﻿… You’ve grown into a beautiful woman, Mimi,” he declared, proudly. “Every day you look more like Margot did when I first knew her. And you’ve ways like hers﻿—only you’ve more spirit, more fire.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“对不起，给你添了这么多麻烦，”米米温柔地吻他时，他道歉说。她俯身向他，第一次注意到他的头发白了多少。他的髭须和下巴上的山羊胡，也因缺了每日的梳理上蜡，不像素日那样显得威严。让的眼睛凹陷下去，脸上刻着他所受的苦楚。一种难以言说的怜惜充满了她的心，她热切地希望——哪怕明知是妄想——自己能替他受这份罪。

“你还记得咱们来这儿之前，在圣路易公墓的那一天吗？”让问。

“记得吗？我一辈子也忘不了，”她向他保证。

“好像已经是好久好久以前的事了。”

让的眼睛里浮起一种追怀的、辽远的眼神，他躺在那里，瘦削的双手叠放在胸前，好几分钟没有说话。米米等待着。他终于动了动，又朝她微笑，这一回带着歉意。

“在那儿咱们是快活的，不是吗，米米？我在这儿从来没有称心过。太忙乱，没有工夫过活——过真正的生活。你和我——在这儿也没有工夫像在家那时一样，出去做长长的散步了。不——不——我不是怪你，”见她要开口，他向她保证。“咱们两个都太忙了——”

可米米觉得这话里有根刺。她知道那不是有意的，抽打她的是她自己的良心。她热切地希望过去别那么自私，多为让想一想。她如今才明白，他在亚特兰大从来不曾快活过。他那闲适好思的天性在这儿受着煎熬，除了他心爱的路易斯安那那样悠闲的地方，他走到哪儿都格格不入。

“不过我想这样终究还是好的。像你这样的年轻人，对老规矩老习俗没有多少耐性——待久了，你也会在那儿闷闷不乐的。可我本不打算这样东拉西扯。我要谈的是你。……你长成个美丽的女人了，米米，”他骄傲地宣称。“你一天比一天更像我们初识时的玛戈了。你也有她的那些做派——只是你更有元气，更有一团火。……”

===Original===

Again he lay silent and Mimi, knowing instinctively he was living again his days with Margot, hardly dared breathe lest she break the spell. The room grew dark but neither of them noticed it. From outside came the soft cries of children at play far away, the bumping of a short, fat street car as it meandered down Auburn Avenue, the shrill voice of a woman calling her child to supper.

“I’m worried, Mimi﻿—not afraid﻿—just worried. I’ve had these heart attacks before but I said nothing about them because I didn’t want to worry you and Mary﻿—but this one last night was the worst one yet.”

She sought to comfort him, telling him little falsehoods about the allegedly minor importance which Dr. Adams had attached to the attack. It’ll pass over and he’ll be none the worse for it, were the words she put in the physician’s mouth.

“Did he say that really?” Jean asked anxiously, yet hopefully.

She assured him that those were the exact words. Jean leaned back on his pillow, a more peaceful look on his face which removed some of the shame she was feeling on account of the lie. She would have lied a thousand times cheerfully if they served to make him more content.

“He didn’t seem so optimistic when he was talking to me,” Jean mused. “I suppose he was trying to scare me so I would stay in bed.”

“Of course that was what he intended. Doctors assume patients will do just about half what they are told to do, so they are told to do twice what they need to do,” she encouraged him.

“I’m tired, Mimi﻿—so tired. I think I’ll take a little nap now,” he said after a pause. “I wanted to talk to you because I didn’t know how serious this attack might be tell you to remember, whatever happened, you’re a Daquin, but now I’ve heard what the doctor said, that can wait. You’re a beautiful girl, Mimi, almost too beautiful. But you’ll come through all right﻿—just make up your own mind what’s the right thing to do, pay no attention to what other people say, and then do what you think is right.”

Automatically she thought of Carl.

“Last night, after you and Mrs. Daquin left, Carl came by the house and I called him in,” she told Jean. “He was very unhappy﻿—I never saw him so downcast. We talked awhile﻿—then﻿—then﻿—oh, Jean, he told me he loved me﻿—and it made me mighty happy﻿—”

===Chinese===

他又躺着不作声了，米米凭本能知道他又在重温他同玛戈共度的岁月，大气也不敢出，生怕惊破了这符咒。房间渐渐暗下来，两人谁也没有留意。外面传来远处孩子们玩耍的细碎叫声，一辆又短又胖的街车磕磕绊绊驶下奥本大道（Auburn Avenue）的声响，还有一个女人尖着嗓门唤孩子回家吃晚饭的声音。

“我心里担忧，米米——不是怕——只是担忧。这样的心脏发作从前也有过，可我什么也没说，因为我不想叫你和玛丽挂心——可昨天夜里这一次最凶。”

她想安慰他，把亚当斯医生对这次发作如何如何轻描淡写的小谎话说给他听。“会过去的，过去之后他不会落下一点亏欠”——她把这话安到了医生嘴里。

“他真是这么说的？”让急切而又怀着指望地问。

她向他保证，一字不差就是这些话。让靠回枕头上，脸上现出较为安宁的神色，这也多少减轻了她为那句谎话感到的羞愧。只要能叫他宽心，她情愿快快活活撒上一千次谎。

“他跟我说话的时候，倒好像不这么乐观，”让思忖道。“我猜他是想吓唬我，好让我卧床。”

“他打的正是这个主意。医生们总是估量病人只肯照吩咐的一半去做，所以吩咐的就得是需要的两倍，”她鼓励他。

“我累了，米米——累得很。我想打个盹儿了，”停了停他说道。“我原想同你谈谈，因为我不知道这次发作会有多凶，嘱咐你记住，不管出了什么事，你是达坎家的人；如今既然听了医生的话，那就等往后再说吧。你是个美丽的姑娘，米米，美丽得几乎过了头。可你会平安度过的——只要自己拿定主意什么是该做的事，别管旁人说什么，然后照你认为对的就是了。”

她不由自主地想到了卡尔。

“昨天夜里，您和达坎太太走了以后，卡尔到咱们家来，我把他叫了进来，”她告诉让。“他非常不幸——我从没见他那样消沉。我们谈了一会儿——后来——后来——哦，让，他告诉我他爱我——这叫我快活极了——”

===Original===

“Carl’s a good boy, Mimi. A little too flighty﻿—a little weak, too, I’m afraid﻿—but he’ll come around all right, I think. They don’t understand him and they’re always rubbing his fur the wrong way but﻿—Carl’s got the right stuff in him.”

Jean lapsed again into one of his retrospective moods. His eyes slowly closed and soon he fell asleep. Mimi kissed him gently, lowered the shade to keep the light from the street lamp from disturbing him, and stole softly from the room.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“卡尔是个好孩子，米米。就是有点太轻浮——恐怕还有点软弱——可我想他会好起来的。他们不了解他，老是逆着毛捋他——不过——卡尔骨子里是好料。”

让又沉入他那追怀往事的心境。他的眼睛慢慢闭上了，不久便睡着了。米米轻轻吻了他，放下窗帘，免得街灯的光搅扰他，然后蹑手蹑脚地出了房间。……

## XI / 十一
===Original===

At midnight Dr. Adams made his last call for the day.

“He’s resting easily now and I think he’ll sleep through the night. Call me if you need me. And you’d better get a little sleep yourself or I’ll have two patients,” he advised Mimi as he left.

His words brought realization to Mimi that she was tired. She sent Mrs. Daquin to bed, telling her she would lock up the house and see that everything was attended to. She then tiptoed into Jean’s room and found him breathing gently, his face, with a smile upon it, turned towards the door.

When he called at seven the next morning Dr. Adams said that Jean had been dead about three hours. Mimi found on his face the same smile that had been there when she had seen him last. Mrs. Daquin came into the room weeping but for Mimi there were no tears. Death had come too suddenly upon her, its swift snatching away of her Jean had rendered her incapable of thought, of every emotion. She knelt by the bed, her head resting beside Jean’s, stunned, inarticulate. Dr. Adams tried to pull her away, telling her it would be better for her if she did not stay too long there.

“No, Doctor, I’m happier here. I’ve just a few more hours with Jean now. Leave me here alone, won’t you?”

He led the weeping Mrs. Daquin from the room and Mimi was alone with Jean. Jean dead? She couldn’t believe it was true. She touched his hand, stiffened, cold. It terrified her. How many times she might have held it, warm, pulsating, responsive.

===Chinese===

午夜，亚当斯医生做了当天最后一次出诊。

“他现在安安稳稳地休息了，我想他能一觉睡到天亮。有需要就叫我。你自己也最好睡一会儿，不然我就要添个病人了，”他临走时嘱咐米米。

他的话使米米意识到自己累了。她打发达坎太太上床，说她自己来锁门、照应一切。随后她蹑着脚走进让的房间，见他呼吸轻匀，脸带微笑，朝着门口的方向。

第二天早晨七点他再来时，亚当斯医生说，让已经死去大约三个钟头了。米米发现他脸上仍是她最后一次见他时的那个微笑。达坎太太哭着走进房间，米米却流不出眼泪。死亡来得太突然，它把她的让这样猝然夺去，使她不能思想，也没有了任何情绪。她跪在床边，头挨着让的头，整个人懵了，说不出话。亚当斯医生想把她拉开，说她在那儿待得太久并没有好处。

“不，医生，我在这儿倒好些。如今我同让只剩下这几个钟头了。让我一个人待在这儿，好不好？”

他把哭泣的达坎太太领出了房间，米米独自同让在一起。让死了？她不能相信这是真的。她碰了碰他的手，僵硬，冰凉。这使她惊恐。那双手，她本来可以握过多少次的，温暖的、搏动的、有回应的。

===Original===

No﻿—she had been too busy with her own petty affairs, her own insignificant worries, to think of him﻿—longing for his quiet old home, unhappy in an environment and among people he did not understand and whose ways were not his. She had known in an indefinite sort of way that Jean was not happy, that he and Mary grew farther and farther apart each year, each month, each day. A spasm of remorse swept through Mimi﻿—remorse that held her in an icy, terrifying grip﻿—when she realized now how she might have been of comfort, of consolation, to Jean, and instead had chased merrily after her own whims and fancies, giving little thought to him. She knew now that these later years must have been terribly lonely ones for him. She felt she would cheerfully have given ten years of her life to have him back for one year, for one month, even for one day. Then she would show him the quality of her love for him. Holy Mother, why did one see these things only when it was too late?

She was glad they had had their talk yesterday afternoon. It had been like old times, she and Jean had been so happy together. Even in his own illness his thoughts had been of her﻿—he had wanted to live not for his own sake but only for hers. “You’ll come through all right,” he had said. She wondered if she would. Without Jean’s physical presence she speculated what her relations with Mrs. Daquin would be. In his quiet way Jean had had a very real control over his wife. Mimi sensed now as she had never done before that despite his gentleness, which sometimes seemed to be almost weakness, his apparent softness had had great strength behind it﻿—strength enough to hold in check Mrs. Daquin’s aggressiveness and domineering attitude. Jean had not spoken sharply often but when he had his wife listened and obeyed. He had never had to speak twice.

Why don’t I cry, she wondered, like all women do and most men when death comes? She was conscious of a slight feeling of guilt because of her dry eyes. She wondered if she was showing proper respect. She looked at Jean’s peaceful face. Her own face was set in hard, tight lines, her teeth were clamped until the muscles of her jaws ached, but no tears came. Jean dead? No﻿—no﻿—no﻿—it couldn’t be true.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

不——她一直忙着应付自己那些琐碎的事，自己那些无关紧要的烦恼，没有为他想过——他渴望着他那安静的老家，在一个他不理解的环境里、在一群路数与他不同的人们中间郁郁不乐。她模模糊糊地知道让不快活，知道他同玛丽一年比一年、一月比一月、一天比一天地疏远。一阵悔恨的痉挛扫过米米——那悔恨把她攥在冰冷而可怕的掌心里——如今她才明白，她本可以给让多少安慰、多少抚恤，却只顾快快活活地追逐自己的奇想和兴致，很少想到他。她如今才知道，这些年头对他必定寂寞得可怕。她觉得，只要能换他回来一年——一个月，甚至一天——她情愿交出自己十年的寿命。到那时她就可以让他看看她对他的爱有多少分量。圣母啊，为什么人总是在来不及的时候才看清这些事？

她庆幸昨天下午他们谈了那一次。那像回到了旧日时光，她和让在一起多快活。哪怕在自己病中，他想的还是她——他想活，不是为自己，只是为了她。“你会平安度过的，”他说过。她不知道自己会不会。没有了让的亲身在场，她猜想着她同达坎太太的关系会变成什么样。让以他安静的方式，对他的妻子有一种十分实在的辖制。米米此刻才比以往任何时候都更清楚地感觉到，尽管他温存——有时温存得近乎软弱——他那表面的柔和背后有着强大的力量，那力量足以遏制达坎太太的盛气凌人和专横做派。让不常疾言厉色，可他一旦说了，他的妻子就听着、照办。他从不需要说第二遍。

我为什么哭不出来，她纳闷，女人都哭，多数男人在死亡来临时也哭，我怎么不哭？她因为自己眼睛干燥而觉得有点负疚。她不知道自己这样算不算尽了应有的哀思。她望着让安详的脸。她自己的脸绷出又硬又紧的线条，牙关咬得腮帮子的肌肉发酸，可眼泪就是不来。让死了？不——不——不——这不可能是真的。……

===Original===

They found her there, dry-eyed, when the undertaker came to prepare Jean for burial. She was calm until the dingy wicker basket, sagging with the weight of its load, was being carried through the door. She was thinking of that day Jean had referred to yesterday﻿—that day in the St. Louis cemetery when the funeral procession passed near them. Jean had said he felt just as though his own body were in the casket. She knew now what he had meant﻿—her own body was there in that straw container.

“Be careful!” she cried as one of the men let the basket strike against the door. A sharp physical pain shot through her body, followed by nausea as the men righted the slipping basket. Then all went dark for her. Without a sound she sank to the floor, unconscious.﻿ ﻿…

---

When she awoke she was in bed. Hilda sat near and smiled at her when she opened her eyes.

“Papa says you must keep quiet, absolutely. Mama is seeing after things and Mr. Hunter is taking care of all the arrangements for the funeral﻿—”

The ominous, horrifying word brought a little cry of anguish from Mimi. Hilda’s eyes filled with tears at her ineptness.

“Ask your mother if she’ll try and get some candles. And see if she can get a priest for the funeral. Jean would be happiest if he was buried that way,” begged Mimi.

When Hilda had hurried away to execute her wishes, Mimi tried to get up but found herself too weak. She was glad to sink back into the soft pillows. Yes, Jean would be happiest if he had a Catholic burial. He hadn’t been to mass for years now but he had never allowed the narrowness and prejudice of priests who after all were human and with human fear and cowardice and prejudice to destroy his faith. Reverently, Mimi made the sign of the cross.﻿ ﻿…

---

The morning passed before she had strength enough to rise. She talked over with Mrs. Daquin, Mrs. Adams and Mr. Hunter plans for the funeral. Jean’s sister in New York whom Mimi had never seen had been telegraphed and had wired she was leaving for Atlanta at once. They decided the services were to be held on Friday. Mrs. Daquin thought it best to have a Protestant service but Mimi was obdurate, immovable. This was the last thing she could do for Jean and she was determined that it should be done, if possible.

===Chinese===

殡仪馆的人来为让入殓时，他们发现她还跪在那里，眼睛干干的。她一直很平静，直到那只不过色的柳条筐，被里面的重量压得下坠，正被抬出门去。她想起让昨天提到的那一天——圣路易公墓里送葬队伍从他们近旁经过的那一天。让说过，他觉得自己就像自己的身体正躺在那口棺材里。她如今懂了他的意思——她自己的身体就在那个装着稻草的容器里。

“小心！”一个男人让筐子撞上了门框，她喊了起来。一阵尖锐的肉体疼痛穿透她的身体，男人扶正下滑的筐子时，她又是一阵恶心。随后她眼前一黑。她无声无息地瘫倒在地板上，失去了知觉。……

---

醒来时她躺在床上。希尔达坐在近旁，见她睁开眼睛便朝她微笑。

“爸爸说你必须绝对静养。妈妈在照料事务，亨特先生在料理丧事的一切安排——”

这个不祥而骇人的字眼给米米带来一声小小的哀呼。希尔达因自己失言而眼里涌上了泪水。

“求求你母亲，看她能不能弄几支蜡烛来。再看看她能不能请到一位神父来主持丧事。让若能那样下葬，就是最快活的了，”米米恳求道。

希尔达匆匆去照办之后，米米想爬起来，却发现浑身无力。她乐得重新陷进柔软的枕头里。是的，让若能按天主教仪式下葬，就是最快活的了。他已多年不去望弥撒，可他从不允许教士们的褊狭与偏见——教士毕竟也是人，也有人的恐惧、人的怯懦、人的偏见——毁掉他的信仰。米米恭恭敬敬地画了个十字。……

---

到中午她才攒起力气起床。她同达坎太太、亚当斯太太和亨特先生商议了丧事的安排。已给让在纽约的妹妹——米米从未见过她——拍了电报，回电说她即刻动身来亚特兰大。他们商定礼拜五举行丧礼。达坎太太觉得最好行新教仪式，米米却毫不通融，寸步不让。这是她能为让做的最后一件事，她决心只要做得到，就一定要这样做。

===Original===

Late in the afternoon, in the lull of visitors who were called home by household duties, Carl came. To both it seemed impossible that less than twenty-four hours before they had been so happy together in that very room.

“There’s no use of my saying I’m sorry, Mimi. You know it﻿—I loved your father better than anybody except you. He seemed to understand me﻿—I﻿—I﻿—” His sudden rush of feeling made it impossible for him to speak. His eyes filled with tears as he touched Mimi’s hand, gently and but for a second.

“Yes, Carl, I know﻿ ﻿…” she whispered.﻿ ﻿…

---

By Thursday evening Mimi felt she could no longer stand the torture of greeting people, answering the same questions over and over again, performing the many additional tasks the death had brought. Mrs. Daquin seemed actually to be enjoying after a fashion the new importance which Jean’s death had brought to her as his widow. Not that she let it show on her face. She saw to it her eyes bore the appearance of much weeping and her general air was that of a Christian martyr about to be thrown to the lions. To Mimi she seemed to have donned the atmosphere of the sorely tried and heavily bereaved with full attention to the effectiveness of details, wearing the role as an accomplished actress would. Mimi found all her old antagonism rising again﻿—Jean’s life, she reflected bitterly, would have been much happier had his wife expended some of that energy now used in simulated grief towards happiness for Jean when alive.

There was but one place where Mimi felt at peace, and that sitting beside Jean’s casket. Tomorrow morning Jean’s sister, Mrs. Rogers of New York, would arrive. Tomorrow afternoon the funeral. Mimi crept quietly into the parlour. The room was unlighted save for the illumination from the flickering candles. Through the partly opened windows floated a warm breeze of an unusually balmy late October evening. The lace curtains swayed lazily back and forth as though bidding a languid farewell to the body in the casket just beyond their reach. In the dimness they seemed to Mimi like long slender fingers seeking vainly to caress the sides of the box which held all that remained of Jean.

===Chinese===

傍晚时分，在客人们各自被家务唤回家的空当里，卡尔来了。对两人来说，不到二十四小时以前，他们还一同在那间屋子里那样快活，这似乎不可能。

“我说难过也没有用，米米。你知道的——除了你，我最爱的就是你父亲。他好像理解我——我——我——”感情的骤然涌起使他说不下去。他碰了碰米米的手，轻轻地，只一秒钟，眼里噙满了泪。

“是的，卡尔，我知道……”她低声说。……

---

到星期四晚上，米米觉得自己再也受不了这份酷刑了：迎接一批批的人，一遍又一遍回答同样的问题，做死亡带来的那一大堆额外的事务。达坎太太呢，让的去世给她这个遗孀带来的新的要人身份，她竟像煞有介事地受用起来了。倒不是她流露在脸上。她留心让自己的眼睛带着哭过很多场的样子，整个神气是一位即将被投给狮子的基督徒殉道者。在米米看来，她把饱经磨折、痛失亲眷的氛围穿戴上了身，细节的效果一处不落，像一位演技纯熟的女演员那样披挂起这个角色。米米发现自己旧日的敌意又全涌了上来——让的一生，她怨愤地想，倘若他妻子把如今用于假哭的那些力气分出几分，趁让活着的时候用来给他快活，那该幸福得多。

只有一个地方米米觉得安宁，就是坐在让的棺木旁边。明天早晨，让的妹妹、纽约的罗杰斯太太就到了。明天下午出殡。米米轻轻地溜进客厅。屋里没有点灯，只有摇曳的烛光照亮着。半开的窗子里飘进一股暖风，那是一个异常和煦的十月末的傍晚。花边窗帘懒懒地来回摆动，仿佛在同那口它们够不着的棺材里的身体作慵懒的道别。在幽暗里，米米觉得它们像长长的纤细手指，徒然地想抚一抚那装着让的一切遗存的匣子的边沿。

===Original===

The house was nearly deserted. One by one they had gone away. Mimi, wearied by the incessant stream, felt a great eagerness to find the peace she could get nowhere else save near Jean. It annoyed her to find she was not alone. Mrs. Plummer and Mrs. King sat near the window conversing in lowered voices. Mimi ignored them, drew a chair close to the casket and sat there gazing at Jean’s face. On it was unmistakable peace﻿—she almost wished that she were there beside him. The extent of her loss was creeping upon her. Without Jean she felt lost, terrified, afraid. To whom could she turn? Mrs. Daquin? Obviously, no. Mrs. Hunter? Hilda? Hilda’s mother? Carl? Each of them had his good points but none of them were so all-inclusive, so gentle, so understanding, so unselfish. Nor even was she herself, she realized. For here I am thinking only of myself﻿—had I done less of that when Jean was alive, I would have made his life and my own far happier.

“… her eyes ain’t even red﻿ ﻿… if she was so crazy about him as she pretended to be, she’d﻿ ﻿…”

Mimi suddenly became conscious of the sibilant whispers which came to her from the two women by the window. Lost in the grief bottled up within her like turbulent waters held by a great dam, she had been oblivious of comments during the days since Jean had died. Even to the undoubtedly sympathetic ones she had replied methodically, listlessly, giving little thought to the words, words, words. Now as the sinister, malevolent whisperings came to her, the inevitable reaction to the apathy she had felt flamed into being. She rose from her chair, bitter hatred welling up for the evil-tongued old women. But even as she opened her mouth to speak, an overwhelming sense of the futility of it all came over her, a sense of inappropriateness, the lack of respect for Jean if she were to engage here in what would be but little more than a fishwives’ quarrel. She checked the hot, bitter words that pressed for utterance and rushed from the room.

===Chinese===

屋里几乎走空了。他们一个个都走了。被这川流不息的人流弄得疲惫不堪的米米，急切地想找到那份她在别处得不到、只在让近旁才有的安宁。她发现自己并非独自一人，心里恼火。普卢默太太和金太太坐在窗边低声交谈。米米没理会她们，拖过一把椅子挨着棺木坐下，坐在那里凝望让的脸。那脸上一派毋容置疑的安宁——她几乎希望自己就躺在那儿他身边。她的损失之重大，正一步一步向她逼来。没有了让，她觉得迷失、惊惶、害怕。她还能投奔谁呢？达坎太太？显然不行。亨特太太？希尔达？希尔达的母亲？卡尔？他们每个人都各有好处，可没有一个像让那样包罗一切、那样温存、那样体贴、那样无私。连她自己也做不到，她意识到。瞧我，此刻想的还只是我自己——让活着的时候我若少想些自己，就会使他的一生和我自己的一生都幸福得多。

“……她的眼睛连红都没红……她要真像她装的那样迷他，她该……”

米米突然听清了从窗边那两个女人那里送过来的喍喍低语。她沉浸在胸中郁积的哀伤里，像大坝拦住的激荡的水，让死后这几天来，她对种种议论一概浑然不觉。就连那些无疑出于好意的话，她也只是机械地、无精打采地应着，几乎没去听那些字、字、字。此刻，那阴毒的、含着恶意的窃窃私语传进她耳朵，她那阵麻木必然引起的反应腾地燃烧起来。她从椅子上站起身，对这两个长恶舌的老女人涌起一阵痛恨。可她张口要说话的一刹那，一股一切都徒劳的感觉压倒了她，一种不合时宜之感——在这里发作，几乎就是一场长舌妇的吵骂，是对让的不敬。她把那些急于出口的火辣辣、苦涩涩的话咽了回去，奔出了房间。

===Original===

In the security of her own room she lay on the bed and envied the peace on Jean’s face. Again and again the picture melted into the face of Carl as he had left the house the night Jean died. She wondered if this fantastic trick of her mind were not sacrilege, if it were not in some manner disloyalty to Jean. Yet, one was living, the other gone, and she felt a comforting sense that Jean would not think her disloyal. She wondered what course her new relations with Carl would take. Now that Hilda was removed from the problem﻿ ﻿… ? Weighing, pondering, wondering, she lightly slept. They’ll surely be gone now, she thought, as she on awaking descended to the parlour once more, drawn back to Jean as iron filings are attracted by a powerful magnet.

The chairs by the window were empty and Mimi was glad. She sat again by the casket and peace again came to her. How long she sat there she did not know, lost as she was in her own rambling, chaotic musings. Again there came to her a feeling she was not alone. The whispering rose once more, this time from the shadows on the other side of the casket. She sought to put them out of her mind, to lose herself again in her own thoughts. After all, she thought, they are trying to show their respect for Jean and their willingness to be of service. It is thoughtful of them to come over here and sit up. From the kitchen came the sound of voices and the aroma of coffee. Maybe they’ll leave soon if only to get something to eat.

They did not leave. Though she made every effort to close her ears and mind to the words which floated jerkily to her, Mimi found her resentment rising again. Mrs. Plummer and Mrs. King discussed the probable price of the casket, the furniture. They speculated as to what the widow and daughter would do now, in what sort of financial circumstances the deceased had left them. Mimi heard Carl’s name linked with her own﻿—followed by an appreciative chuckle from Mrs. King in response to some remark made by her companion which Mimi could not hear. It was obvious the women had not seen or heard Mimi reenter the room, for the frankness of their comment indicated their assumption they were alone. On and on they roamed, by innuendo and by unqualified assertion destroying, maligning, tearing to pieces.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

在自己房间里那份安全里，她躺在床上，艳羡让脸上的安宁。那画面一次又一次地融化成让死去那晚卡尔离开这房子时的脸。她不知道自己头脑玩的这套荒诞把戏是不是亵渎，是不是对让的某种不忠。可是，一个活着，一个去了，而她有一种宽慰的感觉：让不会认为她不忠。她不知道她同卡尔的新关系会走向何样。如今希尔达既已从这道难题里撤了出去……？她掂量着，思忖着，疑惑着，浅浅地睡着了。这会儿她们总该走了吧，她想，一觉醒来又下楼来到客厅，像铁屑被强大的磁石吸住那样又被拉回到让身边。

窗边的椅子空着，米米很高兴。她又在棺木旁坐下，安宁又回到她身上。她坐了多久，自己也不知道，她已沉入自己那漫无头绪、乱作一团的冥想里。那股并非独自一人的感觉又袭上心来。窃窃私语又起来了，这一回是从棺木另一侧的暗处传来的。她想把它们逐出脑海，重新沉入自己的思绪。说到底，她想，她们这是在表达对让的敬意，表达随时帮忙的心意。她们肯过来守夜，也算是有心。厨房里传来人声和咖啡的香气。也许她们一会儿就走，哪怕只是为了弄点吃的呢。

她们没有走。尽管米米竭力堵上耳朵、关上心门，不去听那些断断续续飘过来的话，她发觉自己的怒气又升起来了。普卢默太太和金太太讨论起棺木、家具大概的价钱。她们揣摩这寡妇和女儿往后怎么办，死者给她们留下了怎样一份家底。米米听见卡尔的名字同她自己的连在一起——金太太随之发出一声会心的轻笑，那是应和她同伴一句米米听不见的话。显然两个女人没有看见也没有听见米米重新进屋，因为她们议论得那样露骨，表明她们只当屋里没有别人。她们说个没完，东一句西一句，含沙射影外加直截了当的武断，毁谤着，中伤着，撕扯着。……

===Original===

A cold, blind fury seized Mimi. She jumped to her feet. The women gasped as Mimi’s bloodless face framing flashing eyes filled with fury rose at the head of the casket, the light from the tall candles giving it a terrifying, ghostlike appearance.

“You two get out!” Mimi demanded. “You might have had enough respect for my father here to have silenced your filthy, lying tongues! Get out! Get out! Get out!”

Her voice at first was but little more than a sharp whisper. As her anger mastered her it rose until her words carried to the hall beyond. Mrs. Plummer and Mrs. King, thoroughly frightened, hastily edged around the room towards the door, keeping as great distance as was possible between them and Mimi, who slowly circled her body so that her eyes remained upon them. The two gossips hurried through the little knot which had gathered at the door, drawn there by the commotion. As the door shut behind them Mimi sank into her chair and wept as Mrs. Adams came in and put her arms about her.﻿ ﻿…

Down the street hurried two indignant, surprised women. Mrs. Plummer speculated plaintively in an aggrieved tone as she panted her way homeward.

“I wonder what was the matter with her,” she asked her companion. “We didn’t say nothing to make her r’ar and tear like that﻿—and over her dead father’s body at that! Some folks sho’ is funny!”

“Mis’ Plummer,” her companion declared portentously, “it’s the truth that hurts! You remember the old sayin’s, ‘The hit dog yelps!’ and ‘Where there’s smoke there’s fire!’ Guess we weren’t so far wrong at that﻿ ﻿… Guess that gal will bear watchin’.”﻿ ﻿…

---

Early the next morning Mrs. Rogers came. As she entered the house Mimi rushed to meet her. Her aunt took her in her arms, murmuring gentle words of consolation to Mimi, who felt instantly the presence of a friend and ally. Mrs. Rogers was of medium height, younger than Jean but so like her brother that Jean seemed only a replica to which white, close-cropped hair, a moustache and a little beard had been added.

“Poor, dear Mimi,” she whispered, her eyes wet. She pressed Mimi to her in a warm outpouring of real affection, affection which sprang into existence unreservedly, binding them together in a flash of understanding and love.﻿ ﻿…

---

Mimi and her aunt talked a long time together just before Mrs. Rogers returned to New York two days after the funeral.

===Chinese===

一股冰冷的、盲目的狂怒攝住了米米。她霍地跳起来。两个女人倒抽一口冷气——米米那张没有血色的脸从棺木头上方升起，框着一双怒火闪闪的眼睛，高烛的光照得那张脸煞是怕人，像鬼魅一般。

“你们两个给我出去！”米米喝道。“我父亲的遗体在这儿，你们本该有这点敬意，管住自己那两条肮脏的、撒谎的舌头！出去！出去！出去！”

她的声音起初只比尖利的耳语略高一点。随着怒火把她压倒，嗓门越来越高，话音直传到外面的门厅。普卢默太太和金太太吓坏了，慌忙贴着墙边往门口蹭，同米米保持尽可能大的距离，米米则缓缓地转动身子，眼睛始终盯着她们。这两个搬弄是非的女人匆匆挤过被骚动引到门口的那一小堆人。门在她们身后关上，米米跌坐回椅子里，哭了起来，亚当斯太太进来搂住了她。……

街上匆匆走着两个又气又惊的女人。普卢默太太一面喘着气往家赶，一面用委屈的调子哀哀地猜度。

“不知道她是犯了什么邪，”她问同伴。“咱们没说啥惹得她那样暴跳如雷呀——还是当着她死去的老子的尸首！有些人可真是怪！”

“普卢默太太，”她的同伴煞有介事地宣布，“是实话才扎心呐！你记得老话怎么说的：‘打着的狗才叫唤！’‘无风不起浪！’看来咱们说的也不算离谱……我看那丫头是得留点儿神。”……

---

第二天一早，罗杰斯太太到了。她一进屋，米米就奔过去迎接。姨妈把她搂进怀里，对米米轻声说着温存安慰的话，米米即刻感到了一位朋友、一位同盟的到来。罗杰斯太太中等身材，比让年轻，可同她弟弟那样相像，让看上去不过是她添上了一头剪短的白发、一撮髭须和一小绺胡子的复制品。

“可怜的、亲爱的米米，”她低声说，眼睛湿了。她把米米紧紧搂在怀里，倾泻出一片真正的温情，那感情毫无保留地油然而生，在一瞬间的心领神会与爱里把她们联结在一起。……

---

丧礼后两天，罗杰斯太太回纽约之前，米米同她姨妈长谈了一次。

===Original===

“What are your plans, Mimi? What are you going to do?”

“I don’t know, Aunt Sophie﻿—this thing has come on me so suddenly I’m all upset. I suppose I’ll stay on here as long as I can get along with Mrs. Daquin﻿—”

“Why did Jean marry her? She’s not our sort at all﻿—an estimable woman, I suppose, but﻿—well, she just isn’t the sort﻿—not Margot’s type at all.”

Mimi told her the circumstances of the marriage, of the years of misunderstanding which had followed the union.

“Just like Jean to do a thing like that,” his sister observed when Mimi had finished. “Why stay on here with her? Won’t you go back to New York with me?

“Oh, Aunt Sophie, I’d love to﻿—but I can’t leave Atlanta right now﻿—”

“What’s his name?” Mrs. Rogers demanded with what to Mimi seemed uncanny shrewdness.

“Carl Hunter,” she answered frankly, her face reddening. “You see, he needs me.” And she told the story simply, fully, knowing that her aunt would understand. When she finished, Mrs. Rogers smiled sympathetically.

“I see﻿—I see. But if ever things get unpleasant here, send me a wire and come on to New York. I’ve been pretty lonely since Henry died and I’ve always wanted a daughter. I’d try to make you happy and I don’t think there’s overmuch happiness here for you with Jean’s widow. And up North you’d have a much better chance than here where all you can do is to teach school or get married. The last isn’t bad if you get the right man﻿—but, don’t hurry﻿—a girl with your looks and brains can go a long way if she only keeps her head.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“你有什么打算，米米？往后怎么办？”

“我不知道，苏菲姨妈——这事来得太突然，我全乱了。我想我就在这儿住下去，只要还能同达坎太太处得来——”

“让怎么会娶了她？她根本不是我们这一类人——我猜是个挺受人敬重的女人吧，可是——唉，她就是不对路——完全不是玛戈那一型。”

米米给她讲了这桩婚事的来由，讲了婚后的那些误解的年头。

“做这种事，正是让的为人，”米米讲完，他妹妹说。“那何必还同她住下去？你不跟我回纽约去吗？”

“哦，苏菲姨妈，我愿意极了——可眼下我还不能离开亚特兰大——”

“他叫什么名字？”罗杰斯太太问，那副精明劲儿在米米看来近乎料事如神。

“卡尔·亨特，”她坦然回答，脸红了。“您瞧，他需要我。”她把事情简单地、原原本本地讲了，知道姨妈会理解。她讲完，罗杰斯太太同情地笑了。

“我明白——我明白。不过这儿的事若有一天不好过了，给我拍个电报，就上纽约来。亨利去世后我一直相当孤单，我又一直想要个女儿。我会想法子叫你快活的，我看你同让的遗孀住在这儿，不会有太多的快活。再说在北方，你的机会比这儿多得多——这儿你能做的无非是教书或者嫁人。嫁人若嫁对了人倒也不坏——可是，别急——像你这样模样和脑子的姑娘，只要沉得住气，能走得远着呢。……”

## XII / 十二
===Original===

She would not have believed that so soon after Jean’s death she could find herself settling back into a routined life. At first there had been the long, empty days and the longer nights when the house seemed a vast, empty place from which the spirit or thing or whatever it might be called which had made it different from other places had fled. But Mrs. Daquin had removed most of the things which too vividly brought back memories of Jean.

“There’s no use of keeping things that only make you sad,” she had said. “I get the creeps every time I go to see Mrs. Simpkins﻿—those dried flowers from her husband’s funeral hanging in the frame in her parlour. There’ll be nothing like that around here﻿—a dead person’s dead and you can’t bring him back, no matter how much you loved him.”

At first Mimi had objected strenuously to Mrs. Daquin’s seemingly ruthless disposal of all the things which to her were so intimately associated with Jean. They had, however, brought little furniture from New Orleans, only Jean’s books and a few odds and ends. These latter Mimi removed to her own room and then she willingly consented to whatever changes her stepmother desired. A new parlour set was purchased as soon after the funeral as was decently possible. New wallpaper appeared in the long dark hall and in Mrs. Daquin’s bedroom. Out, too, went the bedroom furniture of mottled walnut. A brass bed with frolicsome curves and angles of shiny metal contrasting with the cloudy dullness of the heavier portions took its place. The imperfect set of plain white china with the narrow border of gold and blue was stored in the attic. In its stead appeared china of awe-inspiring combinations of bluebirds kissing on the wing, many cupolaed pagodas, sampans rowed by fat-bellied Chinamen, and trees of a design never produced even by a profligate Nature. Mimi took only a perfunctory interest in these changes other than vouchsafing a listless “They’re pretty,” when pressed for comment by Mrs. Daquin.

===Chinese===

她本不会相信，让死后这么快，她竟能重新落回一种按部就班的生活。起初是长长的、空落落的白昼，和更长的黑夜，那房子像一个广漠的空场，把那使它有别于旁处的精灵——或者魂魄，或者随便叫它什么——已经遁走了。可达坎太太把大多数过于鲜活地勾起让的回忆的东西都清走了。

“留着那些只会叫人伤心的东西有什么用，”她说。“我每次去看辛普金斯太太都起鸡皮疙瘯——她丈夫葬礼上的干花，还挂在客厅的相框里。咱们这儿不许有那一套——人死了就死了，你再爱他，也换不回来。”

起初，米米竭力反对达坎太太这种近乎无情的处置，那些东西在她看来都同让紧紧相连。不过，他们从新奥尔良带来的家具本就不多，只有让的书和一些零七八碎。后者米米搬进了自己房间，此后继母想怎么变，她都欣然同意。丧礼一过、过得去的第一时间，就买了一套新客厅家具。幽暗的长走廊和达坎太太的卧室糊了新墙纸。那套斑纹核桃木的卧室家具也请了出去，换上一张铜床，闪亮的金属弯出嬉闹的曲线和棱角，同厚重部分云翳般的暗哑恰成对照。那套镶着金蓝细边的素白瓷器，残缺不全，收进了阁楼。顶替上来的是另一些瓷器，花样惊心动魄：凌空相吻的蓝鸟，顶着层层宝塔的宝塔，由大肚子中国佬划桨的舢板，还有一种连挥霍的大自然也从没造出来的树。这些变动米米只敷衍地关心一下，达坎太太逼她发表意见时，她有气无力地赏一句“挺好看”。

===Original===

Mimi at times was amazed at her ready acceptance of the uneventful life. She was grateful that she had apparently been wrong in her estimation of her stepmother. Mrs. Daquin seemed changed, chastened by Jean’s death. She had really loved her husband in her own fashion even when she had been most irritated by what to her seemed his lack of intelligence in grasping opportunities for advancement or gain. Now that he was dead, she found herself regarding him not in the light of the later years but more as she had during their short courtship and the first year of their marriage. This mood was sustained by the very evident advantages which she found coming to her as a recently bereaved widow. She knew that in time this too would pass. In time Jean would be but a faint memory to most of those with whom she associated.

It was not conscious dishonesty nor was she a deliberate *poseuse* when she accepted the little attentions, the deference which they bestowed on her. Mrs. Daquin found herself for the first time a personage in her own right. All her youth she had lived under the dominating influence of her father, and even after her marriage to so retiring a person as Jean, she found that her handicap in being a woman in a conventional society was very great. Her father had not attended the funeral but he had wired and written her repeatedly urging her to return to Chicago. This she resolved not to do﻿—at least not unless circumstances compelled such a step﻿—for she was enjoying too greatly for immediate surrender her new importance.

The mood thus engendered worked to Mimi’s advantage though she did not fully understand the reasons for that good fortune. Mrs. Daquin was kindly in her manner, far more so than she had ever been before. When after a few weeks they began to be seen on the streets, Mimi was always to be seen with her stepmother, both clad in the depressingly sombre blacks of sorrow which custom demanded. Mimi’s oval, cream-coloured face topped by its aureole of reddish gold peeped out with startling beauty from the black bonnet tied under her chin with strings of the same colour. Mrs. Daquin did not fare so well in her melancholy raiment, her brown skin and hair offering not so marked a contrast as in Mimi’s case. She suffered the loss of comeliness cheerfully, however, for its added advantages in sympathetic attention.

===Chinese===

米米有时自己也惊讶，竟然这样轻易地接受了这种波澜不惊的日子。她庆幸自己对继母的估量显然是错了。达坎太太像是变了个人，让的死使她收敛了。她其实是以她自己的方式爱着丈夫的，哪怕在她最被他恼火的时候——在她看来，他抓住进阶或捞钱的机会时总显得不够灵光。如今他死了，她发觉自己看待他，不再是从前那些年头的眼光，而更多是像他们短短的求婚期和婚后头一年那样。这种心境又得到滋养——她发现自己作为新丧的寡妇，种种显见的好处源源而来。她知道这也终将过去。到时候，对与她来往的大多数人而言，让不过是一段淡淡的记忆。

她受下那些小小的殷勤、那些敬重，既不是有意的作伪，也算不上一个处心积虑的装腔作势者。达坎太太生平第一次发觉自己成了个独立要人。她整个青年时代都活在父亲的威权之下，及至嫁给让这样一个退让的人，她发现在一个因循的社会里，做个女人本身就是很大的不利。她父亲没有来送葬，只是接连打电报、写信催她回芝加哥去。这事她决计不做——至少，不做，除非形势逼到那一步——因为她正把这份新的体面受用得太深，不肯即刻缴械。

这样酿成的心境对米米倒有利，尽管她并不完全明白这份好运的缘由。达坎太太态度和气了，比以往任何时候都和气。几个星期后她们重新在街上露面时，旁人看见的米米总是同继母在一起，两人都穿着习俗要求的、叫人心里发沉的一身黑丧服。米米那张鹅蛋形的奶油色脸庞，衬着红金色的光环似的头发，从黑帽子里探出来，美得惊人，帽子用同样黑色的带子系在下巴底下。达坎太太穿起这一身哀伤的行头就吃亏些，她棕色的皮肤和头发，不像米米那样形成强烈的对照。不过容貌上的损失她甘之如饴，因为换来的同情关注更划算。

===Original===

Careless as usual of material things, they found Jean had not made a will. Mr. Hunter had volunteered to relieve them of the burdens of settling the estate and his report had shown that not much had been left. Jean had not secured the full value of his property in New Orleans and the cash he had secured had been invested largely in purchasing stock in the Lincoln Mutual Insurance Company. This would in time prove a sound investment, but at present there was little return from it. The deeds to the house given them by Mr. Robertson had been made out in Mrs. Daquin’s name. Mr. Robertson volunteered to contribute a definite sum each month to their support when he saw that his daughter had no intentions of returning to Chicago as he wished.
