Mimi, trained to do no work which would bring her appreciable income or which she could have accepted without lowering their social status, found herself faced with the Hobson’s choice of teaching school or enduring the loss of many little comforts and even a few necessities which before had seemed to her so natural she had never given thought to their source. She did not grumble but began at once to prepare for the examination as a schoolteacher in the spring. In fact, she was glad to find a definite thing on which to concentrate﻿—it served as an opiate when Jean’s loss seemed too great to bear.﻿ ﻿…

---

Into this new life Carl came with much larger importance than Mimi had supposed was possible. In time Mrs. Daquin, who had never been able to find out from Mimi the purpose of Mrs. Hunter’s mysterious call but who had linked it up after a fashion with Carl and Mimi, offered no objection to Carl’s visits to the house. This lack of hostility changed in time to actual welcome due to the increased cordiality of Mrs. Hunter towards Mrs. Daquin which the latter sensed was in large measure because of his visits. This welcome was strengthened by Carl’s evident reformation. No longer was he seen with questionable companions. He had not been seen under the influence of liquor since he had begun again to visit Mimi, and every Sunday now he was to be seen with his parents and sister at church.

===Chinese===

让同往常一样不管身外之物，他们发现他没有立遗嘱。亨特先生自告奋勇替她们卸下料理遗产的担子，他的报告表明，留下的东西不多。让没有把新奥尔良的产业卖出足价，到手的现钱又大半买了林肯互助保险公司的股票。长远看这会是一笔牢靠的投资，眼下却几乎没有收益。罗伯逊先生赠的那幢房子的地契，写的是达坎太太的名字。罗伯逊先生见女儿无意照他希望的那样回芝加哥，便主动每月贴她们一笔固定的生活费。米米呢——她受的教养是不做任何能带来像样收入的工作，或者说不做任何她接受起来不致降低她们社会身份的事——如今发现自己面临着一个毫无余地的选择：要么去教书，要么失去许多小小的舒适、甚至几样必需品，那些东西从前在她看来天经地义，从未想过它们的来路。她没有怨言，立刻着手准备春天考教师资格。其实，有一件明确的事好专心致志，她倒高兴——每逢让的丧失大得叫人承受不住，它便是一剂鸦片。……

---

卡尔走进这份新生活，分量比米米原来以为可能的还要重。到了后来，达坎太太——她始终没能从米米嘴里问出亨特太太那次神秘来访的目的，却多少把它同卡尔和米米联系了起来——对卡尔上门不再表示反对。这点敌意的消融，随着亨特太太对达坎太太日益热络，渐渐变成了实实在在的欢迎，达坎太太也觉出这热络大半是因他的来访而来。这欢迎又因卡尔明显的改邪归正而加重了。再没有人看见他同来路可疑的伙伴在一起。自他重新开始看望米米，再没人见他喝醉过，如今每个星期天，他都随父母和妹妹上教堂。

===Original===

The old comradeship, deepened and enriched in a subtle but unmistakable way, was returning. Memory of that night during which so much had taken place, their reunion, Jean’s death and all the rest, had kept them from the torridity of feeling which had swept over them then. Instead there was a comforting sense of unity which ripened and deepened through the unspoken avowals of their love that flashed between them whenever they met.

This feeling was always deepest as they walked out in the evening. During the latter part of September and the early part of October there had been many crisply cold nights. Dying October and budding November brought that year balmy nights and pleasant days that were reminiscent of late summer. Through the darkened streets they strolled arm in arm. Here and there were street lamps which shed little light more than to a radius of fifteen or twenty feet. Back in tidy little yards sat squat little houses or imposing two-storied ones with an occasional light peeping from them in friendly manner. Up above, a moon, slender and graceful like a fragile, spreading horseshoe of coruscation, or full-bodied and vigorous in its yellowish brilliance. Mimi always liked the new moon best﻿—it stirred her by its slender grace. But Carl preferred the full ones﻿—“no wonder the Greeks and Romans gave a ‘corona’ as a badge of victory﻿—those old boys were poets, and real ones﻿—they saw that there could be no greater beauty and splendour than in an award resembling that band of light up yonder.”

It was not long before they were accepted as “going together.” With the ready forgiveness accorded a man who has transgressed against local codes of conduct, Carl’s derelictions of the past were forgotten, or, if not forgotten, thrust into that indefinite realm of things not to be mentioned again save in most intimate conversations. It was assumed that, now Jean was dead, Mimi would soon be marrying, and the assumption more or less naturally followed that she would marry Carl.

===Chinese===

旧日的情谊回来了，以一种微妙却毋容置疑的方式变得更深、更厚。对那一夜的记挂——那一夜发生了那么多事，他们的重圆，让的死，以及其余一切——使他们避开了当初席卷他们的那股感情的炽烈。取而代之的是一种令人安稳的浑然一体之感，随着他们每次相见时在彼此之间闪烁的无声的爱情表白，日渐成熟、日渐深沉。

这种感觉总在晚间散步时最深。九月末和十月初有过许多干冷的夜。行将谢幕的十月和含苞的十一月那一年却送来了温煦的夜和怡人的昼，叫人想起迟来的夏天。他们挽着臂，在黑下来的街道上漫步。街上隔一截有一盏路灯，照亮的圆径不过十五二十英尺。收拾得整整齐齐的小院落深处，蹲着一幢幢矮墩墩的小屋，或者气派的两层楼，偶尔有一星友好的灯光从里面窥出。头顶上，月亮或纤纤一弯，像一只易碎的、张开的光做的马蹄铁，或浑圆饱满，在淡黄的光华里元气淋漓。米米向来最爱新月——那窈窕的姿容打动她。卡尔却偏爱满月——“难怪希腊人罗马人拿‘冠冕’作胜利的徽记——那些老小子是诗人，真正的诗人——他们看出再没有比一顶像头顶上那道光带的奖赏更美的光彩了。”

不久，他们就被公认为“在处对象”了。本地行为守则对越轨的男子向来宽宏大量，说原谅就原谅，卡尔过去的失检便被忘掉了，或者即便没忘掉，也被塞进那片不成形的地界——那里的事再不得提起，除非在顶顶亲密的私谈里。人们认定，让既已死去，米米不久就要出嫁，而顺着这认定自然而然推下去，她要嫁的就是卡尔。

===Original===

Between them, however, there was no thought of tomorrow nor, for some inexplicable reason, did there come to their ears the now friendly discussions regarding what they would do which were going on. They floated along happily on the stream of their newfound contentment, as oblivious of the future as two bits of wood held together by a string on the bosom of some placid pond. On rainy nights or evenings when Mimi did not feel like walking or going to a moving-picture show, they sat at home, Carl talking or reading some bit of verse, or some story he had come across, while Mimi sat and sewed or just sat. Carl’s restlessness had almost left him, he seemed more contented, he now actually took a deeper interest in the work his father wanted him to do. More and more there entered his conversations his plans, his hopes, his eagerness to please his father. Mimi learned that even under his former discontent there was a deep respect and a sort of affection that Carl had for his father. She saw that the older man’s will worked with great effect upon that of his son﻿—too much so, she feared.﻿ ﻿…

---

Early in November she found that this was even more true than she had suspected. One morning she was awakened by a dream. In it she saw Jean again, standing beside her bed, his eyes sad, his finger pointing accusingly at her. No word came from his lips, but his face had on it an expression she had never seen in life, one that made her shrink from it into the warm, comforting embrace of the bedclothes. She lay awake and then fell again into troubled slumber.

When she awoke she felt a violent nausea and a dizziness that made her glad to lie down again. She called Mrs. Daquin, who listened to her recital of her ailments and then gave her some medicine, advising her to remain in bed. Later Mrs. Daquin brought her breakfast and sat with her as she toyed with it, eating little. It’s nice of her to do this, thought Mimi. She’s getting more lovable every day.

As she still felt badly in the afternoon, Mimi went at her stepmother’s suggestion to see Dr. Adams. A few questions, an examination, and then Dr. Adams looked gravely at Mimi.

“You’re going to have a baby, Mimi,” he told her.

===Chinese===

然而他们之间没有想明天，说来也怪，那些如今已经友好的、关于他们将来如何如何的议论，也一句没有传进他们耳朵。他们顺流漂荡在新得的满足之流上，快快活活，对将来浑然不觉，像被一根线系在一起、浮在某面平静池塘水面的两片木头。逢着雨夜，或者米米不想散步、不想看电影的晚上，他们就待在家里，卡尔说说话，或者读一小节诗、读他偶然碰上的一篇故事，米米坐着做针线，或者只是坐着。卡尔那坐立不安的毛病差不多褪尽了，他显得更安分，如今对他父亲要他做的差事竟真有了更浓的兴趣。他的谈话里越来越多地出现他的计划、他的指望、他讨父亲欢心的急切。米米这才明白，连在他从前那层不满底下，也埋着卡尔对父亲深深的敬意和一种近于亲情的东西。她看得出，老人的意志在儿子的意志上作用极大——她怕，大得过了头。……

---

十一月初，她发现这比她猜度的还要真切。一天早晨，她被一个梦惊醒。梦里她又见到了让，站在她床边，眼睛悲伤，手指着她，像在控诉。他唇间没有一个字，可他脸上有一种她生前从未见过的表情，那表情吓得她直往被子那温暖慰人的怀抱里缩。她醒着躺了一会儿，又坠入不安的睡眠。

再醒来时她一阵剧烈的恶心，一阵眩晕，乐得重新躺下。她叫来达坎太太，达坎太太听她说了病症，给她吃了点药，劝她卧床。后来达坎太太端来早饭，陪着她，看她拨弄着，几乎没吃。她这样真好，米米想。她一天比一天更可爱了。

下午她还是不舒服，便依继母的建议去看亚当斯医生。几个问题，一番检查，然后亚当斯医生神色凝重地望着米米。

“你要有个小宝宝了，米米，”他告诉她。

===Original===

Mimi gasped, echoing his words. A great happiness filled her, happiness mingled with a sweeping flame of love for Carl. A baby! Hers and Carl’s! Her face shone with a great light, the light of contentment and love. There was no feeling of shame nor could she clearly understand why Dr. Adams’ face should remain so grave and worried. On that eventful night she and Carl had been caught up and swept on by a great tide of passion, an emotion overwhelming, beautiful, sacred. She had given of herself freely, without thought of consequences and without any sense of shame. To her it had been a magnificent, a pure and holy giving. Her smile puzzled Dr. Adams.

“Mimi, I’m afraid you don’t realize how serious a thing this is. Do you know what’ll happen to you and your reputation? And who’s the man?”

She told him simply and freely. Still her manner was a source of wonder to him. Ordinarily such a message would have brought forth tears, protestations of innocence, pleas for help. Mimi did none of these.

His face was set in grim lines as she told of Carl.

“The scoundrel! We’ll make him marry you and save your name from being dragged in the mud.”

“No﻿—no, Doctor, you don’t understand. We’ve done nothing wrong, and if we have I’m as much to blame as Carl. Please don’t say anything about this until I see him.﻿ ﻿…”

---

She telephoned Carl, who came over to her house at once. She greeted him happily, the light of love yet shining in her face.

“Carl,” she cried as soon as he entered, “I’m going to have a baby!”

“You’re what?”

“I’m going to have a baby﻿—Dr. Adams told me so this afternoon﻿—I’m so happy,” she ended, a puzzled tone creeping into her voice.

“Did he ask you who was the father?”

“Yes, of course he did.”

“Who did you tell him?”

“Why, you, of course.”

He turned on her roughly.

“You little fool, what did you want to do that for?”

She gazed at him in dismay. She had expected a happiness equal to her own. Instead Carl glared at her as though he wanted to spring at her throat. With his anger was mingled fear﻿—humiliation﻿—she knew not what emotions passed rapidly over his countenance.

“Don’t you tell anybody else,” he demanded. “And tomorrow I’ll take you to a doctor who’ll fix you up﻿—”

“Fix me up?”

“Yes﻿—fix you up! Don’t you understand?”

===Chinese===

米米倒抽一口气，把他的话重了一遍。一股巨大的幸福涨满了她，幸福里掺着对卡尔的一股席卷一切的爱的火焰。一个小宝宝！她的和卡尔的！她的脸放出大团的光，那是满足与爱的光。没有一丝羞耻之感，她也想不明白亚当斯医生的脸为什么那样沉重、那样忧心。在那个决定命运的夜晚，她和卡尔被一股激情的大潮卷起、推涌着，那情感排山倒海、美、神圣。她把自己自由地交了出去，没有想过后果，也没有半点羞耻之感。在她，那是一场堂皇的、纯净而神圣的奉献。她的微笑叫亚当斯医生纳闷。

“米米，我怕你没有意识到这是多么严重的事。你可知道这会给你、给你的名声带来什么？那男的是谁？”

她坦坦白白地告诉了他。她的态度仍旧叫他诧异。换在平常，这样一句话带出来的是眼泪，是喊冤的辩白，是求援的哀告。米米一样也没有。

她说到卡尔时，他的脸绷出冷峻的线条。

“这个恶棍！我们要叫他娶你，不能让你的名声叫人拖进泥里。”

“不——不，医生，您不明白。我们没有做错什么，就算做错了，我的罪过也同卡尔一样重。求您先别声张，等见过他再说。……”

---

她给卡尔打了电话，卡尔立刻赶到她家。她欢欢喜喜地迎他，脸上还闪着爱的光。

“卡尔，”他一进门她就喊，“我要有个小宝宝了！”

“你要有什么？”

“我要有个小宝宝——今天下午亚当斯医生告诉我的——我多快活呀，”她说完，嗓音里爬进一丝困惑。

“他问没问你是谁的孩子？”

“问了，当然问了。”

“你怎么跟他说的？”

“怎么，当然是说你呀。”

他粗暴地冲她发作了。

“你这个傻丫头，你干吗要那样说？”

她惊惶地瞪着他。她原本指望一份同她自己一样大的欢喜。卡尔却朝她瞪眼，像要扑上来掐她的喉咙。他的怒气里掺着恐惧——掺着屈辱——她说不清还有什么情绪在他脸上飞快地掠过。

“你别再告诉任何人，”他命令道。“明天我带你去找个医生，给你‘收拾’了——”

“收拾我？”

“对——收拾了！你还不明白？”

===Original===

Realization of what he meant came slowly to her. A bitterness filled her, bitterness that knew no end, bitterness worse than any she had ever known before. This, then, was the Carl she had loved, a shriveling coward. What a fool she had been to have believed that to him their love was the beautiful thing which it had been to her.

“My dad’ll kill me for this﻿—he’ll drive me away like a dog﻿ ﻿…” Carl was saying hoarsely.

He straightened up, fear leaving him.

“No﻿—no﻿—Mimi. We’ll go straight to mother and she’ll help us. We’ll go away and get married﻿—pretend we eloped.”

He sought to put his arms around her. In his gesture there was a new note of possessiveness, his indecisiveness of the minutes before now gone.

She shrank from his touch. Her head went high and in her eyes was a look that frightened him.

“We’ll do nothing of the kind!” she cried. “I was a fool﻿—you are right. I thought you were fine﻿—clean﻿—different from the others. You’re not. You’re just the same weak, vile sort that you were always hating and denouncing.”

“That’s all right, Mimi,” he sought to quiet her. “You’re all unstrung now. We’ll get married and give the kid a clean name﻿—”

Mimi looked at him and in that glance was something that silenced him.

“Unstrung? Give the baby a clean name? After what you just suggested? You’ll do nothing of the sort! I’m going to have this baby, do you hear me? I’m going to have him and he’s going to be *all mine*. I guess he can get along lots easier with just a bad name than he can with a cowardly father﻿—”

“Mimi, you don’t know what you’re saying!” Carl, now thoroughly alarmed, pleaded. “We’ve got to get married!”

“Got to? Well, we haven’t got to and we won’t! I’ll get along somehow, don’t you worry, but all the love I had for you has turned to black hate! This baby’ll be mine﻿—all mine!”

She rushed from the room, leaving him standing there.﻿ ﻿…

Safe in her room, she let the tears she had fought so hard to check in the parlour below flow without hindrance. As from a distance, she heard the front door slam and she breathed more easily, knowing Carl was no longer in the house.

===Chinese===

她慢慢懂了他的意思。一股苦味充满了她，那苦没有尽头，比她从前尝过的任何苦都苦。这就是她爱过的卡尔了，一个缩成一团的懦夫。她多傻，竟相信他们的爱于他也像于她一样，是件美丽的东西。

“我爹知道了会宰了我——他会像轰狗一样把我轰走……”卡尔声音嘶哑地说着。

他直起身，恐惧离开了。

“不——不——米米。咱们直接去找我妈，她会帮咱们的。咱们走，结婚去——就说是私奔。”

他伸手想搂她。那姿态里有了一种新的、占有的调子，方才那几分钟的犹疑不见了。

她躲开他的碰触。她的头昂起来，眼里有一种叫他害怕的神色。

“咱们才不干那种事！”她喊道。“我是个傻瓜——你说得对。我原以为你高尚——干净——同旁人不一样。你才不是。你同你一直痛骂、一直憎恶的那种软弱下作的货色一模一样。”

“好了好了，米米，”他想法子让她平静。“你现在是心乱了。咱们结了婚，给孩子一个清白的名分——”

米米看了他一眼，那一眼里有个东西叫他哑口无言。

“心乱了？给孩子清白的名分？就在你刚才说的那种话之后？你什么也不用干！我要把这个孩子生下来，你听见没有？我要生下他，他完完全全是我的。我想，他顶着一个坏名声，也比顶着一个懦夫爸爸好过得多——”

“米米，你不知道自己在说什么！”卡尔这下彻底慌了，哀求道。“咱们非结婚不可！”

“非不可？咱们非不可个什么，也决不结！我自有办法活下去，你不用操心，可我对你所有的爱都化成黑漆漆的恨了！这孩子是我的——全是我的！”

她冲出房间，把他一个人撇在那里。……

回到自己房里，安全了，她任凭在楼下客厅里死命忍住的眼泪畅流。她恍惚听见前门砰的一声关上，呼吸松快了些，知道卡尔已经不在屋里了。

===Original===

She wondered why she had acted as she had, knowing as she did the full reason. Bitterness filled her as she went again and again over the scene just ended. Faith, deep, unlimited, had been killed. A bitter laugh mingled with her tears. She saw Carl now for the insincere poseur that he was﻿—him whom she had thought decent and clean. For the first time there came over her a sense of shame﻿—a feeling of guilt. She wondered how Mrs. Daquin would take it. Mimi determined resolutely that if there were a scene or recriminations of any sort she would leave and never return.

From below came the sound of Mrs. Daquin’s voice, calling her. Mimi dried her tears away and went down. Mr. and Mrs. Hunter sat in the parlour. Behind them stood Carl, his head down. Mrs. Hunter rose and attempted to take Mimi in her arms. Mimi evaded her.

“You poor, dear child!” Mrs. Hunter consoled her.

“Thank you, Mrs. Hunter, but I don’t need any pity﻿—it’s your son who needs it more,” Mimi told her. Her voice was hard, flat, cold.

Mrs. Hunter was not to be deterred. “But you and Carl must get married right away and save your name. When the baby’s nearly here, you two can go off on a long trip. Oh, dear, I’m so terribly upset﻿—I almost feel like I’m the guilty one, pleading with you to save him﻿—”

Back and forth the discussion raged. Mr. Hunter urged. His wife pleaded. Mrs. Daquin stormed. But Mimi remained firm.﻿ ﻿…

After they had gone Mrs. Daquin took her turn.

“You silly little fool. You’re crazy. You’re going to get these crazy notions out of your head and marry Carl. I never in all my life heard of so silly a thing as this idea of yours. What if Carl is a weak and worthless scamp? Any kind of a man for a husband is better than none when a girl’s in your fix.﻿ ﻿…”

On and on she went, denouncing, pleading, scorning, appealing.

===Chinese===

她不明白自己怎么会那样行事，其实她一清二楚缘由。一遍遍重温刚才那一场，苦味充满了她。信仰，那深的、无限的信仰，被杀死了。一声苦笑混在眼泪里。她如今看清了卡尔，一个不诚恳的装腔作势之徒——而她竟以为他正派、干净。生平第一次，一种羞耻之感——一种罪疚之感——罩住了她。她不知道达坎太太会怎么看待这事。米米狠下决心：若有一场吵闹或任何指摘，她就走，走了再不回来。

楼下传来达坎太太唤她的声音。米米拭干眼泪下了楼。亨特先生和太太坐在客厅里。他们身后站着卡尔，头垂着。亨特太太站起来想拥抱米米。米米躲开了。

“你这可怜的、亲爱的孩子！”亨特太太安慰她。

“谢谢您，亨特太太，可我不需要怜悯——需要怜悯的是您的儿子，”米米告诉她。她的嗓音又硬又平又冷。

亨特太太不肯罢休。“可你和卡尔必须马上结婚，保住你的名声。等孩子快生了，你们俩出门做一次长途旅行。哦，天哪，我心里乱极了——我几乎觉得自己才是那个罪人，在这儿求你救救他——”

讨论来讨论去，越吵越凶。亨特先生力劝，他妻子哀求，达坎太太发作。米米却纹丝不动。……

他们走后，轮到达坎太太。

“你个傻乎乎的小傻瓜。你疯了。趁早把你这些疯念头从脑袋里赶出去，嫁给卡尔。我活了一辈子，头一回听说像你这样的傻主意。卡尔又怎么样，一个软弱无能的二流子？姑娘家到了你这步田地，随便什么男人当丈夫，也比没有强。……”

她说个不了，一会儿痛骂，一会儿央告，一会儿讥诮，一会儿动情。

===Original===

Mimi listened to the Niagara of words but they served only to make her more determined to do as she had declared she would. Jean’s words came to her﻿—the advice he had given her long ago as they were about to leave New Orleans﻿—“decide in your own mind the wisest, the best thing to be done, and then do it.” As she looked at the four faces she wondered what Jean would say if he were here now. Even as Mrs. Daquin stormed, Mimi knew there was much right in what she said. She would be condemned, her name derided. She knew she could not remain in Atlanta. Even if she could, the looks of disdain, the insults, would be unbearable. They had done that for years to the girl Carl had been going to see and they had had no definite proof on her of wrong doing.

She wondered what she could do. Where could she go? She had no training by which she could earn a living. And she certainly would not go to any city where she was liable to meet anybody who knew her. She thought of Aunt Sophie and her invitation to come and live with her in New York. No, she couldn’t accept that invitation now. Aunt Sophie would be just like these people here, would hate her for her misdeed. Suppose she did marry Carl. His parents would see that they wanted nothing. But always there would be in their minds, she was sure, the thought that she had come to them under a cloud﻿—that she had done something disgraceful and by that means had married into their family. Mimi felt sure in time they too would hate her as much as she now hated Carl. No, her mind was made up. Whatever she might have to suffer, it was better that she keep her own soul free. That would certainly not be true if she married Carl now.

Wearily she faced her stepmother and spoke. The words came slowly, painstakingly, as though she were explaining a complex matter to a rather stupid child.

“Yes, I know all that you say is true. I am foolish. I am bringing on myself a terrible responsibility. But I can’t marry Carl﻿—not now. He wanted me to go to a doctor﻿—to fix me up﻿—those were his very words. I hate him﻿—and if I live to be a thousand years old I’ll hate him more every day I live. Don’t you worry﻿—I’ll go away﻿—I don’t know where, but anyway you won’t be bothered with me anymore. But I won’t marry Carl. My mind’s made up and I won’t change it.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

米米听着这尼亚加拉瀑布似的一泻千里，可它只使她更铁了心，照她声明的做。让的话来到她心里——那是他们即将离开新奥尔良时他给她的忠告——“自己心里先定下什么是最明智、最好的办法，然后照着做。”她望着那四张脸，心想让此刻若在场会怎么说。哪怕达坎太太正大发雷霆，米米也知道她的话里有不少是对的。她将遭受谴责，她的名字将被人耻笑。她知道自己没法留在亚特兰大了。就算留得下，那一副副鄙夷的眼色、那一次次侮辱，也受不了。卡尔从前常去看的那个姑娘，他们就那样对付了好些年，而她手上并没有抓实什么过错。

她想自己能做什么。能去哪儿？她没有一样谋生的本事。而她断然不肯去任何可能撞见熟人的城市。她想起苏菲姨妈请她去纽约同住的邀请。不，如今她不能接受那番好意了。苏菲姨妈会同这儿这些人一模一样，会为她的罪过恨她。假如她真嫁了卡尔呢？他父母会让他们什么都不缺。可是他们心里会永远——她敢肯定——存着那个念头：她是带着一身污点进他们家的——她干下了丢人的事，靠那个手段嫁进了他们的门。米米深信，到时候他们也会恨她，恨得同她如今恨卡尔一样。不，她主意已定。无论要受什么苦，总好过让灵魂蒙尘。此刻嫁给卡尔，灵魂就保不住了。

她疲惫地面向继母开了口。话说得又慢又吃力，像在同一个相当愚笨的孩子解释一件复杂的事。

“是的，您说的这一切我都明白是真的。我是傻。我在给自己招来可怕的责任。可我不能嫁给卡尔——现在不能。他要我去找个医生——把我‘收拾’了——这可是他的原话。我恨他——哪怕活上一千岁，我也要恨他一天深似一天。您不用操心——我会走——去哪儿我不知道，可无论如何不会再拖累您了。但我不嫁给卡尔。我主意已定，不会改的。……”

===Original===

A few weeks later Mimi boarded a train for Philadelphia. She could give no reason why she chose that city to which to go. The nearest she could explain her choice was that Philadelphia was large, she knew no one there and she was sure she could lose herself in its vastness. Mr. Hunter offered her money but she took only that which was due her through his purchase of half, her half, of the stock Jean had owned. Mrs. Daquin pleaded with her to the hour of departure to marry Carl, but Mimi’s determination grew stronger with her pleading. As the train wound its way through the maze of tracks and puffed its way northward through the bare red hills of Georgia, she gazed from the car-window with the feeling that she had definitely closed the pages of the first book of her life. She stared at the darkening landscape long after the lights in the car were turned on, and wondered what was written on the pages of that second book whose cover she now was lifting.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

几个星期后，米米登上了去费城的火车。她说不出为什么选中那座城。勉强能解释的理由是：费城大，她在那儿谁也不认识，她确信自己能隐没在它的浩大里。亨特先生要给她钱，她只收下该她的那份——他买下了让所持股票的一半，她那一半。达坎太太直到开车那一刻还在恳求她嫁给卡尔，可米米的决心随着恳求愈发坚硬。火车在迷宫般的轨道间蜿蜒穿出，喷着汽朝北驶过佐治亚光秃秃的红土丘陵，她凝望着车窗外面，觉得她生平第一本书的书页已经确确实实合上了。车厢里的灯亮了以后，她还久久望着渐黑下去的景色，想着她此刻正掀开封面那第二本书的书页上写着什么。……

## XIII / 十三
===Original===

Month by month the time rolled slowly by for Mimi. She slept late in the morning, went out for walks in the afternoon or to a moving-picture show, retired early every evening. On Sundays she stole unobtrusively into the Catholic church near where she lived, varying this occasionally with attendance at the Methodist church to which the elderly couple with which she boarded belonged. They were simple, kindly people who made a comfortable living from a small catering business which kept them away from home a great deal. Mimi thus had the house to herself a large part of the time, for which she was grateful, as it relieved her of the strain of meeting and talking to people and answering embarrassing questions.

The old couple frequently speculated to themselves who and what she might be. To the woman’s indirect and friendly questions Mimi gave evasive answers which seemed to satisfy the simple and uninquisitive nature of the elderly woman. To her neighbours she said that Mimi was a young widow grieving for her dead husband, which explanation, in view of the mourning garments Mimi yet wore for Jean, was accepted in good faith. Mimi often wept at the expressions, either by word or unobtrusive actions, of sympathy which came to her from the elderly couple and their friends. And often, too, guilt assailed her, for she felt she was accepting these ministrations under false colours. There were moments when she felt she could not accept them any longer, that she must tell them the true story. Always before taking such a step, however, she restrained her impulses. She knew no one else in Philadelphia who would take her in, she was paying for her room and excellent food a ridiculously low sum, and her little store of money which had seemed so large when Mr. Hunter had given it to her, was shrinking with dismaying speed.

When the long, dreary, slushy days of winter had given way to the invigorating and friendly warmth of an early spring, she spent most of her days sitting in the park. Used to the tropical heat of New Orleans, the biting winds and driving snows of the North made her miserable and depressed, though her years in Atlanta had inured her to a degree against the cold. But she did not like cold weather, and the coming of the days when she could see the delicate green of the sprouting grass and budding trees made her very happy.

===Chinese===

对米米来说，时间一月一月缓缓滚过。她早晨睡到很晚，下午出去散散步，或者看场电影，每晚早早歇息。星期天，她悄悄溜进住处附近的天主教堂，偶尔也换去那一对同她搭伙的老夫妇所属的卫理公会教堂。他们是朴实、和善的人，靠一桩小小的包办酒席生意过挺舒服的日子，那生意使他们常常不在家。于是米米大部分时间独自享有整幢房子，她为此感激，因为省去了见客、寒暄、回答叫人为难的问题的负担。

老两口常常私下揣测她的来历身份。对那妇人旁敲侧击的友好问话，米米给些闪闪烁烁的答复，似乎也满足了老妇人朴实不好奇的天性。她对邻居们说，米米是个哀悼亡夫的年轻寡妇——这个解释，看米米身上还为让穿着的丧服，也就被好心相信了。老两口和他们的朋友用言语或不显眼的举动向米米表示同情，米米常常为之落泪。也常常有负疚袭来，因为她觉得自己是在顶着假名义受这份照拂。有些时刻她觉得自己再也受不下去了，必须把真实情形告诉他们。可每次临到这一步，她又按住了冲动。她在费城再没有第二个可以收留她的人家，她付的房钱和上好的伙食低得可笑，而亨特先生交给她时显得那么大的一小笔积蓄，正以骇人的速度缩水。

漫长、阴郁、雪水泥泞的冬日让位给早春那振奋而友善的暖意之后，她大部分日子都坐在公园里。过惯了新奥尔良的酷热，北方的刺骨寒风和漫天飞雪使她愁苦抑郁，虽然在亚特兰大的那些年多少使她耐了些寒。可她不喜欢冷天，所以等到能望见草芽树苞那嫩绿的日子来临，她快活极了。

===Original===

She would take with her to the park a book or more often a newspaper but she seldom read. She was content to sit and watch children at play or gaze at friendly squirrels who so ludicrously sat rapidly revolving a nut disentombed from earthy caches in their little forepaws as they bit into its hard shell.

Even the sinking of a huge vessel like the *Titanic* after collision with an iceberg off the Canadian coast with great loss of life could not stir her. Nor could the hectic days of an election year stir her from her apathy. A former schoolteacher from New Jersey, a prominent member of Congress, a twice-defeated candidate for the Presidency, a fat, jovial and weak President, and a vigorous ex-President were scrambling madly for nominations, but so far as Mimi was concerned, they might just as well have been struggling for the rulership of an obscure island in the South Seas.

As the time of her ordeal approached she achieved a calmness of which she had never believed herself capable. Her hatred, her contempt for Carl had passed and in its stead had come a complete lack of feeling towards him. She saw him now in perspective more and more clearly as the weak individual he had proved himself to be. She wondered why she had not seen it sooner, why traits of his which now were revealed all too clearly had been invisible to her. His indecisiveness, his succumbing to indifference and easy vices when they had first parted on Hilda’s account, should have been a clear warning to her. She did not object to his drinking or other derelictions on moral grounds. As a matter of fact Carl’s association with the girl who was déclassée had not seemed to her as a thing to be condemned. Towards the girl Mimi had had a very kindly and sympathetic feeling and she had had no word of condemnation or scorn for her. Mimi now realized that she should have seen in these little strayings from which she had saved Carl at such a cost to herself the true measure of Carl’s character. But even as she saw now what might and should have been, she knew that at the time she had been so blinded by her love for Carl she could never have realized the full importance of the things then under her very eyes.

===Chinese===

她去公园总带一本书，更多时候带份报纸，可她很少读。她满足于坐着看孩子们玩耍，或望着那些友好的松鼠——它们用小小的前爪捧着一枚从泥窖里刨出来的坚果，飞快地转动，啃着那硬壳，模样那么滑稽。

就连泰坦尼克号那样的大船在加拿大海岸外撞上冰山沉没、丧生者以千计，也搅不动她。大选之年的沸沸扬扬，也撼不动她的麻木。一位新泽西出身的前教师、一位显赫的国会议员、一位两度败选的总统候选人、一位胖乎乎、乐呵呵而软弱的现任总统，还有一位精力充沛的前总统，正疯狂争夺候选人提名，可是在米米看来，他们争不争，同争夺南太平洋某座无名小岛的统治权没有什么两样。

随着临盆之期临近，她达到一种自己从不相信能有的平静。她对卡尔的恨与鄙薄已经过去，代之而来的是对他全然的没有感觉。如今她越来越清楚地按比例看清了他——他就是他已证明的那种软弱的人。她奇怪自己怎么没有早些看出来，他的那些如今暴露得太清楚的品性，当初怎么在她眼里隐了形。他的优柔寡断，他们头一回为希尔达的缘故分手后他那样沉溺于消沉和轻易沾上的恶习，本该是给她的明白警告。她并不从道德立场反对他喝酒或别的失检。事实上，卡尔同那个跌出体面阶层的姑娘来往，在她看来也并非什么该谴责的事。对那姑娘，米米一直怀着很善良、很同情的感觉，对她说不出一句贬责或轻蔑的话。米米如今才明白，她本该从那些小小的失足中——她为把卡尔从那些失足里拉出来付出了那么大的代价——看清卡尔品格的真实尺寸。可即便如今看清了本来可以、也应该看到的东西，她知道，当时她被对卡尔的爱蒙得那样瞎，眼下这些事的全部分量，她是万万认识不到的。

===Original===

Her sense of contentment had its roots in a deep spiritual awareness which gave her great comfort. She seldom thought now of the condemnation she was doubtless receiving in Atlanta. Instead she was happy, very happy she had acted as she had. She and the baby would get along somehow and they would be very happy together. Lacking respect, despising Carl, she would never have been happy with him, and her religion would not have allowed her to divorce him. What though her money was disappearing so rapidly? By economy she would surely have enough to last her through her confinement and permit her to spend a few weeks in the country until she was strong again. Then she could easily get a job and she and the baby would be happy together.

She loved the restless stirring within her body, she was happy at the signs of creation. It was a sensation at times beautiful, at other times she was overwhelmed with the marvellousness of it all. She experienced little surges of exultant joy that within herself she too had spiritual reserves which kept her soul free and intact despite what the world might say. Gone was the sense of being a depraved, a disgraced, a low creature which had assailed her those last few days in Atlanta. She was free! Free! Free!

Round and round she twisted the wide gold band on her left hand. This she was ashamed of after a fashion. Mrs. Manning, with whom she lived, had glanced significantly one day at her left hand as she sought gently to induce Mimi to talk. To avoid suspicion Mimi had gone to a pawnshop on South Street into whose windows she often glanced as she passed, fascinated by the clusters of knives and revolvers and boxing-gloves and baseball-mitts. Subconsciously a small sign had impressed itself upon her memory. It read:

Large Assortment of Wedding Rings for Sale Cheap

She knew that the bearded Jew who sold her the ring guessed her secret, her guilt had made her so nervous. She had taken the first one that fitted her finger, paid him to his joy and amazement the first price he had named, and hurried from the shop. It was a cheap and awkward-looking affair but it was an orthodox wedding ring. Whenever she glanced at it or felt it on her finger a wave of guilt, a depressing sense of her dishonesty, swept over her, but she kept it on whenever she emerged from her room, for it saved her embarrassing questions and kept down talk.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

她的满足之感扎根在一种深深的心灵觉醒里，那给她很大的安慰。她如今很少想到她在亚特兰大无疑正领受着的谴责。相反，她为自己这样行事觉得幸福，非常幸福。她和宝宝总会过下去，他们会快快活活在一起。不敬重卡尔、鄙薄卡尔，同他在一起她永远不会幸福，而她的宗教又不许她同他离婚。钱花得这样快又何坊？省着点，定够她撑过临盆，还许她在乡间住上几个星期，等重新硬朗起来。到那时找份差事还不容易，她和宝宝就快快活活在一起了。

她爱那在她身体里不宁静的骚动，她为创造的种种征兆而欢喜。那感觉有时美极了，有时她又为这一切的奇妙所压倒。她体验到一阵阵欢腾的喜悦：她自己心里也有灵性的储备，任凭世人说什么，它们都保着她的灵魂自由而完整。在亚特兰大最后那几天袭击过她的那种自甘堕落、身败名裂、低人一等之感，已经去了。她自由了！自由了！自由了！

她一圈一圈捻着左手无名指上那只宽宽的金戒指。这玩意儿她多少有点羞愧。与她同住的曼宁太太，有一天想温和地引米米说话，意味深长地照了一眼她的左手。为避嫌疑，米米去了南街一家当铺——她路过时常朝那橱窗里张望，被那些成挂的刀子、手枪、拳击手套和棒球手套迷住。潜意识里，一块小招牌印进了她的记忆。上面写着：

廉价出售 各式结婚戒指

卖给她的那个大胡子犹太人猜着了她的秘密，她知道——负疚使她那样紧张。她拿了头一枚合手指的，按他开的第一个价钱付了钱——叫他又惊又喜——便匆匆出了店。那是个便宜、笨拙的玩意儿，却是一枚正派的结婚戒指。每当她瞥见它，或觉出它套在指头上，一股负疚、一阵沮丧的欺诈之感便涌上心头，可只要一出房门她就戴着它，因为它替她挡开叫人难堪的问题，压住闲话。……

===Original===

---

One question occupied her more than any other. What her future and that of the child might be did not worry her half so much as did the sex of this stranger from another world who soon would be with her. She wanted it to be a girl on some days, more often she eagerly wished for a boy. Marriage for herself was now obviously out of the question﻿—a boy would be less trouble and there would be fewer people to demand of him the story of his parentage than would be the case with a girl. Day after day this speculation went on endlessly, and she always came back to the exact spot in her reasoning from which she had begun.﻿ ﻿…

---

It was a boy. A hot night in early July saw his entry into the world after two days and two nights of pain which tore Mimi’s body with its burning shafts of agony. When it was all over she lay in her narrow cot in the maternity ward of the public hospital and passed her hand lovingly over the tiny, shapeless mass of red flesh. There had never been any question in her mind regarding the name she should give him﻿ ﻿… Jean, of course. For his sake she had cheerfully lied in answering the usual form questions.﻿ ﻿… She had hoped to take at least a private room. But she had spent her money faster than she had realized and the private room would have made the weeks in the country impossible.﻿ ﻿…

She was glad afterwards she had put up with the lack of privacy and the other inconveniences of the public ward. Mr. Manning had arranged for her to stay with a friend of theirs in New Jersey and there Mimi was so contented, so happy, in the little cottage near Camden, she wished she could have remained there always. She loved to give Jean his bath, to feed him, to shower on him little attentions and superfluous affections. His clutching hands dug into her flesh as he nursed and the exquisite pain of it sent deliriously exhilarating tingles throughout her body. And she talked to him as though he were old enough to understand, whenever she was certain no one could overhear her.

“You’re mine, baby Jean, all mine.﻿ ﻿… No other person owns any part of you.﻿ ﻿… I’ll work for you, sacrifice everything for you﻿ ﻿… and we’ll be happy, so happy, together.﻿ ﻿… You’ll never know the agony your mother went through.﻿ ﻿… I’ll give everything gladly to save you and keep you free.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

---

有一个问题比别的一切更占着她的心。她自己和孩子的前途会怎样，还不及这位即将来到她身边的、来自另一个世界的陌生人的性别一半叫她挂心。有些天她想要个女孩，更多时候她热切盼望是个男孩。她自己的婚姻如今显然谈不上——男孩子省心些，将来要他交代身世的人也少些。这番揣想日复一日没完没了地转，每次都回到她出发的那个原点。……

---

是个男孩。七月初一个闷热的夜里，经过两天两夜撕扯着米米身体的、烧红的痛苦之矛的刑罚，他来到了世间。一切结束后，她躺在公立医院产科病房她那张窄床上，慈爱地抚过那一小团没有形状的红肉。该给他起什么名字，她心里从来没有犹豫过……当然叫让。为了他，她回答那些例行表格问题时快快活活撒了谎。……她原指望至少住个单人病房。可她的钱花得比她意识到的还快，住单人病房，去乡间的那几个星期便不可能了。……

事后她庆幸自己忍受了没有隐私和公立大病房的种种不便。曼宁先生安排她到他们在新泽西的一位朋友家去住，在卡姆登近旁那座小屋里，米米那样满足、那样幸福，恨不得永远住下去。她爱给小让洗澡，喂他，把小小的殷勤和多到多余的爱倾泻在他身上。他吃奶时两只紧抓的手抠进她的肉里，那美妙的疼使一阵迷醉的震颤传遍她全身。她还同他说话，只在没有一个人听得见的时候，仿佛他已大到能听懂了。

“你是我的，小让，全是我的。……没有第二个人拥有你的任何一小块。……我要为你做工，为你牺牲一切……我们要快快活活，快快活活在一起。……你永远不会知道你妈妈吃过的苦。……我甘愿交出一切来救你、护你周全。……”

===Original===

All too soon she was forced to return to Philadelphia. Up to this time Mimi had refused to permit herself to worry over the problem of earning a living for herself and little Jean. She told the Mannings frankly of her financial condition. They so readily showed their willingness to help her, it brought tears of gratitude that could not be checked. Her regular hours during the long months when she was awaiting Jean’s arrival, the willingness she had always shown to help with any task around the house, her regular church attendance, had all combined to endear her to the elderly couple who had no children of their own.

“Don’t you worry one little bit, honey,” Mrs. Manning told her. “You’ll never have to worry about rent or food﻿—so long’s we’ve got anything to eat and a roof over our heads you’ve them too.”

They sat in the dining-room until long after midnight the evening Mimi returned from the country, devising ways and means of Mimi’s earning in some way enough to supply the needs of herself and “Petit Jean,” as she called the baby, to differentiate him from the Jean who had gone. Mr. Manning scratched his white head and pursed his lips as he gazed at the ceiling.

“You say you haven’t been taught a trade﻿—you’re too good for housework﻿—hm﻿—let me see﻿—let me see‑e‑e,” he spoke half to himself, half to the two women. Mimi volunteered the information that she could sew rather well. “You can? Why’n’t you tell me that before? Why, that settles it all! I’ll speak to some of my customers and get you plenty of work doing sewing by the day.” A smile, beautiful in its radiant joy at solution of the vexing problem, wreathed his face.

“But what about the baby?” his wife inquired. Mr. Manning’s face fell, wiped as clean of its elation as a blackboard when a damp cloth is passed over it. “I’ll tell you what we can do,” Mrs. Manning went on. “Days when I’m away we can put him in a day nursery.”

===Chinese===

可惜好日子太快就到了头，她不得不返回费城。到这时为止，米米一直不许自己为她和小让的生计发愁。她坦白把自己的经济状况告诉了曼宁夫妇。他们那样爽快地表示愿意帮她，感动得她眼泪止不住。她等待小让降生的那长长的几个月里作息有常，又一向乐于帮着做家务，又按时上教堂，这一切合起来，叫这对没有儿女的老两口把她疼到了心坎里。

“你一点儿也不用愁，宝贝儿，”曼宁太太对她说。“房租呀饭食呀，你永远不用愁——只要咱们还有一口吃的、头上有片瓦，就也有你的。”

米米从乡间回来的那天晚上，他们坐在饭厅里，直谈到后半夜，替米米设法子挣出够她自己和“小让”——她这样叫这娃娃，好同那位已经去了的让区分开——用的钱。曼宁先生挠着他那白脑袋，嘬着嘴唇，眼睛望着天花板。

“你说你没学过手艺——做家务又屈才——唔——让我想想——让我想——想，”他半自语半对两个女人说。米米主动提供了一条信息：她缝纫手艺相当好。“你会？怎么不早告诉我？嗨，这不全结了！我去同几个主顾说说，给你揽来充足的按日计活的缝纫活儿。”一个问题迎刃而解，那放射着喜悦的光辉的笑容，在他脸上绕成了一圈。

“那娃娃怎么办？”他妻子问。曼宁先生的脸沉下来，方才的得意一扫而光，像湿布擦过的黑板。“我告诉咱们能怎么办，”曼宁太太接着说。“我不在家的日子，咱们送他上日托育婴所。”

===Original===

Mimi was not allowed to begin her new life for some time, however. On one pretext after another her day of beginning work was deferred by the Mannings. The most frequent excuse was that most of the people who needed the services of a seamstress had gone away for the summer. Though Mimi had now been in Philadelphia more than eight months she knew little of the city. Her life before the baby was born had been so limited a one she had seldom been farther from home than a few blocks. The hugeness of the city, its teeming streets, the roar of traffic, the hurrying throngs, each person in it set on his own affairs to the exclusion of everything and everybody else, frightened her. When she ventured out alone she always had the feeling as though some huge hand had picked her up and thrown her into a raging torrent. And always she regained the haven of home with a prayer of thanksgiving in her heart that she had not been killed in the bustle by one of the wagons or trucks that rushed down upon one with such terrifying speed. Used to the somnolence of New Orleans and the lesser traffic of Atlanta, she often wondered why she had ever chosen Philadelphia as her city of refuge﻿—“City of Brotherly Love” was certainly an anachronism to a stranger like herself.

She was happy with the Mannings but her inaction worried her. Except for a few dollars she was now completely without funds. She had had to buy a number of things for the baby but even though she had made most of his clothes herself, cloth and thread and buttons cost. She did not tell the Mannings of her worries, for they invariably sought to dismiss them from her mind. But she had overheard snatches of their conversation when they did not know she was near﻿—in the small house a secret conversation was most difficult﻿—and she had noticed little economies they practised, and not because of parsimony. The two were getting old, there were fewer and fewer calls every year now for their services. Younger and more progressive people were gobbling the bulk of the catering jobs. Their older customers they yet retained, but some of these were dying off and others entertained less, for they too were getting old. Their sons and daughters who now had their own homes went to the established firms where newer and fresher and more bizarre effects could be obtained than those of the old order furnished by the Mannings.

===Chinese===

然而，米米的新生活没有立刻开始。曼宁夫妇借着一个又一个由头，把她开工的日子往后推。最常用的借口是，需要缝娘伺候的人家大多避暑去了。米米来到费城虽已八个多月，对这城市却知道得很少。孩子出生前她的生活圈子那样小，很少走出离家几个街区以外。这城市的庞大、炤攘的街道、车流的轰响、匆匆的人潮——里面的每一个人都只顾自己的事，别的什么都不管、谁都不管——叫她害怕。她独自出门时，总觉得像被一只巨手拎起来，抛进了湍急的洪流。而每次回到家的港湾，她心里总念着感恩的祷告，谢天谢地没有被那以骇人速度冲过来的运货马车或卡车在乱哄哄里碾死。过惯了新奥尔良的酣眠和亚特兰大清淡的车马，她常想自己当初怎么会挑中费城做避难的城——“友爱之城”对她这样一个异乡人，实在是个名不副实的旧称。

她同曼宁夫妇在一起很快活，可这份闲散叫她心焦。除了几块钱，她如今已彻底没有进项。她不得不给宝宝添置了许多东西，虽说衣服大半自己做，可布头、线、纽扣都是钱。她没把愁事告诉曼宁夫妇，因为他们总是想法子替她排解。可她无意间听到过他们不知道她在近旁时的只言片语——小房子里，密谈最难——她也注意到他们实行的小小节俭，那并非出于吝啬。两位老人上了年纪，请他们办酒席的一年比一年少。更年轻、更时新的人把大宗的生意吞吃了。老主顾他们还留着几个，可有的相继故去，有的也老了，宴饮少了。他们的儿女如今有了自己的家，都上老字号的铺子去了——那里能办出比曼宁夫妇那种旧式派头更新鲜、更花哨的花样。

===Original===

Having always been thrifty, the Mannings had saved some money, but they knew and Mimi knew that they would need all of this in the years to come when even the little work they now did would be gone and they would be too old to do any work at all. Mimi was miserable when she thought of this and she seemed to herself to be a leech feeding off the bodies of these two who had been so generous to her, a stranger.

And this guilty feeling was added to when she thought, as she frequently did, that she had eaten of their bread under false colours. Mrs. Manning might as well have applied hot irons to Mimi’s flesh unwittingly as to mention Mimi’s “late husband.” Never was there any malice or inquisitiveness in her voice, and that lack of suspicion hurt Mimi a thousand times more than accusations and recriminations.

“If your poor husband had only lived to see the baby!” or “Does the boy take after you or your husband?” she would say gently, and her innocent words would make Mimi feel like a distillation of all the Judas Iscariots, the Benedict Arnolds, the Tartuffes, the Pharisees and all the other deceivers and hypocrites of history. Now that she had regained her strength, her inaction annoyed her, the very food she ate at times almost choked her because after all it was charity and more, charity granted through deceit and lies. Time and time again she determined to blurt out the truth to the Mannings whatever the result might be, but each time she decided to put off her confession until she was earning enough money to provide for the baby at least if the Mannings should turn against her. She justified this in her own mind by assuring herself that had she been alone she would have told them her story long since whatever the consequences might have been. But when she saw “Petit Jean” cooing and kicking in the old-fashioned crib which Mr. Manning had brought down from the attic where it had lain for many years (they had had one child who was born dead), Mimi felt she could not in common sense risk the possibility of the baby’s suffering.

===Chinese===

曼宁夫妇一向节俭，攒下了几个钱，可他们知道、米米也知道，往后的年头要用的就是这些——到那时连眼前这点活计也没有了，他们也老得什么活都做不动了。米米想到这些就难受，她觉得自己像一条水蛭，叮在这两位待她这个外人如此慷慨的人身上。

而当她想到——她常想——自己是顶着假名义吃他们的面包，这负疚又添了一层。曼宁太太每每一提米米“故去的丈夫”，就等于不知不觉把烧红的烙铁贴上米米的肉。她的语气里从来没有恶意，没有打探，而这份不疑，比指摘和怨责更伤米米一千倍。

“你那可怜的丈夫要是活着看得见这娃娃多好！”或者“这孩子像你还是像你丈夫？”她会轻轻这样说，而这些无辜的字眼叫米米觉得自己是历代一切犹大、一切卖国贼、一切伪君子、一切法利赛人、一切骗子和假善人的精华蒸馏。如今体力既已恢复，这份闲散就叫她恼恨，她吃下的每口饭有时几乎噎住她，因为那到底是施舍，而且是顶着欺瞒和谎话得来的施舍。她一次又一次下定决定，不管后果如何也要向曼宁夫妇和盘托出，可每一次又决定推迟忏悔，等她挣的钱够养活宝宝——万一曼宁夫妇翻脸，至少宝宝有着落。她在心里这样给自己辩解：若是她孤身一人，早就把身世讲给他们了，不管后果怎样。可是一看见“小让”在曼宁先生从阁楼上搬下来的那张老式摇篮里（他们曾有过一个生下来就死了的孩子）咕咕叫着、蹬着腿，米米觉得，凭常理她不能拿宝宝受苦的可能去冒险。

===Original===

Her qualms of conscience were solved for her in an unexpected manner. Early in November the first sleet and snow of the year came down upon the city. Mr. Manning was returning home after a shopping tour, a heavy basket on his arm. Nearing the house, he slipped and fell. The doctor set the broken hip and announced that he would be confined to his bed for many weeks. Mimi stayed as long as she felt she was needed, she did not want Mrs. Manning to feel she would desert her when her services were needed. But she located a boarding place, arranged for Petit Jean to stay by day at a nursery, and secured work sewing by the day through scanning the want ads and inquiry through the Mannings and one of their friends. The elderly couple objected to her going but they were not adroit enough in concealing their feelings to keep from her a note of relief.﻿ ﻿…

Month after month of the drab life rolled by. At her new home Mimi realized now how kind the Mannings had really been to her. Not that her new landlady was unkind. She too was good in her way, but lacking in understanding, in sympathy. Because her income was uncertain and she knew there would be days when she would have no work, Mimi had taken a place where she could obtain lodging at the lowest figure possible and at the same time secure respectability. Early in the morning she rose, gave Jean his bath and dressed him, prepared his breakfast and her own, took him to the nursery and went to her work. Returning in the evening, she brought him home, prepared him for bed, got her dinner and soon afterwards retired. This regular schedule was broken only by the all-too-frequent days when she had no work. The one bright spot in the week was Sunday, when she kept Jean with her and took him for short walks. She gave up attendance at church, for she was envious of anything which took her away from Jean.

===Chinese===

她的良心不安，以一种意想不到的方式替她解了。十一月初，这一年的头一场雨夹雪和雪落到了城里。曼宁先生采购回来，臂上捥着沉甸甸的篮子。快到家时，他滑倒了。医生给接上了摔断的胯骨，说他要卧床许多个星期。米米待到觉得再帮得上忙的时候就走——她不愿意曼宁太太觉得，正需要人手的时候她会弃之而去。可她找好了一处搭伙的寓所，安排小让白天上育婴所，又通过细读招工广告、通过曼宁夫妇和他们的一位朋友问询，找到了按日计活的缝纫活儿。老两口拦她，可他们遮掩的手段不到家，没能瞒住话音里那一丝如释重负。……

单调的岁月一月一月滚过去。到了新住处，米米这才明白曼宁夫妇待她实在有多好。倒不是新房东太太刻薄。她按她的路数也算好人，只是缺了体谅，缺了同情。因为进项没有准，又知道总有不开工的日子，米米挑了个能以最低的价钱落脚、同时又保全体面的地方。清晨她早早起来，给小让洗澡、穿衣，预备他的早饭和自己的，送他去育婴所，然后去做工。傍晚回来，领他回家，安顿他睡下，自己吃饭，随后不久也就歇了。这刻板的日程，只被那些太常见的无工可做的日子打破。一个星期里唯一的亮点是星期天，那天她把小让留在身边，带他出去散短短的步。教堂她不去了——凡是把她从小让身边拉走的东西，她都要妒忌。

===Original===

Of social life or recreation there was none for her. She could not afford to spend money for even moving-picture shows. She occasionally visited the Mannings, telling them glowing accounts of the ease with which she was meeting her new life, telling these little falsehoods so convincingly that they in their innocence believed her. Mr. Manning was slowly getting better but his age was against him, his bones did not knit as rapidly as they had hoped. Always they asked Mimi when she would return to them. But she could tell with no great difficulty from their voices and the worried look on their faces that things were not moving so well with them and that their invitations, while sincere, would, if accepted, have multiplied burdens already heavy. She assured them she was having no difficulties whatever, and even brought little gifts of flowers or fruit to them which meant the sacrifice of food often for herself.

She made no calls nor had she any other intimates than these. Her trials and difficulties had given a depth to Mimi’s expression which, instead of making her less attractive, had added a richness to what had been a childish, flowerlike beauty. Almost automatically she drew to her the gaze of men whom she passed on the street and she knew what these looks meant. She hurried along with downcast eyes to escape them, frightened by their boldness. She felt safe, for she seldom ventured forth after nightfall, but even in the daytime these unmistakable glances caused a nausea to well up within her. In the episode with Carl, Mimi had given herself freely and with no sense of shame or guilt but she had loved him when that had taken place. But these unsolicited attentions caused a revulsion within her which swept through her like a physical illness.

One of her best-paying customers for whom she sewed regularly every Thursday lived in Germantown. Often the work was so great she did not leave until very late but she was glad of this, for she was paid generously for her overtime and received her dinner, which meant an additional saving. One Thursday afternoon as she came down the stairs from the room where she always worked she met the husband of her employer.

“Hello,” he greeted her, “and who are you?”

===Chinese===

社交生活和娱乐，于她一概没有。她连电影也花不起钱。她偶尔去看看曼宁夫妇，给他们讲自己应付新生活如何从容不迫的亮堂话，这些小小的谎话说得那样动听，老两口也就天真地信了。曼宁先生慢慢见好，可年岁拖累着他，骨头长得没有他们指望的那样快。他们总是问米米什么时候回去。可她不用费多大劲就从他们的嗓音里、从他们脸上的愁容里听出，他们的日子过得并不顺，那邀请虽然出于真心，一旦接受，只会给他们本已沉重的担子再加码。她向他们保证自己毫无难处，甚至给他们带去小小的礼物，鲜花或水果——那常常意味着她自己要省下一顿饭。

她不拜客，除了这一家再没有亲近的人。磨难与困苦给米米的神情添了深度，那非但没有减损她的动人，反而给她那原本孩子气的、花一般的美丽添了丰韵。街上走过的男人几乎不由自主地把目光投到她身上，她懂得这些目光的意思。她垂着眼匆匆走过，躲开它们，被那份大胆吓着了。她觉得安全，因为天黑后她很少出门，可就是大白天里，这些明明白白的凝视也叫一阵恶心从她胃里往上涌。同卡尔那一节，米米是自由地交付自己的，没有羞耻或罪疚之感，因为那时她爱他。可这些不请自来的殷勤在她心里引起的憎恶，像一场肉体的疾病那样卷过她。

一位付工钱最阔绰的主顾，她每星期四定期去做活，住在日耳曼敦。活常常多得她很晚才走，她倒乐意，因为加班有优厚的加班费，还管一顿晚饭，等于又省下一笔。一个星期四的下午，她从她一贯做工的那间屋子下楼，迎面碰上了雇主的丈夫。

“你好，”他招呼道，“你是哪位？”

===Original===

She told him. After answering various questions regarding the length of time she had worked there and listening to his surprised comment that he had not seen her during all that time, he let her pass. But the following Thursday he was there again, this time earlier than before. He came into the room where she was hurrying through her work.

“Tell me about yourself,” he demanded, pleasantly, too pleasantly Mimi thought.

“There’s little to tell. I work for my living.”

“Your husband’s dead, then?” he remarked, looking at her black dress. Mimi yet wore mourning, partly because she could afford no new clothes. She began to gather her work to avoid his questions.

“You don’t mean to tell me you’re coloured?” he pursued, incredulously. “My wife told me about having a coloured girl sewing for her but I never expected to see anybody looking like *you*!”

“I am coloured.” Mimi assured him firmly as she tried to leave the room.

“Wait a minute﻿—wait a minute,” he hastened to add. “I don’t mean any harm. I was born in the South and I always liked Negroes.”

“That’s very kind of you﻿—but I’ve got to hurry home,” Mimi nervously answered as she sought to leave the room. He stood in front of her, his hands clasped behind his back, and the smile on his face made her think instinctively of a fat, sleepy-eyed and sleek cat teasing a mouse before devouring it.

“Those pretty little hands of yours are too delicate for hard work﻿—and I never saw such lovely hair﻿—do you know, I always was peculiarly susceptible to yellow hair﻿—”

He drawled the words in what doubtless seemed to him to be an effective manner. He almost purred them. Mimi did not know whether to laugh or cry. She had seen scenes like this in the movies, and the cheap melodrama of such episodes had invariably made her want to snicker at their absurdity. But here was the thing she had laughed at in mild amusement happening to her, and it wasn’t all play, she knew, nor was she unaware of the very real danger behind his smooth and would-be seductive manner. At first she had been frightened, now she had to put forth a very real effort to keep from smiling. He felt the change in her manner and mistakenly thought it to be progress on his part.

===Chinese===

她告诉了他。回答了几句她在那儿做了多久的话，又听他惊讶地说这么久了怎么从没见过她，他便放她过去了。可下一个星期四他又在，这回来得比上次还早。他走进她正赶着活计的那间屋子。

“跟我讲讲你自己的事，”他要求道，那股客气劲儿——米米觉得，客气得过了头。

“没什么可讲的。我靠做工吃饭。”

“这么说你丈夫死了？”他说，眼睛望着她的黑衣裳。米米还穿着丧服，一半因为添不起新衣。她开始收拾活计，想躲开他的问题。

“你不会是说，你是有色人？”他追问，一脸的不相信。“我太太倒是提过请了个有色姑娘做针线，可我从没想过会见到长得像你这样的！”

“我是有色人。”米米一面坚决向他声明，一面想离开屋子。

“等一等——等一等，”他赶忙补上。“我没有恶意。我生在南方，我一向喜欢黑人。”

“您真好——可我得赶紧回家，”米米紧张地答着，想法子出屋。他挡在她面前，两手扣在背后，脸上的笑使她本能地想起一只肥胖的、睡眼惺忪、皮毛油亮的猫，在吞下老鼠之前先戏弄它一番。

“你那双漂亮的小手太嫩了，干不得粗活——我从没见过这么可爱的头发——你知道吗，我一向对黄头发的女人格外没有抵抗力——”

他拿腔拿调地拖着长腔，那方式在他自己看来无疑很有效。他几乎是把这些字句念得呼噜作响。米米不知道该笑还是该哭。这样的场面她在电影里见过，这类廉价情节剧总叫她想嗤笑它们的荒唐。可如今这她曾当作笑料看的东西落到她自己头上，而且她知道，这不全是戏，他那一团和气、自以为勾魂的姿态背后，有着非常真实的危险。起初她害怕了，这会儿她得费真正的力气才憋得住笑。他觉出她神态的变化，错以为是自己有了进展。

===Original===

“You don’t have to work so hard if you don’t want to,” he suggested, his words becoming bolder not so much in the actual phrases he used as in their increased suggestiveness. “I have no objection to coloured girls﻿—in fact, I really prefer them﻿—”

“That’s really very nice of you﻿—very generous indeed,” Mimi observed, her amusement now rapidly conquering her apprehension.

“Oh, no, not at all﻿—not at all!” he protested, thinking her serious. “Even when I lived in the South I had none of the usual prejudice.”

“How very remarkable!” cooed Mimi. Her voice had in it the velvety smoothness of swan’s-down.

To himself he thought, this is easier than I expected, as he advanced possessively towards her.

“How soon am I going to see you?” he queried softly.

“Don’t be silly!” Mimi calmly advised him. “In the first place you are fat. In the second, you are old and bald. In the third, you are white. Fourth, you are vain and stupid and ignorant and repulsive. Don’t think I’m falling back on the sentimental melodramatics of the ‘poor working girl.’ I’m not﻿—I let you run along just to set the stage for telling you my opinion of you.”

Her voice was as dispassionate as that of a schoolgirl monotonously listing the products from Brazil. There were no heroics, no tears, but only a relentless cataloguing of the physical and mental defects of the would-be Casanova. He gasped.

“You little fool﻿—you ought to feel proud that I, a white man, would even want you﻿—a nigger!”

Her face flamed at the despised word but she kept her temper.

“I’m not surprised at your thinking that. I suppose even that such a notion is natural﻿—you’ve made your own ideas about your own attractiveness and irresistibility and you’ve told yourself so often you’re invincible you believe it yourselves. Seducers of servant-girls! A noble accomplishment!”

Infuriated at her impassivity and her ridicule, so markedly a contrast to what he had assumed was complacence, he sought to seize her. She eluded him with the same coolness and remarked as she left the room: “It doesn’t really seem wise, does it, to create a scene in your own home where your wife is within hearing distance?”

A faint, mocking laugh trailed back to him as he stood there after she had gone.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

“你要是不想这么辛苦，也可以不这么辛苦，”他提示道，话愈发大胆了——倒不在字面上，而在那越来越露骨的暗示里。“我不反对有色姑娘——说实在的，我倒真偏爱她们——”

“您可真好——真是慷慨极了，”米米说道，她的好笑这会儿正迅速压倒她的惊惶。

“哦，不，哪里哪里——哪里！”他还当她认真，抗议道。“就是住在南方那阵子，我也从没有一般人那种偏见。”

“多么难能可贵呀！”米米软声软气地说。她的嗓音里有天赐绒般的柔滑。

他心里想：这比我想的还容易呢，一边朝她占有过似的逼过来。

“我什么时候能再见到你？”他柔声问。

“别傻了！”米米平静地劝他。“第一，你胖。第二，你又老又秃。第三，你是白人。第四，你虚荣、愚蠢、无知、惹人厌。别以为我要搬出‘可怜的做工姑娘’那套催泪戏码。我不是——我方才让你一路说下去，只为搭好台子，好对你下个评语。”

她的嗓音平平淡淡，像个女学生没精打采地背巴西的物产。没有豪言，没有眼泪，只有一份对这位自封的唐璜身心缺陷的无情清单。他倒抽一口气。

“你这小傻瓜——我一个白人，肯要你——一个黑鬼——你就该觉得光荣！”

那个下贱的字眼使她的脸腾地烧起来，可她按住了火气。

“你这么想我不奇怪。恐怕连这个念头都是天生的——你们给自己编了一套自己的魅力、自己的不可抗拒，又跟自己念叨了那么多遍‘我是常胜将军’，念得自己都信了。专诱哄女仆的老爷！好一桩光荣的成就！”

她的不动声色、她的讥诮，同他方才自作多情以为的默许恰成对照，他恼羞成怒，伸手来抓她。她以同样的冷静闪开了，一面走出屋子一面说道：“在自己家里、太太就在一墙之隔的地方大闹一场，好像不大聪明吧？”

他站在那里，她走后，一缕微弱的、嘲弄的笑声飘回他耳边。……

## XIV / 十四
===Original===

Mimi was not greatly surprised when she received a message that she would no longer be expected on Thursdays at the Germantown home. At first it amused her. Had she resorted to tears or pleading for release or any of the usual methods of damsels in defence of their virtue, she reflected, the curt note of dismissal would not have been sent. Man is a peculiar creature. So long as by implication or any other means he is allowed to imagine himself the superior being, whether that superiority came through brute strength or intellect or wealth or any other means, he is manageable and easily gulled. But when he is made ridiculous and the little bubble of his conceit is pricked by a woman’s obvious contempt, he becomes a vengeful and ridiculous person.

Mimi was sustained by her elation at the ease with which she had escaped an unpleasant and possibly dangerous situation. She was glad she had retained her poise. She felt angry only when she remembered the nasty way in which he had implied and later definitely stated that in his opinion she should have been happy, being coloured, to have attracted his notice. For the first time she saw the reasons for Jean’s apprehension in those talks which seemed to have taken place so many years ago. Life for any woman who was unprotected and who sought to live up to certain ideals was hard. But when that woman was coloured she was more than ever at the mercy of those who were her constant pursuers. She found her old race-consciousness surging up again. Bitterness against the husband of her former employer welled in her not so much because he had assumed she would be amenable to his suggestions but more because he had so readily assumed that she, being coloured, would offer no objections whatever.

But when her joy in her victory and her bitterness at the vanquished had passed, she found that the revenue lost in this manner was seriously affecting her. She found other jobs but none of them were as regular nor did they pay as well. Every dollar counted, every fraction of a dollar earned or expended made a difference. Once or twice she had been a few days late in paying the rent for the small room in which she and Petit Jean lived. The lessened cordiality with which her landlady greeted her pleas for more time for payment made this an ordeal she avoided even when it meant, as it frequently did, the forgoing of meals for herself.

===Chinese===

米米接到口信，说日耳曼敦那家人家星期四不再用她了，她并不很吃惊。起初她只觉得好笑。她想，倘若她哭哭啼啼、苦苦哀求脱身，或者用上姑娘们护守贞洁的任何惯技，那封简短的辞退字条就不会送来了。男人是一种奇怪的造物。只要旁敲侧击也好、别的什么手段也好，容他想象自己是高一等的生灵——那优越无论是凭蛮力、凭头脑、凭财富还是别的什么来的——他就好驾驭，容易哄骗。可一旦他被人弄得可笑，他那自负的小气泡被一个女人明白的轻蔑一针刺破，他就变成一个睚眦必报又可笑的人。

米米靠一股得意撑着——她那样轻易地脱身了一场不愉快、也许还危险的处境。她庆幸自己保住了镇定。只有想起他那下作的做派她才动怒——他先是暗示、后来干脆明说：在她看来，她一个有色人，能引来他的注意，就该欢天喜地。她第一次看懂了让的忧虑——在那些仿佛已是许多年前进行的谈话里。一个没有保障、又力求按某些理想做人的女人，日子是艰难的。而当这女人又是有色人，她就愈发任凭那些一味纠缠的人摆布了。她发觉自己旧的种族意识又涌了上来。对先前那位雇主的丈夫，她心里泛起的苦味，倒不很因为他料想她会顺从他的提议，更因为他那样轻易地断定，她一个有色人，是无论如何不会拒绝的。

可等到胜利的欢喜和对败将的苦味都过去之后，她发现这样失去的那笔进项正实实在在地伤着她。她另找了几份活，可没有一份那样固定，工钱也没有一份那样好。每一块钱都要算，挣来或花掉的每一角每一分都顶用。有一两回，她付她和小让住的那小间的房租，晚了好几天。房东太太对她缓期付款的请求越来越冷淡，这使她宁可绕开这场磨难，哪怕照例要饿掉自己几顿饭。

===Original===

Petit Jean thrived and grew, singularly free from the maladies of childhood. With what to Mimi seemed amazing speed, his monthly birthdays sped by and accumulated﻿—the fifth, the sixth, the seventh, the eighth. As long as he was well and clothed and fed, Mimi’s own difficulties seemed of little more than passing moment. Nothing greatly matters, she comforted herself, when seen in perspective﻿—it is only when a thing is happening that it frightens and pains one. She found a new strength coming to her out of the problems and perplexities she was meeting. It was, she found, like looking into a mirror. When one did look the image was there and if one was sick or unhappy it gave back a reflection of that condition or mood. But as soon as one removed himself from a place in front of the looking-glass the image was gone. She consoled herself with this convenient philosophy and found in it a courage of which she would not have believed herself capable.

But inevitably there came to her periods of depression. Most frequently these occurred when she thought of Jean’s future. Suppose her own health failed. She could not go on indefinitely this way, common sense told her, going without food, improperly clothed, saving nothing. Her rather ample wardrobe which she had brought from Atlanta was nearly threadbare and she just could not afford to spend money on adornment for herself. Little garments for Jean, talcums, soaps, and the infinite variety of essentials which an infant required for his comfort took all she could earn and more.

===Chinese===

小让长得又壮又大，奇怪地不沾儿童的那些病症。在米米看来快得惊人，他一个月一个月的生日飞过去，攒起来——第五个，第六个，第七个，第八个。只要他好好的、有衣穿、有饭吃，米米自己的难处就不过是过眼的事。从长远看，没有什么了不起，她这样宽慰自己——事情只有正在发生的时候才吓人、才疼。她发觉一股新的力量正从她遇到的种种难题和困顿里朝她涌来。这就像照镜子，她发现。人朝镜里看，像就在那儿，人若是病了、不快活，镜里就还你那份病容、那份愁容。可人一从镜前挪开，像就没了。她用这套方便的哲学安慰自己，并从里面得着一种勇气，一种她原不信自己能有的勇气。

可沮丧的时期不可避免地还是来了。多半是在她想到小让的前途的时候。万一她自己的身体垮了呢。她不能无限期这样下去，常识告诉她，不吃饱、穿不暖、一个子儿不攒。她从亚特兰大带来的那相当充实的衣橱已经穿得快要露线，她实在花不起钱给自己添妆。小让的衣裳、爽身粉、肥皂，还有婴儿舒服所需的无穷无尽的各色必需品，吃光了她挣得的一切，还不够。

===Original===

It seldom occurred to her she could have asked and received aid from the Hunters, Mrs. Daquin or her Aunt Sophie. The idea at times of greatest need came fleetingly to her, hazily, but she resolutely put it from her mind before it could find lodgment there. Whatever else might come to her, she would never yield to such temptations, at least she would never permit herself to beg from her stepmother or Carl’s parents. To do so would have been surrender of the principle for which she had so bravely fought﻿—the determination to keep her own soul free. However adroitly worded, a plea to them would have been admission of defeat, of her failure to make good the lofty words of determination she had spoken. She knew they did not understand the motives which had driven her on. In truth, she sometimes wondered if she herself knew them. But when she held Jean in her arms, when his toothless smile greeted her on her calling for him at the nursery, when his warm flesh rested against her side in bed, she was happy to a degree which she knew would never have been possible had she accepted the easier way out of her difficulties. No, despite all, she knew, her course had been the only one she could have taken in the face of Carl’s abject failure to measure up to the high qualities with which she had in fancy endowed him.

One extravagance, a pitifully small one, she permitted herself. In the late spring she passed an old secondhand book shop on a side street. She lingered at the weather-beaten old table outside the shop, fingering, examining, peeping into the battered and worn books displayed there. Volume after volume she looked into but most of them were dull and prosy stuff which bored her. She lingered so long the proprietor came to the door and stood there looking everywhere except at her, with too great casualness, Mimi reflected with a smile. It amused her to watch him with equal lack of obviousness, knowing that his suspiciousness led him to remain there. She named him “Old Scrooge,” for he looked as though he had stepped from Dickens’ pages with his fringe of dusty grey hair, his square, steel-rimmed spectacles, his baggy and nondescript clothing.

===Chinese===

她很少想到过，她本可以开口，从亨特夫妇、达坎太太或苏菲姨妈那里得到接济。在最窘迫的时辰，这念头也偶尔飞快地、影影绰绰地掠过，可她总在它落地生根之前坚决把它逐出脑海。无论再遭遇什么，她绝不向这样的诱惑低头，至少她绝不容许自己去向继母或卡尔的父母乞求。那样做就等于放弃她如此勇敢地为之奋斗过的原则——保住自己灵魂自由的决心。信写得再乖巧，向他们开口也是认输，是承认她没能兑现她说过的那些豪言壮语。她知道他们不理解驱使她这样做的动机。说实在的，她有时怀疑她自己懂不懂。可是，当她把小让抱在怀里，当她在育婴所唤他、他咧开没牙的嘴朝她笑，当他在床上温热的小身子贴着她的腰，她就幸福到一种程度——她知道，倘若当初拣了那条省力的路，这幸福是万万达不到的。不，纵有一切，她知道，面对卡尔那样卑怯地够不上她凭想象加给他的那些高贵品质，她的路是她唯一能走的路。

一样奢侈，小得可怜的一样，她容许了自己。暮春时节，她路过一条横街上一家旧书店。她在店外那张风吹日晒的老桌子边流连，摸摸、翻翻、窥探那些摆着的破旧的书。一本一本看过去，多半是叫她生厌的乏味啰嗦的东西。她流连得太久，店主走到门口站住，眼睛望着四面八方，独独不望她——那份漫不经心太过了，米米含笑想道。她同样不露痕迹地望着他，觉得有趣，知道是多疑拽着他不走。她给他起名叫“老斯克鲁奇”，因为他那圈灰扑扑的头发、方框钢边眼镜、鼓鼓囊囊不成样子的衣裳，活像从狄更斯的书页里走出来的人。

===Original===

The game interested her and she lingered, idly fingering book after book. Her delay was repaid when a dingy, much-handled volume seemed to spring from the table, bringing with it a strange thrill. Jean had had a copy of this same book. So had Carl. Both of them had with elaborate indirection kept it from her and, with natural curiosity, she had always been eager to know why. And, with equal naturalness, she had made a solemn vow to read it from cover to cover when she could put her hands upon a copy. It was *Leaves of Grass*. She opened it eagerly. Her eyes fell on the yellowed page. They read:

O Me! O Life!

O Me! O Life! of the questions of these recurring,

Of the endless trains of the faithless, of cities fill’d with the foolish,

Of myself for ever reproaching myself (for who more foolish than I, and who more faithless?),

Of eyes that vainly crave the light, of the objects mean, of the struggle ever renew’d,

Of the poor results of all, of the plodding and sordid crowds I see around me,

Of the empty and useless years of the rest, with the rest me intertwined,

The question, O me! so sad, recurring﻿—What good amid these, O me, O life?

Answer

That you are her﻿—that life exists and identify,

That the powerful play goes on, and you may contribute a verse.

The old man, the shop, the passersby, everything was blotted out by the words, powerful, true, so applicable to her own case. “That the powerful play goes on, and *I* may contribute a verse,” she repeated softly, consciously changing the pronoun. She paid the man for the book, cheerfully giving up the food which the book would cost, and hurried home with it.

When she had put Jean to bed she sat and read over and over again the words. “Foolish and faithless.” “For who more foolish than I, and who more faithless?” She had been foolish, to the dead Jean she had been faithless. To herself, but a wild happiness filled her that in the hour of difficulty, at the time when she had been forced to make the biggest decision of her life, she may have been foolish but not faithless. She fell asleep whispering: “That the powerful play goes on, and *I, Mimi Daquin*, may contribute a verse.”

===Chinese===

这场游戏使她起劲，她流连着，一本一本信手翻去。她的耽搁得到了报偿——一本灰扑扑、被摸旧了的书仿佛从桌上跳了起来，带来一阵奇异的战栗。让有过同一本书。卡尔也有。两人都煞费苦心地绕着弯子不让她看见，怀着自然的好奇，她一直巴望知道为什么。怀着同样自然的心思，她郑重起过誓，一旦弄到手，要从头到尾读一遍。那是《草叶集》。她急急翻开。眼睛落在发黄的书页上。读到：

哦，我！哦，生命！

哦，我！哦，生命！这些问题周而复始，

无信者长长的行列，愚人塞满的城池，

我永远在责备自己，（因为谁比我更愚，谁比我更无信？）

徒然渴望光明的眼睛，卑琐的物事，永在更新的挣扎，

这一切可怜的结局，我四周跋涉而污浊的人群，

其余空虚无用的岁月，同它们缠在一起的我自己，

那问题，哦我！这样悲伤，周而复始——这一切之中有什么善，哦我，哦生命？

答

你在此——生命存在，本体存在，

这恢弘的戏继续演下去，而你可以贡献一行诗句。

老人、店铺、过路人，一切都被这些字句抹去了——有力量，真实，如此切合她自己的处境。“这恢弘的戏继续演下去，而我，可以贡献一行诗句，”她轻轻地重复，有意换了代词。她付了书钱，快快活活地舍弃了这本书将顶掉的一顿饭，抱着它匆匆回家。

安顿小让睡下之后，她坐着把那些字句一遍遍重读。“愚昧而无信。”“因为谁比我更愚，谁比我更无信？”她曾是愚的，对死去的让，她曾是无信的。对她自己——可是此刻充溢她的是一阵狂喜：在困难的时辰，在她被迫作出一生最重大决定的当口，她也许愚，却不曾无信。她一面睡去一面低语：“这恢弘的戏继续演下去，而我，米米·达坎（Mimi Daquin），可以贡献一行诗句。”

===Original===

The words gave her a comforting sense of direction. She had been blindly wandering, groping, striving towards a goal that had never been clear, indeed she had never even vaguely visualized any destination other than the struggle to do those things which seemed to her to be right. Even for this indefinite rightness or wrongness she had no tangible definition﻿—she sought only by instinct or conscience or some other indefinable guide the path to truth and beauty and happiness.﻿ ﻿…

This new consciousness gave her faith and courage at a time when she needed these things more than she had ever needed them before. Summer came with the usual migration of the few regular customers she had to the seashore or the mountains. Mimi no longer called on the Mannings, for she could no longer conceal even from their unsuspecting eyes the extent of her distress. She saved the small fee at the day nursery, for it was seldom now that she had to be away from home. Her rent was several weeks overdue and the relations between Mimi and her landlady had reached an actually strained point.

“Stuck-up niggers think they can sponge off of common ones﻿—humph! I’ll show her she can’t make no footmat out of me!” she overheard her landlady saying to her husband one evening. “An’ I ain’t never been satisfied she’s respectable﻿—if that baby’s got a daddy he must have had some relatives she could get money from. I’m tired of foolin’ with her and if she don’t pay me by Sad’dy I’m goin’ to put her and her baby right out there in the street!”

Sick at heart, hungry, discouraged, Mimi slowly struggled up the stairs to her room. Even Petit Jean’s coos of welcome could not cheer her up. She lay on the bed beside him and wept.

And as though she had not already enough to bear, she found new trouble awaiting her that dwarfed all that had gone before, when she returned to the house the following evening after another day of fruitless search for work. Mrs. Williams, her landlady, had volunteered to care for Jean while she was out, despite her words of the night before. When the rickety screen door banged behind her, Mimi heard Mrs. Williams calling from the rear of the house. On an old davenport lay Jean, vomiting wretchedly. Intermittently he screamed, writhing in the paroxysmal pain which recurred every few minutes. On his face was a ghastly pallor and he seemed in a state of complete collapse.

===Chinese===

这些字句给了她一种安适的方向感。她一直盲目地流浪、摸索，朝着一个从没有清楚过的目标奋斗，说实在的，除了挣扎着去做那些在她看来是对的事情，她甚至从没有隐隐约约地设想过任何终点。连这模糊的对错，她也没有摸得着的定义——她只是凭本能、凭良心、凭某样说不清的向导，寻求通往真、通往美、通往幸福的路。……

这新的自觉给了她信念和勇气，正在她比以往任何时候都更需要这些东西的时候。夏天来了，她那寥寥几家固定主顾照例迁去了海滨或山间。米米不再去看曼宁夫妇了，因为连他们那双不疑的眼睛，她也瞒不住自己的窘迫到什么地步了。育婴所那点小费用她省下了，因为如今她很少必须整天出门。房租欠了几个星期，米米和房东太太的关系已经真正紧张起来。

“神气的黑鬼，当她们能从普通人身上揩油——哼！我要叫她瞧瞧，别想拿我当擦脚垫！”一天晚上她听见房东太太对她丈夫说。“再说她正经不正经我一直犯疑——那娃娃要是有爹，总该有门亲戚可以弄钱。我伺候她伺候烦了，星期六再不把钱交来，我就把她和她那娃娃一块儿摆到大街上！”

米米心里发沉，肚子空着，万念俱灰，慢慢挣扎着上楼回自己房间。连小让欢迎的咕咕声也提不起她的精神。她在他身边躺下，哭了。

仿佛她受的还不够，第二天晚上，又在外面徒劳地找了一天活计回来，她发现新的祸事在等着她，把先前的一切都压了下去。她的房东威廉斯太太虽在前一晚说了那些话，还是自愿在她外出时照看小让。破纱门在她身后砰的一响，米米听见威廉斯太太从屋子后头喊她。一张旧长沙发上躺着小让，凄凄惨惨地呕吐着。他一阵阵尖叫，在每隔几分钟便复发一次的痉挛性疼痛里扭动。他脸上一片死灰，整个人像完全虚脱了。

===Original===

Mimi rushed to the cot and fell on her knees beside Jean as she flung anxious questions at the perspiring, frightened woman above her.

“Yes’m, I called a doctor﻿—my doctor﻿—and he said it wasn’t nothing but cramps. He gave your baby some castor oil, but soon as the doctor left he began throwin’ up and yellin’ mo’ than ever.”﻿ ﻿…

Mimi, frightened as she was, remained calm.

“While I take him up to my room, will you get Dr. Newton?” she asked. She knew him by sight, having seen him at church often when she had gone there with the Mannings. His name came instantly to her, for she had been told many times of his eminence among the coloured physicians of Philadelphia. He came at once, trim, alert, intelligent. His brown face was a mask as he asked questions and examined Jean. Long brown fingers probed the baby’s abdomen, methodically, confidently he measured pulse and temperature. Coolly he recited the symptoms as he found them:

“… vomiting, at first contents of stomach, then bilious﻿ ﻿… collapse﻿ ﻿… pallor﻿ ﻿… feeble pulse﻿ ﻿… temperature normal﻿ ﻿… abdomen relaxed﻿ ﻿… increasing prostration﻿ ﻿… rising temperature﻿ ﻿… tumour, sausage-shaped, curving﻿ ﻿…” He turned to Mimi. “What’s been done for him?” Mimi told him of the doctor called by Mrs. Williams, of the administration of castor oil.

“He said somepin about ’fectious diarrhea,” Mrs. Williams added, her fright now gone with the coming of Dr. Newton.

“Gave him castor oil?” Dr. Newton asked, incredulously. “The blithering fool!”

Mimi and Mrs. Williams showed their dismay at his words.

“Thank goodness he’s probably vomited it all up by now.” His words were punctuated by Jean’s pathetic screams.

“Mrs. Daquin, your baby’s got a bad case of intussusception﻿—folding back of a part of the intestine over another part,” he added as she showed her lack of comprehension of the longer word. “Or, to make it simpler, it’s acute intestinal obstruction. The only chance of saving him is through an operation﻿—and it’ll have to be done without an hour’s delay.”

Mimi sank her head into her hands. Had she just heard the death sentence pronounced on her, she would have felt not half as terrified as the thought of her Petit Jean going under the knife. She felt sick, disheartened, beaten. She raised a haggard face, her hair dishevelled, to the doctor.

“Isn’t there anything else than operating?” she pleaded.

===Chinese===

米米扑到小床边，跪在小让身旁，一边朝那个满头大汗、惊惶失措的女人连声急问。

“是的太太，我请了医生——我的医生——他说不过是肚子抽筋。他给你娃娃吃了点蓠麻油，可医生前脚一走，他就吐得、叫得比先前更凶了。”……

米米虽然怕，却还镇定。

“我把他抱回我房里去，您能把牛顿医生请来吗？”她问。她同他面熟，从前同曼宁夫妇上教堂时常常见到他。他的名字立刻来到她嘴边，因为人家多次告诉她，他在费城有色人医生里声名赫赫。他立刻就来了，利落，敏捷，聪明。他一面问一面检查小让，那张棕色的脸像一副面具。修长的棕色手指探按婴儿的腹部，又有条理又有把握地量了脉搏和体温。他冷冷地宣读他查到的症状：

“……呕吐，先吐胃内容物，后带胆汁……虚脱……苍白……脉搏微弱……体温正常……腹部松弛……衰竭加深……体温上升……肿块，腊肠形，弯曲……”他转向米米。“给他做过什么处置？”米米说了威廉斯太太请的那个医生，说了灌蓠麻油。

“他说了句什么传染性腹泻，”威廉斯太太补充道，牛顿医生一来，她的惊吓全没了。

“给他灌了蓠麻油？”牛顿医生问，一脸不敢相信。“这个蠢货！”

米米和威廉斯太太听他这样说，都露出惊惶。

“谢天谢地，这会儿多半已经全吐出来了。”他的话被小让一阵阵可怜的尖叫敲着点。

“达坎小姐，你的孩子得的是重症肠套叠——一段肠子折回来套进了另一段，”见她对那个长词露出不解，他又补了一句。“或者简单说，是急性肠梗阻。救他唯一的指望是手术——而且一个钟头也不能耽搁。”

米米把头埋进双手。哪怕方才听的是对她本人宣判死刑，她的恐惧也不及想到小让要挨刀的一半。她觉得要吐，心灰，认了败。她抬起一张憔悴的脸，头发散乱，望着医生。

“除了开刀，没有别的法子了吗？”她哀求道。

===Original===

“Nothing,” he told her. “And even then the chances are somewhat less than one in ten. This sounds hard and brutally frank﻿—but it’s best you know the truth,” he added kindly, deep pity in his voice for her distress.

“Then operate,” she declared, firmly, all panic gone from her face and voice.﻿ ﻿…

---

They drove rapidly to the coloured hospital where Dr. Newton was to operate on Jean. Mimi held his tiny form close to her, stifling its screams in the softness of her breast. On her face was fear, despair, agony without hope. She was haunted by the spectre of death, she saw before her Petit Jean lifeless, never again to coo and smile and welcome her with soft little cries. Not until now did she realize how much he meant to her. She felt guilty of a great crime, of putting food into her own body when it might have averted this horrible thing. Even Dr. Newton’s assurance in answer to her timid questioning that physicians did not know the cause of the malady other than that it was due to the thinness of the intestinal walls of an infant, that most frequently it occurred in apparently perfectly healthy and well-cared-for children, her feeling of guilt did not leave her. Jean’s eyes were closed. He seemed to be in a deep stupor from which he roused only when another spasm of pain came over him. Mimi kissed him feverishly, madly, as the nurse took him gently from her.﻿ ﻿…

---

Up and down the hall she paced oblivious of everything save the tragic scene being enacted on the other side of the closed door. The odour of the anaesthetic stifled her, choked her, made her want to cry out in suffocation. The feeble little cries and groans as Jean slowly passed into unconsciousness made her frantic. She wanted to burst open the door, snatch him from the cruel knives, take him and the two of them plunge to simultaneous death in the waters of the river nearby. The immaculate nurses who passed rapidly infuriated her, they seemed so calm, so callous. She wanted to seize them and shake them until their teeth rattled, screaming: “Don’t you realize that my Jean﻿—*mon pauvre* Petit Jean﻿—is being cut to pieces in there!”﻿ ﻿… She wanted to do all these things but she did none of them. She paced frantically back and forth, back and forth, for hours, days, centuries, past the closed door﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

“没有，”他告诉她。“就算开了刀，把握也不到十分之一。这话听着硬，说得残忍——可你最好知道实情，”他又和善地加了一句，声音里满是对她苦难的深切怜惜。

“那就开刀，”她决然说道，脸上和嗓音里的惶恐一扫而空。……

---

他们飞快驱车前往牛顿医生要给小让动手术的那家黑人医院。米米把他小小的身子紧紧搂在怀里，用胸口的柔软捂住他的尖叫。她脸上是恐惧、绝望、没有希望的痛苦。死亡的鬼影缠着她，她看见小让没有生气的模样横在眼前，再也不会咕咕叫、不会笑、不会用细细的嗓音迎她。直到此刻她才明白他对她意味着多少。她觉得自己犯了一桩大罪——往自己嘴里填了吃食，而那些东西本可以拦住这场灾祸。尽管牛顿医生回答她怯生生的问话时说，这病的起因医生们也不知道，只知道是出于婴儿肠壁太薄，多半发生在看上去十分健康、照料得极好的孩子身上，她的罪疚还是不散。小让闭着眼睛，像陷在深度的昏迷里，只有新的一阵疼痛袭来才把他激醒。护士轻轻把他从她怀里接过去时，她狂乱地、发疯似的吻他。……

---

她在走廊里走来走去，除了那扇关着的门后正在上演的悲剧场面，什么都忘了。麻药的气味呛着她，憋着她，叫她想喊出声来。小让慢慢失去知觉时发出的微弱的啼哭和呻吟使她发狂。她想撞开那扇门，把他从那些残忍的刀子底下抢出来，抱着他，娘儿俩一同跳进近旁的河水里同死。那些一尘不染、脚步匆匆的护士叫她火冒三丈，她们显得那样平静，那样无动于衷。她想揪住她们摇晃，摇得她们牙齿打颤，尖喊：“你们不明白吗，我的让——我可怜的小让——正在里面叫人一刀刀剐碎！”……她想做这一切，可一样也没有做。她只是发疯似的来回踱，来回踱，几个钟头，几天，几个世纪，在那扇关着的门前踱过……

---

===Original===

“Operation’s a success﻿—he’s resting easily﻿—won’t know until a day or two.﻿ ﻿…” The words came to her as from a great distance. “You’d better go home﻿—there’s no good you can do here﻿—it’ll be half an hour or so before he comes out from under the anaesthetic﻿ ﻿… and then he’ll probably sleep.﻿ ﻿…”

“Mother of God! Save him! Save him!”

It had been a long time since a prayer had passed Mimi’s lips. But now she prayed﻿—prayed with the fervour of Luther, of Savonarola, of St. John, of all the martyrs, yea, with a fervour greater than all of these, the plea of a mother for her child.﻿ ﻿…

All night she alternately tossed in her narrow bed or sat by the window looking into the darkness outside. Above, a pale moon was sheathed in dusky blue swirls of clouds, so delicate and wispy they looked like graceful twistings of chiffon, but this beauty she did not see. From near at hand the fast-dying noises of the city came to her, the hum imperceptibly fading away as the crowds thinned out, but she heard none of these. Any minute Jean might be dying, she thought with a shiver. And then she would hurry down the stairs to telephone the hospital. Always she received the same answer, he’s resting easily.

Though she tried to keep the thought from her mind, she knew she had come to the parting of the ways. I wonder I ever thought I could go through with it, she reflected. For my own sake I don’t care. But I’m not making enough money to keep us and I won’t be able to save anything at this rate for the future﻿—for Petit Jean’s future, she amended. Rent due Mrs. Williams, the surgeon’s fee, bills for the hospital. She cudgelled her brains for some way of making money. The Hunters? No! Mrs. Daquin? A thousand times no! The man in Germantown? A bitter smile came over her face. It wouldn’t be wise for him to make such an offer now.

===Chinese===

“手术成功了——他安安稳稳休息着——过一两天才见分晓。……”这些字句像从极远的地方向她传来。“你最好回家去——你在这儿帮不上什么忙——还要半个钟头上下他才从麻药里醒过来……之后他多半要睡。……”

“天主圣母！救救他！救救他！”

米米的唇间已有很久没有滑过一句祷告了。可如今她祈祷了——怀着路德、萨伏那洛拉、圣约翰、全体殉道者的热忱祈祷，是的，怀着比他们所有人都更大的热忱——一个母亲为她的孩子的祈求。……

一整夜，她时而在这张窄床上翻腾，时而坐在窗前望着外面的黑暗。头顶上，一轮苍白的月亮裹在暗蓝的云涡里，那云又轻又细，像雪纺绸优雅的扭绞，可这美丽她没有看见。近处，城市渐渐死寂的声浪向她传来，人潮散尽时那嗡嗡声不知不觉地消褪，可这些她一样没有听见。随时随刻小让都可能正在死去，她一阵冷战地想。于是她便奔下楼梯去医院那边打电话。回答永远是那一句：他安安稳稳休息着。

她虽想把那念头挡在心外，却知道自己已到了岔路口。奇怪我从前怎么会以为撑得下去，她想。为我自己，我无所谓。可我挣的钱养不活我们俩，照这样下去，将来——为小让的将来——一个子儿也攒不下，她更正道。欠威廉斯太太的房租，外科医生的诊金，医院的账。她绞尽脑汁想弄钱的法子。亨特家？不行！达坎太太？一万个别想！日耳曼敦那个男人？她脸上浮起一丝苦笑。这会儿由他来出这个价，可就不明智了。

===Original===

The thought brought her to herself with a shock. Defiantly she answered her own question﻿—I’d do even that if it would save Jean! She had reached the stage of desperation. The long months of anxious toil and uncertainty, of worry and undernourishment, had gathered their toll. Now that Jean’s life hung in the balance, she felt that there were no means to which she would not go. But all the possible sources of help she could think of would serve only to help her out of her present dilemma and would offer no lasting solution. Only one thing was certain﻿—it was not only impossible to go on as she had during the last few months but she was seriously jeopardizing Jean and his future. She did not know but that she might have a serious accident or become dangerously ill, perhaps die. What then would happen to him if he should live? She had sacrificed so much for him already, she would under no circumstances want him to go to the Hunters or to her stepmother, even if either of them would take him.

She wrestled with her problem but morning found her no nearer its solution. As soon as daylight came she dressed and went to the hospital. Jean had come out from under the anaesthetic. He greeted her with a faint smile, infinitely pathetic in its unconscious bravery. She sank to her knees gazing into his face as though he had died and had come to life again before her very eyes. And as she looked deep into his brown eyes and pallid face she offered a prayer of thanksgiving to the Virgin for life for Jean.﻿ ﻿…

Dr. Newton found her there.

“Well, he pulled through all right and, barring setbacks or complications, he’ll be well before long,” he cheerfully told her.

After they left the room Mimi told Dr. Newton frankly of her financial position. It galled her to be forced to confess her poverty but she could see no other way out of it and she was unwilling to do other than be truthful about it.

===Chinese===

这念头猛地把她惊回了自己。她挑衅地回答自己的问题——只要能救小让，连那个我也肯！她已经到了孤注一掷的地步。长年累月的焦灼操劳、没有着落，忧愁和营养不足，都收足了它们的账。如今小让的性命悬在秤上，她觉得再没有什么手段是她不肯用的了。可她能想得出来的一切求助的门路，只够帮她渡过眼下的难关，提不出一个长久的解决。只有一件事是定了的——不光照最近这几个月那样下去不可能，她还在实实在在地拿小让和他的前途冒险。天晓得她会不会出一场大祸、害一场大病，也许死掉。到那时他要是不幸活着，他会怎样？她已经为他牺牲了那么多，无论如何也不能让他落到亨特家或她继母手里，哪怕他们肯收。

她同她的难题摔跤，可天亮时也没见接近解决半步。天一亮她就穿好衣服去了医院。小让已从麻药里醒过来。他朝她淡淡一笑，那笑里有一种浑然不觉的勇敢，说不出的叫人心疼。她跪下去，凝望他的脸，仿佛他死过一回，又在她眼前活了过来。她深深望进他棕色的眼睛和苍白的脸，向圣母献上感恩的祷告，谢她把生命还给小让。……

牛顿医生看见她跪在那里。

“好了，他平安闯过来了，只要不反复、不生并发症，不久就好，”他高高兴兴地告诉她。

出了病房，米米向牛顿医生坦白讲了自己的经济状况。被迫承认自己的贫穷刺痛着她，可她看不出别的出路，也不愿在这件事上说半句假话。

===Original===

“Oh, that’s all right,” he assured her. “We doctors are used to waiting for our money. And the Mannings have told me about you, I’m not worried. Pay me when you can﻿—no, I’ll tell you what. Operations like the one I did on your baby are mighty rare. I’ve been practising medicine for fourteen years now and it’s the first one I’ve ever seen outside of a clinic. You pay the hospital bills and I’ll just dismiss the bill. The case is so remarkable a one that I’ll gladly let the fee go﻿—”

She protested vigorously against his proposal. She was grateful, so grateful the tears stood in her eyes. But, following so closely upon her confession of poverty, it smacked too much of charity, and she would not, she could not permit that.

“Well, have it your own way,” he finally agreed. “But don’t hurry.”

She was touched by his kindness. He hurried away. When he was gone a dozen phrases of thanks came to her mind, vexing her that she had been too stunned by his generosity to think of them before he had gone.﻿ ﻿…

As if Fate felt ashamed of the blows it had dealt her, there came to Mimi several profitable jobs during the four weeks Jean was in the hospital. One of these lasted more than a week, the making of the trousseau of a girl who belonged to one of the old Philadelphia coloured families. She never knew that she had been recommended by Dr. Newton, but the money she earned enabled her to pay not only the hospital bill but to settle a part of the debt she owed Mrs. Williams. Now that Jean was out of danger and well on the road to recovery, Mimi worked with a song in her heart and happiness in the belief that her darkest days were past. And when he was returned from the hospital she bought little things for his amusement, toys, games, dainty bits of the foods he was allowed to eat. She was envious of everything which separated her from him even for a short time, she begrudged the hours they spent in sleep, for they robbed her, she felt, of so many moments of conscious knowledge of and contact with him.

===Chinese===

“哦，那没关系，”他宽慰她。“我们当医生的，惯了等人还钱。曼宁夫妇又同我讲过你的事，我不担心。什么时候方便什么时候给——不，我告诉你这么办。像我在你宝宝身上做的这种手术，非常少见。我行医十四年，除临床上，这是我头一回遇见。你付医院的账，我那份就销了。这个病例太稀罕，诊金我情愿不要——”

她激烈反对他的提议。她感激，感激得眼泪涌上了眼眶。可是这话紧跟在她承认贫穷之后，施舍的气味太重，她不肯，也不能容许。

“好吧，随你，”他终于依了。“不过别急。”

他的好心使她感动。他匆匆走了。他走后，一打句感谢的话才涌到她心头，叫她懊恼，方才叫他的慷慨惊得发怔，竟一句也没想起来。……

仿佛命运也为自己给她的连番打击害了臊，小让住院的四个星期里，几宗进项可观的活计接连落到米米头上。其中一件做了一个多星期，是给费城一家有色人旧族的小姐做嫁妆。她始终不知道那是牛顿医生的举荐，可挣来的钱不但付清了医院账单，还清了欠威廉斯太太的一部分债。小让既已脱险、康复在望，米米做工时心里哼着歌，相信她最黑暗的日子已经过去。等他从医院回家，她买了些逗他玩的小东西，玩具，游戏，他吃得着的那些精细吃食。凡是把她同他隔开的东西，哪怕只隔一小会儿，她都要妒忌；他们睡去的那些钟点她也心疼，因为她觉得，那夺走了她多少有知觉地知道他、亲近他的时刻。

===Original===

The acuteness of her desire to be with Jean was rooted not alone in the to her more than miraculous escape he had had. It was, too, in the decision she had reached through that illness and the inevitability of execution of that decision pained her beyond measure. Mimi had faced during her dark hours when Jean’s life hung in the balance the practical situation before her.
