Ruthlessly she had cut away all the entangling meshes created by her love for Jean and had considered as dispassionately as she could the question as to what course would be for his greatest good. She found she could not approach the Hunters or her stepmother and she would rather have died herself than to have surrendered Jean to either of them. Those contingencies therefore were definitely out of the question.

So too was the possibility of her attempting to carry on the struggle she had now been making for more than a year. If only I could get some money saved and secure a steady income, perhaps start a small dressmaking establishment of my own, I could see my way clear. But these possibilities seemed to her much more remote than the probability of her jumping safely to the moon. Another thing was certain. There was little chance of her making any great progress in Philadelphia. New York to her seemed the only field in which any marked advance could be made, but here again there were the same problems if not greater ones.

Mimi found a certain grim amusement in remembering the tenuous plans she had so confidently builded and which now had tumbled about her. Realizing her own weakness for the first time, comprehending that she could not make the fight alone, she wrote tentatively of her distress to her Aunt Sophie. The second day after her letter was posted there came an answer﻿—a warmingly cordial letter﻿—scolding Mimi because she had not called upon her aunt for aid long before, offering Mimi whatever aid she needed. From the envelope there fell a cheque of generous size. And the letter ended:

“Alone you will have little trouble, especially if you come to New York. But with the baby it will be harder﻿—you would have to be away all day﻿—and people, even here, do talk. I know just how you feel about it, but why don’t you put Jean in a home until you can get on your feet? I don’t urge this﻿—I merely suggest it. Do what you think is wisest and best.”

===Chinese===

她要同小让在一起的渴望那样尖锐，根子不单在他这场在她看来近乎神迹的脱险，也在于她经由这场病得出的决定，而那决定的无法回避，使她痛苦到无以复加。在小让性命悬秤的那些黑暗时辰里，米米正视了自己面前的实际处境。她无情地斩断她对他的爱织成的种种缠累，尽她所能地冷静掂量：哪一条路对他的好处最大。她发现自己不能去找亨特夫妇，也不能找继母，而她自己宁可死，也不肯把小让交给他们任何一方。这几条路因此断然排除。

照她这一年来打的这场仗那样再撑下去，同样不行。只要我能攒下一笔钱，寻一份稳定的进项，兴许自己开一间小小的裁缝铺，我就能看清路了。可这些前景在她看来，比纵身一跳安全落到月亮上的把握还渺茫。还有一件事也是定了的：在费城，她再有多大长进的可能也微乎其微。纽约在她看来是唯一能有大起色的地方，可到了那儿，同样的难题照样有，说不定更多。

米米想起自己当初信心十足搭起、如今塌在脚边的那些空中楼阁，觉得有一种冷冷的滑稽。生平第一次认清自己的无力，明白这场仗她一个人打不下去，她试探着给苏菲姨妈写信诉说了自己的窘境。信寄出后第二天，回信就到了——一封热得烫手的信——责备米米为什么不早得多就来求姨妈帮忙，并表示米米需要什么帮助都行。信封里落出一张数目大方的支票。信的结尾是：

“你一个人，麻烦不大，尤其若来纽约。可带着孩子就难了——你得整天在外头——人，就是在这儿，也要说闲话。你的心事我一清二楚，可你何不把小让送进一家育幼院，等你站稳了脚跟再说？我不劝你——只是提一句。你认为怎样最明智、最好，就怎样做。”

===Original===

Finally a decision had been made, the only possible way out, and she wept many bitter tears before she would let herself even consider this solution. Always, however, she had come back to it﻿—the suggestion of Aunt Sophie﻿—the one stable thing in a sea of uncertainties. She would put Jean in a children’s home, work as hard as mortal could toil, save her money until she had accumulated enough to assure herself and him freedom from their more immediate wants, and then get him again when she had gained her objective.

And here she was confronted with another problem. She was unwilling to put him in a Negro orphanage, for their all too-slender resources made it problematical if he would receive the care and attention he needed. She would rather struggle along in her present hopeless way than have him neglected. Nor would she want to place him in a white orphanage as a Negro child﻿—she knew the insults and slights he would be forced to suffer. The only recourse left to her and the one she decided upon was to place him in a Catholic orphanage and say nothing about his Negro blood. This had been done, she knew, even with children not nearly so fair as Jean. His French name would be an additional safeguard to him and further assurance he would be given all the advantages available.

She had had some qualms of conscience about this procedure. She had wondered if she were doing the right thing, if she were not placing Jean at such a disadvantage in the event his coloured blood were discovered that it would be greater than if his race were told of at the beginning. But she soon dismissed these fears from her mind. In the first place, it would be only for a short time, and in the second, he had so little coloured blood there was really little question that could be raised. Overshadowing both of these was the mood of desperation in which her ill fortune had forced her. Just as in the hours of that night when Jean was lying at the hospital she had been willing to go to any length to save him, so now she was willing to take any step, legal or illegal, to secure for him as much security and comfort as was possible. It was the relentless, the ruthless, the uncompromising logic of a mother fighting for its own, and obstacles of whatever size were bowled over in eagerness to gain that end.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

决定终于作出了，唯一走得通的路，而在容许自己哪怕考虑这个解决办法之前，她流了许多苦泪。可她一次次还是回到它上来——苏菲姨妈的建议——茫茫不定之海里唯一不动的东西。她要把小让送进一家儿童养育院，然后拼却凡人所能的力气做工，攒钱，攒到足以使她和他不再有眼前这些匮乏，然后，等达到目的，再把他接回来。

而在这里她又碰上另一个难题。她不肯把他送进黑人孤儿院，因为那里经费太紧，他能不能得到需要的照料大成问题。她宁可照眼前这样无望地苦撑，也不愿他受怠慢。她也不愿把他当作一个黑人孩子送进白人孤儿院——她知道他会被迫忍受的种种侮辱和白眼。留给她的唯一一条路，也是她选定的路，是把他送进一家天主教孤儿院，对他身上的黑人血统只字不提。这样的事，她知道，连肤色远不如小让白的孩子都有人做过。他的法国名字对他又是一重保障，进一步保证他能得到那里的一切好处。

对这一做法她良心上不是没有不安过。她疑心自己做得对不对，疑心万一他的黑人血统被发现，她这样安排反倒叫他吃更大的亏，还不如一开始就讲明他的种族。可这些恐惧很快被她抛开了。第一，那不过是很短的一段时间；第二，他的黑人血液那么少，实在没有什么可挑剔的。压倒这两条的是厄运逼出来的那股孤注一掷的心境。正如小让躺在医院里的那一夜的时辰里，为了救他她什么都肯干，如今为了替他争得尽可能多的安稳和舒适，任何一步她也肯迈，合法的也好，不合法的也好。那是一个母亲为自己骨肉搏斗时的那种不容情的、狠辣的、决不妥协的逻辑，为了达到那个目的，多大的障碍也一并撞翻。……

---

===Original===

She had decided on a home in Baltimore, for she felt that there where so many Catholics lived there would be greater advantages for the unfortunate children of that faith. The bleak November day when she carried Jean there was no more cold and dreary than Mimi’s heart. Petit Jean seemed to feel the impending separation. He clung to Mimi, showering little affections upon her that tore her heart with pain. Her eyes filled with tears, she rushed from the place as though it were plague-stricken to take the train to New York. She had written her Aunt Sophie telling her plans and she had received a letter, short, but warm and sincere. Her aunt had approved her plans and urged her to come to New York and live with her while she was working out a solution to her problems. Mimi had accepted gratefully, glad of the haven to which she could go.

===Chinese===

她选中了巴尔的摩的一家养育院，因为她觉得，那里天主教徒众多，同教的苦命孩子在那边会有更多的好处。她抱着小让去的那个十一月的日子，天色阴冷，却冷不过米米的心。小让像是感到了迫近的分离。他缠着米米，把小小的爱意一阵阵泼在她身上，疼得她心都撕裂了。她眼里喻着泪，飞奔着逃离那个地方，仿佛那里染着瘟疫，去赶开往纽约的火车。她已写信把计划告诉了苏菲姨妈，也收到了回信，短短的，却温暖而恳切。姨妈赞成她的计划，催她来纽约住在她那里，一面设法解决自己的问题。米米感激地接受了，庆幸自己有一个可以投奔的港湾。

## XV / 十五
===Original===

Just as she had had the feeling on leaving Atlanta that she had closed the pages of a volume in the story of her life, so now did Mimi sense intuitively that the second book was being shut, never to be opened again. Through the fading light of a dreary, cold day the train sped across the desolate Jersey meadows. Just dipping below the horizon, a frigid sun gave a sickly brilliance but no warmth. Mimi shivered. The dreariness of the landscape was relieved only by the lank chimneys of factories belching turgescent billows of smoke. From these dingy buildings poured streams of men as dingy as the factories from which they were emerging. Dinner pails in hand, they plodded wearily across the waste places in knots of twos and threes, home to slatternly, waspish wives.

Into the murky darkness of Newark the train rumbled, paused momentarily, and then went its way into the gathering darkness. Lights began to twinkle in the dusty windows of the factories and the nondescript homes along the tracks. Into Manhattan Transfer and out again, past a fertilizer factory that filled the car with the fetid odours of offal, and the train plunged into the roaring, alarming bowels of the earth beneath the Hudson River. Mimi knew of the tunnel and was prepared for it but the shock coming so suddenly following the dismal screeching of the whistle blowing for the tunnel frightened her. Midway where there is an opening going up from the tunnel to the air above she felt relieved that the ordeal was over but again the train rushed on into darkness after the flash of grey. Her ears pounded. She pressed the heels of her hands against them to relieve the roaring in them. She was glad when at last the electric engine pulled its way into the maze of winding tracks on the New York side.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

正如离开亚特兰大时她曾觉得自己合上了生平一册的书页，此刻米米也直觉地感到，第二本书正在合上，永不再翻开。一个阴冷凄凉的日子，火车趁着渐暗的天光飞驰过荒凉的泽西草泽。一轮刚要沉下地平线的冷阳，发出病恹恹的亮，却没有一丝暖意。米米打了个寒噤。这片荒凉里，唯一能提提神的是工厂瘦长的烟囱，喷出一团团肿胀的浓烟。从那些灰扑扑的厂房里，涌出一股股同厂房一样灰扑扑的人流。他们手拎饭盒，三三两两疲惫地跋过荒地，回到邋遢、凶悍的妻子那里去。

火车轰隆隆驶入纽瓦克昏暗的暮色，略停一停，又继续奔进四合的黑暗。铁道沿线工厂的积尘的窗子、说不上名目的住家，亮起了灯。驶进曼哈顿枢纽站，又驶出来，过一座肥料厂——满车厢都是下水的恶臭——随后火车一头扎进哈得孙河底下那咆哮的、骇人的地腹。米米知道这条隧道，也有准备，可汽笛为隧道发出凄厉的长鸣之后，冲击来得这样突然，还是吓了她一跳。中途有一处从隧道向上通到地面空气的豁口，闪光般的灰色掠过，她宽了宽心，以为磨难到头了，火车却又冲进黑暗。她的耳朵轰轰作响。她用手掌根抵住耳朵，压一压那轰鸣。当电力机车终于把自己拖进纽约一侧迷宫般的弯轨时，她高兴了。……

---

===Original===

Bewildered by the hugeness of the Pennsylvania Station, made timid by the rushing throngs all seemingly with some definite destination the reaching of which in the shortest possible time was apparently of vast importance, Mimi with protest yielded her shabby bag to the porter and followed him up the narrow stairs. She peered upwards at the faces which scanned the incoming passengers through iron fences which made her think of prisoners or animals eager to escape. She was gripped with fear﻿—no Aunt Sophie was to be seen, but that fear was changed to happiness when she was inundated with affectionate greeting by her aunt.

“You poor dear, I ought to be angry with you for not coming to me sooner, but I’m so glad to see you I just can’t be,” her aunt welcomed her.

Threading her way deftly through the crowd, she led Mimi, her eyes distended at the bewildering massiveness of the building, down into the earth again. Ashamed of her greenness, Mimi shrank from the roaring monster that leapt upon them from the yawning blackness of a cavern made blacker by dim lights but faintly discernible in the cavern. With a horrible grinding of brakes the creature came to a stop and Mimi found herself shoved into it. Station after station they passed and once more they came to the street, this time in a setting more familiar to her, yet strange, and her bewilderment was decreased but little.

For here was a new life, teeming, exotic, individual. Hurrying along the streets, coming out of restaurants, standing in doorways and on street corners were groups of Negroes, well dressed, jubilant, cheerful. Here and there hurried coloured men in twos and threes, clad in smartly fitting dinner jackets, snow-white bosoms peeping from heavy overcoats, musical instruments, violins, saxophones, mandolins in cases clutched in their hands. They hastened into the subway kiosk Mimi and her aunt had just quitted and were swallowed up. From nearby there came to them the bewitching odours of frying corn and chicken, of pig’s-feet. They came from a stand where a middle-aged coloured woman was serving her wares to passersby.

“That’s ‘Pig-Foot Mary’﻿—she’s making lots of money at that little stand and she’s saving it, too,” Mrs. Rogers informed Mimi.

===Chinese===

宾夕法尼亚车站的庞大把她弄懵了，匆匆涌动的人潮又使她怯场——那些人个个仿佛都揣着一个明确的去处，而尽快赶到那里显然是天大的事——米米半推半就地交出寒酸的提包，跟着搬运工走上窄窄的楼梯。她抬眼窥望那些隔着铁栅栏打量到站旅客的脸，那栅栏使她想起急欲逃走的囚徒或野兽。恐惧攝住了她——不见苏菲姨妈的影子——可那恐惧随即化作欢喜，姨妈潮水般的爱抚把她淹没了。

“你这可怜的宝贝，你早不投奔我，本该生你的气，可见了你实在生不起来，”姨妈这样迎她。

她敏捷地领着米米穿过人群——米米的眼睛瞪得溜圆，被这建筑的庞大浩繁弄晕了——又下到地底。米米为自己的土气羞愧，在那咆哮的怪物面前直往回缩——它从洞窟张开的大黑洞里朝她们扑来，暗淡的灯把洞窟衬得更黑，只有那怪物在暗中依稀可辨。刹车一声可怕的摩擦，那家伙停了下来，米米发觉自己被推进了它的肚子。一站一站过去，她们重又回到街上，这一回的环境对她熟悉些了，却仍旧陌生，她的晕头转向也没减轻多少。

因为这里是一种新的生活，沸腾的，异域的，独一份的。街上匆匆走着的、从饭馆里出来的、站在门口和街角的，是一群群黑人（Negro），衣冠楚楚，喜气洋洋，欢欣快活。这里那里，有色男人三三两两赶着路，身穿合体的夜礼服，雪白的胸衬从厚重的大衣里探出，手里攔着装提琴、萨克斯、曼陀林的盒子。他们匆匆钻进米米和姨妈刚走出的地铁口，被吞没了。近处飘来诱人的香气，煎玉米、煎鸡、炖猪脚。香气来自一个小摊，一个中年的有色女人正在向过路人售卖她的吃食。

“那是‘猪脚玛丽’——她靠那小摊挣大钱呢，还攒，”罗杰斯太太告诉米米。

===Original===

She was thrilled by the new scene. Gone was the morbid, morose, worried air of the people she had encountered at the other end of the subway. Here there was spontaneous laughter, shrewd observations which brought loud and free laughter from listeners. There was an exhilarating sense here that these people knew the secret of enjoying life. Black and brown and yellow faces flitted by, some carefree, some careworn. Mimi sensed again the essential rhythm, the oneness of these variegated colours and moods. It was all vivid, colourful, of a pattern distinctive and apart, and she warmed to the friendliness of it all.﻿ ﻿…

Behind them stood two flashily dressed coloured men as they took it all in. The voice of one worried, the other conciliatory.

“Say, Sam, I sent the fifty dollars and my application to Jim Washington like you told me to,” the worried voice complained, “and I ain’t heard nothing a-tall from him or the Idlehour Social Club either.”

“Ain’t you heard you was blackballed?” the other inquired with a markedly incredulous air.

“No, I ain’t. I wonder who could’ve blackballed me,” he speculated, the worry in his tones increasing. “There’s Pudd’n Jones﻿—he’s a friend of mine and he wouldn’t do that to me. And there’s Babe Carter and Spider and Bill Johnson﻿—they’re friends of mine and they wouldn’t blackball me, I know. Say, was there many black balls in that box?” he inquired, hopefully.

“You’ve seen a bowl of Russian caviar, ain’t you?” his friend asked.

“Yeh﻿—but what’s that got to do with them blackballing, me?”

“Well, boy, just this﻿—that box when they voted on your name looked just like a bowl of caviar.”﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

这新景象使她激动。地铁另一头那些人身上的阴郁、愁苦、忧心忡忡的神气，在这儿一扫而空。这里有无缘无故就爆出的笑，有逗得听众放声大笑的俏皮话。这里有一种使人振奋的感觉：这些人懂得享受生活的秘诀。黑脸、棕脸、黄脸一张张掠过，有的无忧无虑，有的满腹心事。米米又一次感到那本质的节律、这斑驳的色彩与心绪的浑然一体。一切都那么鲜明，那么多彩，自成一格、与众不同，这一切的友善使她心里发热。……

她们把这一切尽收眼底时，身后站着两个穿戴花哨的有色男人。一个的声音发着愁，另一个在打圆场。

“我说，萨姆，我照你说的，把五十块钱和申请书寄给吉姆·华盛顿了，”发愁的声音抱怨道，“可他那边，还有闲时俱乐部那边，愣是一个字也没回我。”

“你没听说你被投了黑球？”另一位问，一副明显的不敢相信。

“没有哇，我没听说。真纳闷谁能投我黑球，”他猜度着，话音里的愁愈来愈重。“布丁·琼斯——他是我的朋友，不会干这个。还有贝布·卡特、蜘蛛、比尔·约翰逊——都是我的朋友，我知道他们不会投我黑球。哎，那箱子里黑球多不多？”他抱着指望问。

“你见过一碗俄罗斯鱼子酱吧？”他朋友问。

“见过——可那跟他们投我黑球有啥相干？”

“这个嘛，老弟——就是这么回事——他们表决你的名字那阵子，那口箱子看上去活像一碗鱼子酱。”……

===Original===

“Come along, Mimi, we can’t stand here all night﻿—dinner’s ready at home,” urged Mrs. Rogers. Mimi could have stood there for hours. The colourful scenes fascinated her, made her want to stay there and watch the comedies, the tragedies, the shifting panorama of life as it flowed, now swiftly, now slowly, by. Down Lenox Avenue they walked. Harlem, which first had been Dutch “Haarlem,” then Irish, afterwards Jewish and German, was in the flood-tide of transition to a Negro city within a city. From the doorway of a former private dwelling in a side street near bustling Lenox Avenue came a resonant flooding swell of music. High above the rest sounded a heavy baritone, chopping off of the words like little sausages, setting the beat like a metronome for the other singers.

“What a friend we have in Jesus,

All our sins and griefs to bear.”

Verse after verse poured forth, mingling with the clatter and roar of the crowds outside. Mimi and her aunt paused unwittingly outside. The song had ceased. The baritone voice of the preacher was lifted in prayer, his words punctuated by fervent “Amens!” and “Hallelujahs!” and “Praise Gods!”

“We are vile, sinful creatures, O Lord, strayed from the path You tol’ us to follow. (Ain’t it the Gospel!) Reach down from Thy heaven with a sword of flaming fire and wipe from the face of the earth these hellholes of vice that’re sending the souls of our boys and girls to damnation! (Yes, Lord, reach down!﻿—Amen!) Wipe out, Jesus, the dance halls and theatres, the gamblers and the drunkards, the wolves in sheep’s clothes who’re workin’ ’gainst Thy cause.﻿ ﻿… Purify us, Lord! Save us! Save us!﻿ ﻿…”

The preacher’s voice swayed and rumbled, excoriating the sinners, pleading for redemption of their souls, damning to everlasting hellfire those who were unrepentant. The unseen voice was talking to a God who was no mystery to him, a very real and ever present and potent Deity. Down the street his words followed them as they went home.﻿ ﻿…

---

===Chinese===

“走吧，米米，咱们不能在这儿站一晚上——家里饭都好了，”罗杰斯太太催促道。米米本来能在这儿站上几个钟头。这五光十色的场面迷住了她，叫她想留在这里看那一出出喜剧、悲剧，看人生这幅流动的画卷时而疾、时而缓地淌过。她们沿勒诺克斯大道走下去。哈莱姆，起初是荷兰人的“哈勒姆”，后来归了爱尔兰人，再后来归了犹太人和德国人，如今正处在变成城中黑人之城的洪峰般的过渡里。紧挨着热闹的勒诺克斯大道的一条横街上，一幢从前的私宅门口，涌出一阵洪亮的乐声。高出众人之上的是一个厚重的男中音，把字剁成一小节一小节的香肠，像节拍器似的给其余的歌手定拍子。

“何等朋友我主耶稣，

担当我们一切罪忧。”

一节又一节倾泻出来，同外头人群的嘈杂与轰响搅在一处。米米和姨妈不知不觉在门外站住了。歌声停了。传道人那男中音响起来做祷告，他的话被火热的“阿们！”“哈利路亚！”“赞美上帝！”敲着点。

“我们是卑污的罪人，主啊，迷失了你吩咐我们走的路。（这不正是福音！）从你的天上下手来，拿着烈火的剑，把地上那些把我们的男孩女孩的灵魂送进沉沦的罪恶渊薮统统抹去！（是，主，伸手下来！——阿们！）抹掉吧，耶稣，那些舞厅和戏园子，那些赌棍和醉汉，那些披着羊皮破坏你事业的豺狼。……洁净我们，主！救我们！救我们！……”

传道人的声音摇曳着、轰鸣着，痛斥罪人，恳求他们灵魂得救，把不悔改的打入永劫的地狱之火。那看不见的声音在同一位上帝说话，那上帝于他不是什么玄奥，而是一位实实在在、无时不在、全能的神。她们往家走，他的话顺着街一路跟着她们。……

---

===Original===

Mimi looked back on her days of suffering and poverty almost as though it had been a terrible dream. Her aunt deluged her with kindness, delicately drew from her the story of her unsuccessful struggle in Philadelphia against odds that proved too great for her. The one link with that nightmare of pain and worry and anxiety was her aching need of Petit Jean. Night after night she cried herself to sleep. Night after night she awoke when all the rest of the household was asleep, feeling him near her, his warm flesh nestled against her side. She could feel his tiny lips against her own, his hands clutching at her breast, his laugh sounding in her ears. Only the thought that someday soon she would be able to have him with her again comforted her, only the realization that she could not possibly make her way as fast towards that goal if he were with her restrained her from going back to Baltimore and taking him from the home.

Her Aunt Sophie was, she found, gifted with a fund of hard common sense but with it she possessed a warmth of feeling that made Mimi know the older woman understood all her difficulties without the necessity of putting into words her various moods. Mr. Rogers had held for a number of years before his death a responsible position in a bank down town. He had left his widow some money and a few shares of stock in various corporations that brought her a steady, but small, income. By practice of rigid economy Mrs. Rogers could have lived in fairly comfortable circumstances but she was unwilling to do this. She had married young and was of a more progressive nature than her brother, although she understood Jean as well as had Margot. After her husband’s death Mrs. Rogers had taken a course in manicuring, massaging and the treatment of the hair and scalp. She then had opened a small “beauty parlour,” from which she derived a small income after all expenses were paid. Best of all, it kept her busy, and that was of greater importance to her than the money she earned. With a widowed sister of her husband she kept a small flat near Lenox Avenue, and it was to this she welcomed Mimi.﻿ ﻿…

The morning after her arrival Mimi had a long talk with her aunt.

===Chinese===

米米回望自己受苦受穷的那些日子，几乎像看一场可怕的梦。姨妈对她倾泻着慈爱，又巧妙地从她嘴里引出了她在费城那段寡不敌众、终告败北的挣扎。同那场痛苦、忧惧、焦灼的噩梦相连的只有一样：她对小让钻心的思念。一夜又一夜，她哭着入睡。一夜又一夜，全家都睡熟了，她醒来，觉得他就在身边，温热的小身子偎着她的腰。她能感到他小小的嘴唇贴着她的唇，他的手抓着她的胸口，他的笑声响在她耳边。只有想到不久的哪一天她就能重新把他带在身边，才安慰得了她；只有想到他若跟着她，她朝那个目标赶路的脚步必会慢得多，才拦住了她，没有回巴尔的摩把他从养育院里接走。

她发现，苏菲姨妈天生有一大笔扎实的常识，而同这常识在一起的，还有一种感情上的温暖，使米米知道，老人家懂得她全部的难处，用不着她把各样心绪一一说出口。罗杰斯先生去世前，多年在城里一家银行担任要职。他给遗孀留下几个钱和几家公司的一点股票，给她带来一份稳定却不多的进项。只要狠狠地省，罗杰斯太太本可以过得相当舒服，可她不肯这样过。她结婚早，天性比她弟弟进取，虽然她同玛戈一样了解让。丈夫死后，罗杰斯太太学了修指甲、按摩和头发头皮护理的课程，随后开了一间小小的“美容院”，付清一切开销后还能剩点小进项。顶要紧的是，它使她忙碌，这在她看来比挣的钱更要紧。她同丈夫的一位守寡的妹妹合住勒诺克斯大道附近一套小公寓，米米投奔的就是这里。……

到达后的第二天早晨，米米同姨妈长谈了一次。

===Original===

“You’ve had a hard time, Mimi,” the older woman said. “You’ve made what people call a serious mistake﻿—it’s not for me to judge you nor am I going to. If you did, you’ve paid for it﻿—and you’ve done a mighty brave thing, to my way of thinking, in sticking it out as you have. Your problem now as I see it is to forget the past and decide what you’re going to do with your future. You’re young yet﻿—just twenty-one, aren’t you?﻿—you’re much better-looking than the average. The best part of your life’s ahead of you. The question you’ve got to decide is what you’re going to do with it, make it fruitful and full and happy? Or let the past swallow you up?”

She paused while Mimi sat in thought.

“Jean﻿—and his future count much more than mine, Aunt Sophie.”

“Yes, I know how you feel. What I’m going to say may sound mighty hard and unfeeling but you know I don’t feel that way. With Jean you’ll be so handicapped you’ll settle into a rut out of which you’ll probably never emerge. He’s well fed, he’s sheltered, he’s clothed, he’s being trained, educated. He hasn’t your care and training, I know, but working all day, you wouldn’t be able to give him much attention if he were with you.﻿ ﻿… In Philadelphia you by some miracle were able to avoid meddling inquiry about Jean’s parentage. Here you wouldn’t be so successful, I know. There are gossips everywhere﻿—and in every race. So my advice is to let Jean stay there a few years, run down to see him whenever you can afford it, and get to the place where you can have Jean with you again. And﻿—who knows?﻿—perhaps the right man﻿—”

“No, no, Aunt Sophie. That’s all done for now. I’m through with men﻿—”

Mrs. Rogers smiled. “You say that now. But wait awhile. Youth and especially youth like yours can work wonders. Standards are changing﻿—New York isn’t New Orleans or Atlanta﻿—the man who may come may have sense enough to see what’s back of your﻿—well, your so-called misfortune. Men are simple creatures after all though we women make of them in our own minds an insoluble riddle.”

Wise in the ways of the world, tolerant and understanding of human foibles and weaknesses, Mrs. Rogers smiled reminiscently.

===Chinese===

“你吃了苦了，米米，”老人家说。“你犯了人家所谓的严重过失——轮不到我来评判你，我也不评判。就算犯了，你也偿付过了——而且照我看，你这样咬牙撑到底，是一件非常有勇气的壮举。依我看，你眼下的难题是忘掉过去，拿定主意往后怎么办。你还年轻——才二十一，对不对？——你的模样远在一般人之上。你一生最好的部分还在前头。你必须决定的问题是：拿它怎么办，让它丰饶、充实、幸福？还是让过去把你吞掉？”

她停住，米米坐着出神。

“让——和他的前途，比我的重得多，苏菲姨妈。”

“是，我知道你的心思。我底下要说的话，听着也许硬心肠、不近人情，可你知道我并不是那个意思。带着小让，你会被拖累得死死的，陷进一条车辙里，多半再也爬不出来。他吃得饱，有屋住，有衣穿，正受着管教、教育。他缺你的照应，这我知道，可你整天做工，他就算跟着你，你也给不了他多少工夫。……在费城，你凭着奇迹躲开了关于小让身世的盘问。到这儿你就不会那样顺当了，我知道。哪儿都有长舌的人——哪一个族里都有。所以我的劝告是：让小让在那边待上几年，攒得起就下去看看他，一路走到能把小让重新接回身边的那一步。再说了——谁知道呢？——也许有那么一个合适的男人——”

“不，不，苏菲姨妈。这已经完结了。我跟男人完了——”

罗杰斯太太笑了。“你如今这么说。等等看吧。青春，尤其是你这样的青春，能创造奇迹。风气在变——纽约不是新奥尔良，也不是亚特兰大——来的那个人，兴许有那个见识，看得清你那——嗯，你所谓的不幸背后是什么。男人到底是简单的造物，尽管我们女人在自己的想象里把他们弄成了一道解不开的谜。”

深谙世故、对人性的小毛病小弱点又宽容又体谅的罗杰斯太太，若有所思地微笑了。

===Original===

“I’ve seen lots of things in my time, Mimi, and I’ve learned a lot of things, too. There are some people whose notion of getting along in the world is to butt their brains out against the walls that come up in front of them. Others go to the other extreme and dodge every obstacle, taking the easiest way out. Maybe they’re both right﻿—but I doubt it. The best way is to hold on to your ideals, yield only when you have to, but keep that thing we call our soul intact, no matter what we go up against.”

She toyed with the handle of her coffee-cup ruminatively.

“Take men, for example. Mr. Rogers and I had our times of disagreement, every married couple does. Much as I loved him, there were times when he irritated me so I could almost cheerfully have choked him. But I learned soon how to handle him﻿—and when all’s said and done, all men are alike﻿—they differ in little ways but, by and large, they’re the same. One of the things I learned was this﻿—never, never argue with a man. On the other hand, don’t fall into agreement too easily with his opinions. If you always insist you’re right﻿—even if you are and you know it﻿—he’ll think you stubborn and mulish and you stir up all sorts of stubbornness in him. And if you always agree with him he’ll think you’re soft and weak and silly. But if you express an opinion different from what you’re sure is right and then﻿—not too easily let him convince you of the error of your reasoning﻿—then, my dear, he’ll be putty in your hands!”

“But suppose he should have the same opinion as your wrong one﻿—what then?” Mimi inquired, interested in spite of herself in the philosophy her aunt was unfolding.

“Then exercise a woman’s right﻿—the privilege of changing her mind. But you won’t have to do that often﻿—man likes to prove his self-assumed masculine superiority too much to realize how you’re handling him.”

“That sounds dangerously like opportunism﻿—er﻿—it might even be called deceit,” Mimi laughed.

===Chinese===

“我这一辈子见过的事多了，米米，学到的东西也多了。有一种人，他们所谓在世界上混，就是拿脑袋去撞迎面竖起的墙，把脑浆撞出来。另一种人走另一个极端，什么障碍都绕着走，专拣最容易的路。也许他们两边都对——可我犯疑。最好的路是：守住你的理想，万不得已才让步，可要把我们称作灵魂的那样东西保得完完整整，不管撞上什么。”

她若有所思地摆弄着咖啡杯的柄。

“拿男人打个比方。罗杰斯先生和我也闹过别扭，哪对夫妻不闹。我那么爱他，可有几回他把我气得，简直能痛痛快快掐死他。可我很快就学会了怎么摆弄他——说到底，天下男人都一样——小处不同，大体上是一样的。我学会的一条是这个——永远、永远不要同男人争论。可另一方面，也别太轻易附和他的意见。你要是回回咬定自己对——哪怕你真对、你自己也知道——他就嫌你倔、嫌你犟，把他一身的犟劲全逗起来。你要是回回依他，他又当你软弱、没主意、傻。可你若先说出一个明知不对的意见，然后——别太顺溜地——让他把你道理上的错处说服过来，那么，亲爱的，他就成了你手里的一团烂泥！”

“可要是他碰巧同你的那个错意见一模一样——那怎么办？”米米问，不由自主地对姨妈展开的这套哲学上了瘾。

“那就行使女人的权利——改主意的特权。不过这种时候不多——男人太爱证明他那自封的男性优越，根本发觉不了你是怎样摆布他的。”

“这听着危险得很，像机会主义——呢——简直可以叫欺骗了，”米米笑了。

===Original===

“Yes, I suppose it can,” Mrs. Rogers frankly admitted. “But life’s like that﻿—you can go tilting at windmills and getting knocked over for your pains or you can adapt yourself to the things you find around you. After all, the important thing is to work out for one’s own guidance the philosophy in keeping with one’s ideas and ideals and then extract from life all the happiness one can without destroying what’s more important than everything else﻿—one’s inner self. When that’s gone, then you can have every material thing there is﻿—and you’ll know misery such as even you have never experienced or ever will know.﻿ ﻿…”

---

The new life she was ushered into through Aunt Sophie seemed by comparison with her experiences since she left Atlanta almost idyllic to Mimi. Through her aunt’s influence, she secured work sewing, the only thing she could do by which she could earn money. But for this she had a certain gift. She loved the feel of silks and satins and chiffons; the finer the fabric, the more she liked to handle it and make it into alluring garments. All day she worked and in the evenings she either stayed at home and made dresses and other garments for herself to replace the worn things she had brought to New York, or with her aunt she walked through the streets of Harlem or rode on the bus down Fifth Avenue. Occasionally this schedule was varied by a visit to the theatre, which Mimi loved. A new world was opening to her and in it she found greater happiness than she had believed she would ever know again. These unfolded before her eyes a gloriously fascinating scene and she sometimes felt as though a giant scroll were being unwound for her own amusement and interest. This joy was added to by the modest yet steady progress she was making towards reclaiming Jean. Each week she added to the little sum in the bank. At times she seemed to be making tragically little headway, at times she was delighted at the speed with which the dollars accumulated.

===Chinese===

“是，我想是可以，”罗杰斯太太坦然承认。“可生活就是这样——你可以去同风车搏斗，落个人仰马翻，也可以随遇而安，适应你周遭的东西。说到底，要紧的是替自己立一套同自己的观念理想相合的处世哲学，然后从生活里榨取你能榨的一切幸福，同时不毁掉那比一切都更重要的东西——你的内在自我。那个没了，你就是把世上的财物样样抱全——你尝到的苦，连你也是从没尝过、往后也尝不到的。……”

---

经由苏菲姨妈引进的这份新生活，同她离开亚特兰大以来的经历一比，在米米几乎算得上田园诗了。靠着姨妈的门路，她找到了缝纫的活计——她唯一能挣钱的本事。好在这份本事她有点天分。她爱真丝、缎子和雪纺绸的手感，料子越精细，她越喜欢上手，把它们做成勾人的衣裳。她整日做工，晚上或者待在家里给自己做衣裳和别的，补换带来纽约的那些穿旧的东西，或者同姨妈一起去逛哈莱姆的街道，或者坐公共汽车沿第五大道下行进城。间或也变变花样看一场戏，米米爱看戏。一个新的世界正朝她打开，她在里面找到了比她相信自己还能再知道的更大的幸福。这一切在她眼前展开一幕光辉迷人的景象，她有时觉得，仿佛一幅巨幅长卷正为她一人的消遣和兴味舒卷开来。这份喜悦上又添了一份：她朝赎回小让的目标稳步而有限地进展着。每星期她往银行里那笔小小的存款添一笔。有时她觉得进展慢得叫人寒心，有时那一块块美元积攒的速度又叫她欢喜。

===Original===

Most interesting to her was the transition she saw going on around her in Harlem. Gradually yet surely there was being builded a Negro city here in northern Manhattan. For hours she would sit silent, listening with a strange eagerness to the stories her aunt told her of her own people in New York. She insisted that Mrs. Rogers take her to the sections where Negroes had lived in years past; they wandered through Broome and Spring Streets, where Negroes had lived before the Civil War, through Sullivan and Bleecker Streets and Minetta Lane in Greenwich Village, now settled thickly with Italians, among them little groups of coloured people who had never lived anywhere else.

But neither of these settlements nor the former habitats in the Twenties and Thirties interested her nearly so much as the section which Negroes had but lately quitted for Harlem. She made her aunt tell her of the Maceo and Marshall’s and other rendezvous of Negro and white musicians and writers and bohemians. She asked many questions about Williams and Walker, about Aida Overton, Jim Europe, Buddy Gilmore who served as model for trap drummers in all the orchestras, Ernest Hogan, Cole and Johnson, and many others of the stars of the stage. Mrs. Rogers had lived near the Marshall and had known everybody who was worth knowing. She told Mimi of the brilliant Sunday nights when they used to dine to the music of a Negro orchestra at Marshall’s, when one was always sure of finding interesting companions and acquaintances. She told of this orchestra, the members of which played and sang and danced and established the vogue out of which sprang the modern cabaret. Mimi wished fervidly she had been a part of this brilliant scene, might have known in the flesh these of whom she had heard so often even in New Orleans.

===Chinese===

她最感兴趣的，是她看见在哈莱姆四周进行着的这场变迁。一座黑人的城，正渐渐然而确凿地在曼哈顿北部建立起来。她会一连几个钟头默坐着，怀着一种奇异的渴望，听姨妈讲她自己人在纽约的故事。她一定要罗杰斯太太带她去看从前那些年黑人住过的地段；她们漫游了布鲁姆街和斯普林街——内战前黑人聚居的地方——漫游了格林尼治村的沙利文街、布利克街和米内塔巷，如今那里意大利人住得密密实实，其中夹着一小簇一小簇的有色人，他们从没在别处住过。

可这两个旧居，还有二十几街、三十几街那些旧巢，都远不及黑人新近才迁出、搬来哈莱姆之前住的那一片叫她入迷。她要姨妈讲马切奥和马歇尔旅馆，讲黑人和白人乐师、文人、波希米亚们的其他聚点。她问了许许多多关于威廉斯和沃克、艾达·奥弗顿、吉姆·尤罗普、巴迪·吉尔摩——全城各乐队小鼓手的样板——欧内斯特·霍根、科尔和约翰逊，还有舞台上许多别的明星。罗杰斯太太从前就住在马歇尔附近，凡是值得认识的人她全认识。她给米米讲那些辉煌的星期天夜晚，他们惯常在马歇尔听着黑人乐队的音乐进晚餐，那一晚你准能遇上有趣的朋友和相识。她讲那支乐队，队员又奏又唱又跳，立起了那股风气，现代卡巴莱便从那里面长出来。米米热切地但愿自己曾是那辉煌景象的一部分，但愿亲眼见过这些人——连在新奥尔良她都常常听人说起的人。

===Original===

She was new enough to this growth to enable her to see how it formed itself and gradually spread over a greater territory. It seemed that every month saw the opening up to coloured tenants and buyers of new blocks. A group of white property-owners became alarmed and formed a holding company to buy all property occupied by Negro tenants and eject them. This step was promptly met by a countermove on the part of an intelligent coloured real-estate dealer who formed a Negro realty company to purchase for coloured tenants apartment houses of the best class. Then came panic among the whites, who deserted houses long before there was any invasion of their neighbourhoods by coloured tenants. Prices dropped and Negroes reaped the benefit by buying at these figures. The district spread with increasing rapidity and the new city was definitely in the making.﻿ ﻿…

Mimi kept track of these changes through the interesting circle of friends which frequented her Aunt Sophie’s house. There were types she had not known before﻿—business men who had a broad vision of the city that was to be, men who were eager, of course, to make money but who made personal gain secondary to the deep love of race and pride in the advances of that race toward a more secure foothold than was theirs in most parts of the country. There were the wives of these men, some of them of small minds fixed on nothing but dress and gossip and petty, unimportant social affairs, but others of them intelligent and valuable aids to their husbands. Some of the women were in business for themselves like Mrs. Rogers and they were no less shrewd and farseeing than the men, in a number of cases more so. It was through these and their conversations, to which was added her own deductions and observations, that she saw the thing which interested her develop. This live, pulsating, vigorous movement fascinated her, for it gave her interests which kept her mind alert, and free from too great concentration on her own problems.

===Chinese===

她来得够新，正好看得见这番生长如何成形、又如何渐渐漫过更大的地盘。仿佛每一个月都有新的街区向有色租客和买主敞开。一群白人业主慌了，组了一家控股公司，要买下全部黑人租住的房产，把他们撵走。这一步立刻遭到回击，一个精明的有色地产捐客针锋相对，组了一家黑人地产公司，替有色租客买进头等公寓楼。随后白人那边起了恐慌，不等有色租客真侵入他们的街区，早早便弃屋而去。价钱落下来，黑人趁低价买进，得了实惠。地盘以越来越快的速度扩展，新城确确实实在成形了。……

这些变化，米米是从常来往于苏菲姨妈家的那个有趣的圈子得知的。那里有她从前不认识的人——一些对这个将成之城怀有远大眼光的生意人，他们当然也想挣钱，可把个人的进项排在后面，先是对种族的深爱与对种族在全国大部分地方尚不及的更稳固立足点的进展的自豪。有这些人的太太，有的眼界窄小，一心只在那衣裳、闲话和琐碎无谓的应酬上，可另一些聪明能干，是丈夫们得力的内助。还有些女人同罗杰斯太太一样自己做生意，她们不比男人少精明、少远见，有几桩比男人还强。正是通过这些人、这些谈话，加上她自己的推想和观察，她看着那件使她感兴趣的事一步步展开。这场鲜活的、搏动的、蓬勃的运动迷住了她，因为它给了她种种兴味，使她的头脑保持敏锐，不至于过分纠缠在自己的难题上。

===Original===

So too, in addition to Aunt Sophie’s friends, who soon became her friends as well, she and her aunt found great joy in modest excursions down town to the theatres and greater happiness in music. They purchased the cheapest seats available and climbed the innumerable stairs at the Metropolitan or at Carnegie Hall to hear a favourite opera or singer. To Mimi these were as drugs or liquor to addicts﻿—they swept her up, up above her narrow, difficult existence to a world where cares and sorrows and toils did not exist, where there was an ecstatic abandon to the intoxicating music that thrilled her so. She would have enjoyed the music under any circumstances but it gave her greater happiness because she knew she could not afford even these inexpensive and harmless debauches. It was her aunt who urged her to take them, who frequently paid for the tickets, telling Mimi they had been given to her because of Mimi’s insistence upon bearing an equal share of all costs of their journeys. On this point they often had good-natured quarrels﻿—Mimi had a violent hatred of sponging, of feeling under obligations to any person, even to one so near as her aunt. She knew her aunt had obligations she must meet, rental of her apartment and shop, the current expenses at both places, the care of her sister-in-law, a chronic invalid who seldom left her room and who played as little a part in the household as the decrepit chair in the closet on which old newspapers were piled. She was unwilling to add further to her aunt’s burdens and insisted upon paying for her room and board as though she had been merely a boarder.﻿ ﻿…

===Chinese===

同样，除了苏菲姨妈的朋友们——她们很快也成了她的朋友——她和姨妈还在朴素的进城远足里得到很大的快乐：去看戏，更大的快乐在音乐。她们买最便宜的票，爬上大都会或者卡内基音乐厅数不清的楼梯，去听一出心爱的歌剧或一位心爱的歌手。对米米，这些好比瘾君子的鸦片烈酒——把她卷起来，向上，升到她那狭窄艰难的日子之上，升到一个没有忧愁、悲哀、劳苦的世界，在那里只管出神地沉醉在叫她战栗的音乐里。无论什么境况她都会爱这音乐，可它给她更大的幸福，因为她知道自己连这些廉价而无害的放荡都负担不起。是姨妈劝她去的，常常是她掏的票钱，还哄米米说票是人家送的——因为米米坚持两人的出游费用一律均摊。这一点上她们常起善意的争执——米米痛恨揩油，痛恨欠人情，哪怕是姨妈这样亲的人。她知道姨妈有她必须应付的负担：寓所和铺面的房租，两处的日常开销，还有对小姑的照料——一个常年缠绵病榻、几乎不出房门的病人，她在这个家里的分量，同壁橱里那把堆着旧报纸的破椅子差不多。她不愿再给姨妈添担子，坚持照搭伙房客的规矩付她的房钱饭钱。……

===Original===

Another spring came and went, this time a happier one than Mimi had known for several years, merged imperceptibly into summer, when the only variations she permitted to her regular routine of work were trips to Coney Island with her aunt and some of their friends or rides down town and back of an evening on the Fifth Avenue buses. Mimi had decided that she could never make the progress which was so necessary if she continued only to do plain sewing. She always had a knack for devising new and unusual designs, many of them impractical but all of them touched with a distinctiveness that separated them from other clothes. She began in the fall a course in designing at a trade school, taking the course in the evenings. Despite the uneventfulness of her life and the steadiness of her application to the uninspiring routine, her health improved, colour returned to her cheeks, and her face lost its worried, haggard look. For Mimi was sustained by the goal towards which she was pushing﻿—all else seemed trivial and unimportant beside the reaching of the day when she could bring Petit Jean to her again.

===Chinese===

又一个春天来了又去了，这一次比米米好几年来过的哪个春天都快活，不知不觉便融进了夏天，这时她给自己刻板的工作日程容下的唯一调剂，是同姨妈和几个朋友去科尼岛，或者傍晚坐第五大道的公共汽车进城兜一圈再回来。米米认定，若照旧只做平缝活计，她绝达不到必需的进展。她素来有一手设计的新巧，会想出新鲜别致的式样，许多并不实用，可全都带着一种与众不同的气息，同别的衣裳一望而别。入秋后她在一所职业学校报了设计课程，晚上上课。尽管生活平静无事，尽管日复一日对着这不出彩的功课埋头用功，她的健康倒好了，脸颊有了血色，脸上那忧愁憔悴的样子褪去了。因为米米有那个她一路朝它推去的目标撑着——同能把小让重新接回来的那一天相比，别的一切都显得琐屑，无关紧要。

## XVI / 十六
===Original===

Mimi forgot the days of her misery. The years spent in Atlanta became hazy and unreal in the eager happiness of her new life. Again she had the feeling that the things which had distressed her so had never really happened﻿—that they were either the phantasmagoria of a nightmare or the sorrows of an existence she had lived prior to this life. Her stepmother she rarely thought of, she had returned to Chicago and there had married again. The Hunters she equally had removed from her thoughts. When she did think of them or of Carl they seemed unreal, like apparitions of an indistinctly remembered dream. She had completely recovered from even her hatred of and contempt for Carl. In the light of her life since she had left Atlanta, she saw him now even more clearly as the weak individual he was. She was amused now when she remembered how devotedly, how passionately she had loved him during those short months. “I *was* a silly little fool then,” she laughed amusedly at herself when she did remember. The entire episode seemed to her now a rather cheap and worthless thing. The only comfort she could extract from it was the satisfaction that she had not succumbed to a dishonest compromise.

Even her years in Philadelphia began to fade into the land of half-remembered, half-forgotten things. She occasionally corresponded with the Mannings but as the months sped by she found her interest lessening even in this contact with the old couple who had been so kind to her. The one link to both of these episodes in her life, for she now looked upon them only as episodes, was Petit Jean, and he served as a link and as the centre of all her hopes for the future. Under the skilful guidance of her aunt she had thrown off her moroseness and spells of despondency and was working indefatigably towards securing all the things which would bring happiness to her and Jean.

===Chinese===

米米忘掉了自己那些苦日子。在新生活的急切欢快里，亚特兰大度过的那些年变得模糊、不真实。她又生出那种感觉：那些曾那样折磨她的事其实从没有真正发生过——它们要么是一场噩梦里的幻灯连环，要么是她前一世活过的愁苦。继母她难得想起，那人回了芝加哥，又嫁了人。亨特夫妇她也同样撵出了思绪。真想起他们或卡尔的时候，他们显得不真实，像一场记不分明的梦里的人影。连对卡尔的恨和鄙薄，她也彻底痊愈了。凭着她离开亚特兰大以来的生活之光，她如今把他看得更清楚了，他就是那样一个软弱的人。想起自己那短短几个月里爱他爱得那样痴、那样狂，她如今只觉得好笑。“我那时候真是个傻乎乎的小傻瓜，”当真想起来时，她便这样自嘲地一笑。整件事如今在她看来是一件相当廉价、一钱不值的东西。她能从中榨出的唯一安慰，是那份没有向不老实的妥协低头的满足。

连费城那几年也开始褪进那片半记得、半忘却的地界。她偶尔同曼宁夫妇通通信，可随着月份飞逝，她发觉自己连对这对曾待她那样好的老夫妇的联系，兴味也淡了。她生平这两段插曲——如今她只把它们看作插曲——之间唯一的链环是小让，他既是链环，也是她全部未来希望的中心。在姨妈娴熟的引导下，她用掉了阴郁和一阵阵的消沉，不知疲倦地朝着争得一切能给她和小让带来幸福的东西做工。

===Original===

Back of her lay disappointment and sadness, ahead of her lay only hope. It was in this mood that she went with her aunt one Friday evening to a dance at Manhattan Casino. They had been invited to join a box party in which were included several younger people, three or four young men and three girls beside Mimi, in addition to Mrs. Rogers and two older persons. For the dance Mimi had made for herself a party dress of pale green charmeuse-green with the greenness of young leaves in springtime﻿—a green that was indefinably between jade and Eau-de-Nil.

“You look like a rosebud﻿—a marvellously golden flower springing out of its leafy greenness,” Tom Henderson whispered to her as they danced together.

The not very subtle compliment pleased Mimi. She was happy, too, she was capable of pleasure from such a source﻿—she had thought herself impervious to polite remarks of this sort﻿—she fancied she had put all this behind her forever. She was glad that this was not so﻿—after all, she yet was young. And he had seemed so sincere when he had said it.

All the evening she danced, danced with a lightness and ease that brought young men in droves to the box when she rested there between dances. Her feet seemed endowed with wings, for she moved over the floor without conscious effort. And the music! She had heard of Ford Dabney, had been told of the way in which his orchestra could play. But she had never dreamed that any human beings could entice from inanimate objects such intoxicating, inspiriting music. Once, and once only, was she able to sit in the box and watch the dancers.

“It’s a pretty sight,” her aunt said to her.

“Pretty? That isn’t the word for it!” she enthusiastically declared.

===Chinese===

身后躺着失望与悲伤，前头只躺着希望。正是带着这副心绪，一个星期五的晚上，她随姨妈去曼哈顿游乐场参加一场舞会。他们受邀加入一个包厢聚会，其中有几个年轻人：三四个青年男子，米米之外还有三位姑娘，再加上罗杰斯太太和两位年长的人。为这场舞会，米米给自己做了一身浅绿的绉缎赴会裙——那种绿是春日嫩叶的绿，一种说不清界于玉色和水绿色之间的绿。

“你像一朵玫瑰蓓蕾——一朵从满叶的翠绿里迸出来的金光灿烂的花，”汤姆·亨德森同她跳舞时，凑在她耳边说。

这不算很含蓄的恭维讨米米喜欢。她也快活——她居然还能从这样的源泉里得到快乐——她原以为自己早刀枪不入、不吃这一套了——她以为这一切已被她永远抛在身后了。她高兴事实并非如此——说到底，她还年轻。何况他说这话的时候显得那样诚恳。

一晚上她跳啊跳啊，跳得那样轻盈自在，她一回包厢歇脚，青年们便成群地涌到包厢来。她的脚像生了翅膀，在舞池里滑动，全不费力。还有那音乐！福德·达布尼的大名她听说过，人家也给她讲过他的乐队演奏起来是怎样一副光景。可她从没想到，人竟能从无生命的东西里引出这样叫人沉醉、叫人振奋的音乐。只有一回，仅仅一回，她得空坐在包厢里看人跳舞。

“好看的景致，”姨妈对她说。

“好看？这个词可不够！”她热烈地宣称。

===Original===

It was a waltz. Faces of all colours, peeping from gowns of all shades ranging from delicate pinks and blues and lavenders to gorgeously passionate reds. There were faces of a mahogany brownness which shaded into the blackness of crisply curled hair. There were some of a blackness that shone like rich bits of velvet. There were others whose skins seemed as though made of expertly tanned leather with the creaminess of old vellum, topped by shining hair, blacker than “a thousand midnights, down in a cypress swamp.” And there were those with ivory-white complexions, rare old ivory that time had mellowed with gentle touch. To Mimi the most alluring of all were the women who were neither dark nor light, as many of them were, but those of that indefinable blend of brown and red, giving a richness that was reminiscent of the Creoles of her own New Orleans.

“Look at that girl over there, Aunt Sophie the one with that marvellous black hair and that brownish red skin﻿—the Spanish-looking one with the dress of cornflower blue. There is my ideal of beauty﻿—no sallow, colourless type but the warmth of a thousand Caribbean suns is in her face.”

Mrs. Rogers laughed easily.

“No﻿—you’re right, there is no colourlessness there﻿—but those you see who are so fair they can pass can get ahead lots faster than those with lots of colour. Do you see that girl who looks like white﻿—the one with the black dress covered with passementeries? She works down town in one of the most exclusive shops in New York. She’s a forewoman and makes more money than three-fourths of the men in Harlem.”

“What’s she doing up here?” Mimi asked. “Isn’t she afraid she’ll be seen or that some coloured person will go down and tell she’s a Negro?”

“Nobody’s told on her yet﻿—and when she’s through with her work she comes back to Harlem. She’s got mighty little use for white people﻿—hates them﻿—she was born in Georgia and her brother was lynched when he beat up a white man in a quarrel.”

“Who’s the fellow over there the one with the black hair who looks like a Japanese?” Mimi queried, now thoroughly interested. She scanned the flaming chequer-board of colour, picking out the ones who interested her.

===Chinese===

那是一支华尔兹。各种肤色的脸，从各种色调的裙裾里探出来——色调从娇嫩的粉、蓝、淡紫，一直到浓烈如火的绯红。有的脸是红木的棕，渐渐隐进鬈发的黑色里。有的黑得发亮，像上好的丝绒。有的皮肤像熟鞭的皮革，带着老羊皮纸的乳白，覆着黑亮的头发，比“柏树泽里的一千个午夜”还黑。还有一些，象牙白的脸色，是那种被岁月以温柔的手摩挲得温润了的珍稀老象牙。在米米眼里，最迷人的还不是那些或深或浅的女人——这里这样的人很多——而是那种说不清的红棕交融的脸色，透出一种丰厚，叫她想起自己新奥尔良的克里奥尔人。

“瞧那边那个姑娘，苏菲姨妈——那个一头妙极的黑发、棕红皮肤、西班牙模样的、穿矢车菊蓝裙子的。那才是我的美的理想——不是那种菜色的、没有血色的类型，她脸上有着一千个加勒比太阳的暖。”

罗杰斯太太轻松地笑了。

“不——你说得对，那是不乏色彩——可是你瞧见的那些白到能冒充白人（passing）的，比那些颜色重的前进得快得多。你看见那个长得像白人的姑娘了吗——穿一身缀满珠绦的黑裙子的那个？她在城里纽约最排外的铺子里做工。她当了领班，挣的钱比哈莱姆四分之三的男人都多。”

“那她上这儿来干什么？”米米问。“她不怕叫人瞧见，或者哪个有色人下去说她是个黑人（Negro）？”

“还没有人告发过她——下了工她就回哈莱姆来。她对白人没什么用处——恨他们——她生在佐治亚，她兄弟跟一个白人吵架、把人打了，就叫人私刑绞死了。”

“那边那个家伙是谁——黑头发、长得像日本人的那个？”米米问，这会儿彻底来了兴致。她细细打量这片火焰般的色彩棋盘，把使她感兴趣的一个个挑出来。

===Original===

“Where is he? Oh, that’s Tod James﻿—he’s assistant cashier of a bank down in the financial district. He’s married white but ever so often the call of Harlem is too great and he bobs up here again. He’s had an amazing career﻿—born in Texas, was mixed up in two or three revolutions in Mexico and South America, has travelled all over the world, made and lost three or four fortunes, and now has settled down to a comfortable, placid existence in Wall Street.”

“Does his wife know he’s coloured?”

“That’s she dancing down there with the slender brown chap﻿—looks like she knows what he is. And she was born in Tennessee but had sense enough not to stay there.”

“And who’s the flashily dressed fellow standing there looking on?”

“I don’t know his name but he runs a club where they have entertainers and music for dancing﻿—they call them cabarets. You’ll find all sorts of people here﻿—it’s a pay dance and anybody who’s got the price can get in. There are women who are being kept and men who live the same way, doctors and lawyers, and about every class in the world represented here.”

Mimi felt a thrill of adventure run through her. Perhaps she had brushed against some of these fearful characters and it made her tingle with adventure.

“Goodness, haven’t they any lines they draw?” she wanted to know.

“Strictest in the world﻿—you’ve noticed you’ve been asked to dance only by folks you know and who move with our set.”

Mimi laughed.

“I don’t know about that. See that nice-looking chap over there in that box? I’ve never seen him before and he asked me if I wanted to dance with him.”

Mrs. Rogers peered in the direction of Mimi’s nod.

“No, you haven’t seen him,” she told Mimi grimly, when she had located him. “He’s probably one of the most notorious gamblers in New York﻿—has never been known to do a lick of work. I told you you’d see all sorts of people here, didn’t I?”

===Chinese===

“在哪儿？哦，那是托德·詹姆斯——金融区里一家银行的助理出纳。他娶的是白人，可隔三差五，哈莱姆的召唤太强，他就又在这儿冒头了。他经历惊人——生在得克萨斯，卷进过墨西哥和南美的两三场革命，周游过全世界，挣下又赔掉过三四份家财，如今在华尔街安顿下来，过一份舒坦平静的日子。”

“他太太知道他是有色人吗？”

“那不就是她嘛，在同那个瘦长的棕色小伙子跳——看那样子是知道他是什么。她生在田纳西，可有那个头脑，没待在那儿。”

“那个站在一边看的、穿得花哨的家伙是谁？”

“名字我不知道，可他开一家俱乐部，有艺人表演、有伴舞的音乐——他们管那叫卡巴莱。这儿什么人都有——这是卖票的舞会，出得起票钱的都进得来。有被人包着的女人，也有同样过活的男人，有医生有律师，世上差不多每个阶层这儿都全。”

米米感到一阵探险的战栗窜过全身。说不定她已经同这些可怕人物擦过肩了，这使她兴奋得发麻。

“天哪，他们就分毫界线都不划吗？”她想知道。

“天底下顶顶严的界线——你没留意吗，请你跳舞的，全是认识你的人、同咱们一个圈子的人。”

米米笑了。

“这可说不定。瞧那边包厢里那个俊俏的小伙子？我从没见过他，可他问过我，想不想同他跳。”

罗杰斯太太顺着米米点头的方向眯眼望去。

“不，你没有见过他，”她找到那个人之后，冷冷地告诉米米。“他多半是纽约最出名的赌棍之一——从没人见他做过一天正经营生。我说过这儿什么人都有，是不是？”

===Original===

The music began again and there was a wild rush to the dance floor. Mimi sat and watched the kaleidoscopic scene, brilliant, colourful, fascinating. New York is the only place on earth where such a crowd could be got together, she mused. Like one body the crowd on the huge, densely packed floor swayed and moved with an easy grace, laughing, carefree, exuberant. There was a natural spontaneity to the movement, a rhythmic unity that gave Mimi the impression that the dancers had been rehearsed with great pains by an expert *maître de danse*. They moved with graceful animation, with a decorous but fascinating abandon. Her aunt was speaking.

“It’s lucky for the Negro he was given the ability to forget﻿—there are at least a dozen people down there I’ve recognized who probably don’t know where they’ll get money enough to pay their rent. Yet there’s not a soul having a better time than they. He can work all day methodically and steadily as long as he knows there will be opportunities to forget harsh and unpleasant things.”﻿ ﻿…

Mimi did not see Mrs. Plummer as she descended the stairs when the music began for the next dance. That estimable person had come that afternoon to New York and she now was receiving her first glimpse of New York life. She wore a brilliant purple confection of velvet, adorned with extraordinary little flowers of an indescribable and unknown species and shape. She seized the arm of her cousin, with whom she was staying, and though in the noise there was no need of caution, she whispered:

“Who is that girl with the green dress and red hair﻿—I mean what name does she go by here?”

“That’s Miss Daquin﻿—she’s Mrs. Rogers’ niece and she’s very popular﻿—some of the women don’t like her but that’s because she’s better-looking than most of them. Men like her because she pays no attention to them﻿—”

Mrs. Plummer snorted.

“Miss Daquin! So that’s how she’s getting by, is it?”

She was in her element. New York did not terrify her any more by its hugeness and strangeness. The eyes of her friend picked out Mimi’s form as it appeared and disappeared through the dancing forms. Mrs. Plummer was giving a complete, more than complete, account of Mimi and her life.

===Chinese===

音乐重新响起，人群疯狂地涌向舞池。米米坐着，看这万花筒般的场面，辉煌，多彩，迷人。纽约是天底下唯一能把这样一群人聚到一处的地方，她想。拥挤不堪的大舞池上，那人群像一具身体似的摇曳、移动，轻松优雅，笑着，无忧无虑，兴高采烈。那动作里有一种天然的自在，一种节律的统一，给米米的印象是，这些舞者曾由一位专家舞蹈教师煞费苦心地排练过。他们舞得优雅活泼，带着一种端庄而迷人的忘情。姨妈开口了。

“黑人幸而被赋予了忘却的本事——底下我认出来的就不下十二个，多半不知道下个月的房租从哪儿来。可没有一个人比他们玩得更痛快。只要知道有机会忘掉那些又苦又不痛快的事，他就能成天有条不紊、踏踏实实地做工。”……

下一支舞的音乐奏响时，米米下楼，没有看见普卢默太太。那位可敬的人物当天下午来的纽约，此刻正接受她对纽约生活的第一瞥。她穿一身耀眼的紫色天鹅绒的“作品”，缀着说不清科属、道不明形状的奇花。她一把抓住她借宿的那位表亲的胳膊，尽管吵得根本无须压低声音，她还是咬着耳朵说：

“那个穿绿裙子、红头发的姑娘是谁——我是说，她在这儿顶着什么名字？”

“那是达坎小姐——罗杰斯太太的侄女，人缘好得很——有几个女人不喜欢她，那是因为她比她们大多数都好看。男人们喜欢她，因为她不理他们——”

普卢默太太哼了一声。

“达坎小姐！原来她是这样混的，是吗？”

她如鱼得水了。纽约的庞大和古怪再也吓不住她。她那位朋友的眼睛，把米米的身影在舞动的人影间时隐时现地盯住不放。普卢默太太正在对米米和她的生平作一份完整的、不止完整的交代。

===Original===

“These society coloured folks make me tired,” Mrs. Plummer was saying. “They look down on us ordinary folks but they’re carrying on their deviltry just as much as any of us. And they do some of the craziest things! That girl was carrying on round Atlanta with this Hunter boy and when they found out she was going to have a baby she just went crazy﻿—said she didn’t want to marry the boy! Can you imagine anybody doing such a fool thing?” she demanded rhetorically.

“I always did think there was something funny about her,” her vis-à-vis answered satisfactorily. “You can’t fool me﻿—I can spot ’em a mile off. What became of the baby?”

“The Lord only knows! I heard tell she had it and I don’t know whether it lived or not. Must not of if you say you ain’t never heard about it. Some funny things happen in this world﻿—”﻿ ﻿…

---

On and on the thread was unspun. In the years since Mimi had left Atlanta, the stories about her, with the facile growth that age always brings, had gained almost the proportions of a myth. Never had any girl taken so absurd a position as she. Always they had been only too glad to marry even when they had lived with their husbands but a short time. Not understanding, gossip had assumed there was more to the story than appeared on the surface, and various inventive minds speculated as to the untold part.

Rumour had scoffed at the notion Mimi had refused to marry Carl even though he and his parents were willing. That was too improbable, too farfetched. The Hunters had volunteered no information and tongues had run wild. Some had it that the father of the child was uncertain﻿—every man under seventy years of age who had ever been on the most casual terms of friendship with Mimi was discussed as the possible father. Others were firmly convinced Mimi had trapped Carl with an eye on the wealth which his father was credited with possessing. Those of this persuasion were convinced she had been bought off or frightened by the Hunters.

===Chinese===

“这些上等的有色人真叫我腻味，”普卢默太太说着。“他们看不起我们这些普通人，可他们干起缺德事来，一点也不比我们少。还有些事干得才疯呢！那丫头在亚特兰大跟那个亨特家的小子厮混，等到人家发现她有了身子，她倒发了疯——说她不肯嫁给那小子！你能想象有人干这种蠢事吗？”她自问自答。

“我早就觉得她有点邪性，”对面的那位答得叫她满意。“你糊弄不了我——隔着半里地我就认得出。那孩子后来怎么了？”

“天知道！听说她生了，是死是活我不清楚。照你说的你从来没听说过，那准是没活成。这世上怪事多着呢——”……

---

线越抽越长。米米离开亚特兰大以来，关于她的种种故事，随着年头必然带来的飞快滋长，几乎攒成了神话的规模。从来没有哪个姑娘摆过她那样荒唐的架势。人家哪怕同未婚夫只同居了短短时日，也总是千肯万肯地嫁。因为不理解，闲话便断定这故事内情远不止表面这些，各种富于发明的头脑纷纷揣度那没说出口的部分。

米米连卡尔和他的父母都愿意、她却拒不嫁给卡尔——这个说法被谣言嗤之以鼻。太不像话，太牵强了。亨特家一个字也不肯吐，舌头便撒开了欢。有人说孩子的父亲是谁根本说不准——凡是同米米有过一面之交的七十岁以下的男人，个个被议论成可能的父亲。另一些人深信米米是盯着亨特先生据说有的那笔家财，设局套住了卡尔。持这派意见的断定，她不是叫亨特家收买了，就是叫他们吓住了。

===Original===

Forgotten except by a few, Hilda and her mother among them, were the derelictions popular report had credited to or charged against Carl. Mimi had fled, therefore she was the guilty one. “The wicked fleeth when no man pursueth,” the wise ones said. The scandal was a juicy morsel that served its purpose well over many back fences and at innumerable parties, at church and on front porches. Only Hilda and Mrs. Adams championed the cause of Mimi, but soon, because their views were well known, the subject was not mentioned to them. Scandal thrives only when it is retailed to those with open ears and credulous minds.﻿ ﻿…

---

Mrs. Plummer’s friend was well capable of holding her own in the dissemination of interesting news. Mimi alone would have been a target almost too small for the guns of the talkative ones. But as the niece of Mrs. Rogers, in business, well known, and with enemies gained through the frank comment and eyes with which Mimi’s aunt saw readily through pretence and sham, Mrs. Rogers herself could be hurt through the mud plastered on her niece. Mimi found herself being avoided at church, on the street, at the few affairs which she attended. At first she paid no attention to them but in time they were too obvious to be ignored. She had not seen Mrs. Plummer during the two weeks that she had visited in New York. The first intimation Mimi had of the talk which was going on was given her in a weekly publication, *The Blabber*.

Modelled upon *Town Topics*, it devoted its pages to not too cryptic references, almost invariably scandalous ones, to persons of prominence. Its editors had been handled roughly several times, the paper had been sued frequently, but seldom were any copies left on Harlem newsstands more than a few hours after it appeared on Thursday mornings.

Mimi read the reference to herself three times before she could realize that once again she was confronted with suspicion.

===Chinese===

卡尔名下那些流行的传闻归给他的失检，除了寥寥几个人——希尔达和她母亲在内——谁都不记得了。米米是逃走的，所以有罪的是她。“恶人无人追赶也奔逃”，明白人这样说。这桩丑闻是一块多汁的肥肉，在后院的栅栏边、在数不清的聚会上、在教堂里、在前廊上，好好使唤了许多时日。只有希尔达和亚当斯太太替米米说话，可不久，因为她们的看法人所共知，人家当着她们就不再提这个题目了。丑闻只在说给耳朵张开、心思轻信的人听时，才长得旺。……

---

普卢默太太的那位朋友，在传播有趣新闻上也是一把好手。单是米米一个人，还嫌靶子太小，供不上这帮健谈者的炮口。可她是罗杰斯太太的侄女——姨妈开着铺子，交游广阔，又因说话直率、眼光锐利、一眼看穿矫饰与虚套而结了些仇家——于是往侄女身上糊的泥，就能溅到罗杰斯太太身上。米米发觉自己在教堂里、在街上、在她参加的寥寥几个场合里，被人回避。起初她没有理会，可日子一久，那些做派显眼得没法再装看不见了。普卢默太太来纽约做客的那两个星期，米米没有见到过她。米米对这场正在进行的闲话所得的第一个风声，来自一份星期刊，《碎嘴》。

这份刊物以《城谈》为范本，版面专登对显要人物的影射——不算太隐晦，而且十有八九带秽。它的编辑挨过好几回胖揍，报纸屡吃官司，可每逢星期四早晨出刊，哈莱姆报摊上没过几个钟头就见不着货了。

米米把那段关于她自己的文字读了三遍，才明白过来：她又一次面对着嫌疑了。

===Original===

“All Harlem is agog,” the paragraph ran, “with some hitherto hidden chapters in the life of one of our most attractive and popular young women. She and the relative with whom she lives have been seen often at the smartest affairs and the breath of scandal which has been stirred will undoubtedly prove a nine days’ sensation. Innocence, according to all who know the young lady of French extraction, has been her most evident characteristic. But it seems there has been a coloured gentleman in the firewood. Harlem is asking what the charming miss has done with the baby. Something should really be done about the doors of family closets﻿—they will pop open at the most inopportune moments and permit glimpses of unsuspected and embarrassing skeletons.﻿ ﻿…”

---

“What’s the use, Aunt Sophie, of trying to be decent? I don’t see the need of trying﻿—I might as well give up the struggle and admit I’m beaten,” she despairingly told Mrs. Rogers.

“Don’t be silly, Mimi. They’ve talked about everybody. It’ll all blow over in time.”

“No﻿—you’re just trying to console me. People forget decent things﻿—they never forget a thing like this.”

“There’s something in that, but the people you really care about will have sense enough to see this in a broad light. And those who don’t see it and give you credit for trying to atone for your mistake don’t count﻿—you’ll be better off without their friendship.”

===Chinese===

“整个哈莱姆都在哗然，”那一段写道，“为本城最迷人、最有人缘的年轻女子之一的生平中几章至今隐藏的篇章。有人屡屡看见她同与之同住的那位亲戚出入最时髦的场合，掀起的这口丑闻之风，无疑将成为一场九日轰动的奇谈。凡是认识这位法裔小姐的人都说，天真无邪一向是她最显著的品性。可是柴堆里似乎一直藏着一位有色绅士。哈莱姆正在问：那位迷人的小姐把娃娃怎么处置了。家家那口藏骣髅的柜子，柜门实在该修一修了——它们总在最不合时宜的当口自己弹开，让人瞥见意想不到的、叫人难堪的骣髅。……”

---

“苏菲姨妈，规规矩矩做人还有什么用？我看不出有什么可挣扎的——我索性认输投降得了，”她绝望地对罗杰斯太太说。

“别傻了，米米。谁的闲话他们都说过。日子一久自会烟消云散。”

“不——您不过是想宽慰我。人家正经的事转眼就忘——这样的事永远忘不了。”

“这话也有几分对，可你真正在乎的那些人，会有那个见识，把这件事往宽处看。那些看不清、也不体谅你想弥补过失的人，算不得数——没有他们的交情，你倒更好。”

===Original===

They sat at dinner and Mimi could almost hear the tumbling down of the walls she had so confidently been building. For the first time she felt sick at heart, crushed, without hope. She could withstand poverty, physical pain. But the averted glances of those who had been her friends, the ostentatious turning of heads when she passed on the street those with whom she had been on terms of intimate acquaintanceship, the sudden death of conversations at her approach which told her more plainly than if the information had been shouted through a megaphone that they had been talking of her, the boldness in the eyes of some of the men where there had been only respect before, all these and a thousand other little cruelties were the things which hurt. Hurt her painfully, more painfully than mere physical pain. She envied her aunt’s calmness, her philosophical acceptance of people’s habits, whether these habits were good or bad. She tried to keep her mind on the comforting flow of words with which Mrs. Rogers was seeking to bolster her courage.

“People have gossiped from the beginning of time and I suppose they’ll keep right on until Gabriel blows his trump. I don’t know why it is but there’s one thing that’s always true﻿—they tear you to pieces not for what you’ve done but for getting caught. Take some of the biggest men in America﻿—men who everybody knows have stolen railroads and banks but who’ve never been caught at it﻿—why, they’re popular idols. There are lots of magazines with big circulations that fill their pages with stories of how these giants have gained wealth and fame by ‘honesty, hard work, sticking to the job.’ That’s bunk! Do you remember the story of one of these ‘self-made’ men who was telling a college class that his secret of success was ‘pluck! pluck! pluck!’ and the irreverent boy who asked the pompous one who he had plucked? That’s the true story of a lot of these people who haven’t been caught with their hands in the other fellow’s pockets.”

Mimi smiled a wan smile. This was interesting, perhaps, but it didn’t help her much. Mrs. Rogers went on.

===Chinese===

她们坐着吃晚饭，米米几乎听得见她一直信心十足砌着的那几堵墙轰然倒塌。生平第一次，她心里发呕，被压垮，没了指望。贫穷她顶得住，肉体的疼她顶得住。可是朋友们的回避，走在街上那些曾经过从亲密的人夸张地扭过头去，她一走近谈话便夏然而止——这比用扩音喇叭喊出来还明白地告诉她，方才谈的正是她——还有一些男人，原先眼里只有敬意，如今多了胆气，这一切，还有一千样别的小小的残忍，才是伤人的东西。伤得她疼，比单纯的肉疼疼得多。她艳羡姨妈的镇定，艳羡她对人们习性——无论好习坏习——的哲人般的接受。她努力把心思拴在罗杰斯太太那番安慰话的流水上。

“开天辟地以来人就嚼舌头，我看要一直嚼到加百列吹响号筒。我说不清是什么缘故，可有一条永远是不错的——他们把你撕碎，不是因为你干了什么，而是因为你被抓住了。瞧瞧美国那几个顶大的人物——人人知道他们偷过铁路、偷过银行，可从没当场抓住过——怎么样，还不是大众的偶像。多少销路大大的杂志，版面填满了这些巨头怎样靠‘诚实、苦干、咬定不放’挣下财富名声的故事。全是胡说！你还记得那故事吗——一个这样的‘自我奋斗’的人对一班大学生说，他成功的秘诀是‘魄力！魄力！魄力！’一个没大没小的孩子问那位大人物：您捋的是谁的魄？这许许多多没被人赃并获的家伙，真实的故事就是这一份。”

米米淡淡一笑。这也许有趣，可帮不了她多少忙。罗杰斯太太接着说。

===Original===

“And coloured people are no better than anybody else. The motive is the same everywhere in the world and with all races and sexes and classes. If you aren’t of any importance they don’t gossip about you because nobody’s interested. But they’ve got to have something humiliating, something that’ll bear down a person’s reputation, something malicious and spiteful. It’s got to be about somebody who’s got something to lose, it’ll have to be something that’ll stick, something that’ll be dangerous but not too dangerous for the person who’s gossiping. And above everything else it’s got to be spread with a high and lofty manner﻿—the biggest spreader of tales I ever knew always started her most vile story the same way. She’d say: ‘I stay at home attending to my own business and I don’t meddle in other people’s affairs, but have you heard that﻿—’ and then she proceeds to give you a long account of what some person has done, all of it told in a tone of Christian forbearance and pity and tacit regret.”

“That’s a very interesting and logical analysis but it doesn’t offer much comfort. It’s just like a doctor explaining why your leg broke when you’re lying in a hospital bed.”

“You’re depressed and worried﻿—there’s no need giving in to what these people say. Just face it and it will all blow over. And I’ll stick with you always, Mimi.”

Mrs. Rogers watched Mimi, more worried over the girl’s troubles than she would admit even to herself. In a few months Mimi had become a part and a very important part of the household. Younger people came and she had always preferred associating with young people. “It keeps me young, too,” she would say when the subject was mentioned. The house would be unbearable if Mimi went away or did anything desperate. She feared this though she laughed at her fears. But the set look on Mimi’s face, the despondency which was greater now than when Mimi had first come to New York after putting Petit Jean in the orphanage, the utter dejection so evident in Mimi’s face and body, made her definitely apprehensive.

===Chinese===

“有色人不比谁高。动机在全世界、全种族、全性别、全阶级都一样。你要是个无足轻重的人，人家不嚼你，因为没谁感兴趣。可他们必须有一样丢脸的东西，一样压得下人名声的东西，一样恶毒、歹毒的东西。它必须是关于一个有东西可输的人的，必须是粘得住的，必须危险——又不能危险到嚼舌头的人自己。而比一切更要紧的，是必须拿一副高不可攀的姿态来散布——我认得的最能传话的一个女人，回回开头都是那一套。她会说：‘我待在家里管自己的事，从不管人家的闲事，可是你可听说——’然后她给你长篇大论讲某人干了什么，全用一种基督徒的隐忍、怜悯和默默惋惜的调子讲出来。”

“这分析有趣，也合逻辑，可给不了多少安慰。就像医生给你解释你的腿为什么会断，而你正躺在医院的床上。”

“你是又灰心又发愁——用不着向这些人说的话低头。挺直了面对它，一切自会过去。而且我永远同你站在一起，米米。”

罗杰斯太太望着米米，为这姑娘的烦恼担的心，比她连对自己肯承认的还多。几个月间，米米已成了这个家的成员，而且是要紧的成员。年轻人来来去去，而她一向喜欢同年轻人来往。“这也能叫我年轻，”提起这事时她总这样说。米米若是走了，或者做出什么不顾死活的事，这幢房子就要没法待了。她怕这个，尽管又笑自己这份怕。可是米米脸上那副决绝的神情，那比她把小让送进育幼院、初来纽约时更深的消沉，那写满在米米脸上和身上的全然的颓唐，使她当真不安起来。

===Original===

“You’re a darling, Aunt Sophie, but I just can’t stick it out. I can stand a lot of things I’ve had to﻿—but what’s happened here will just keep on happening every place I go. I’ve tried to go straight﻿—God knows I have. But these meddling people won’t let me have any peace. Do you remember that night at the dance you pointed out a girl to me who was working down town, passing for white?”

Mrs. Rogers admitted she had, but wondered what that had to do with Mimi.

“Just this,” was the answer she received. “I never thought I’d want to leave my own people. I wouldn’t leave them now but they’ve driven me away﻿—driven me to the point where I’ve either got to drop out of sight where I won’t be hounded again or else I’ll do something terrible. If that girl can pass I think I can too. My name is French, I speak French﻿—at least well enough to fool anybody who isn’t French﻿—I can sew, and they’ll never think me anything else but French. I’ll see you, of course, but I’m leaving Harlem, leaving coloured people for good. I’ll live my own life, make more money than I can here, I’ll be able sooner to have Jean with me, and﻿—well, there’s no other way out.﻿ ﻿…”

===Chinese===

“您真是天使，苏菲姨妈，可我实在撑不下去了。许多事我都受得住，也受过了——可这儿发生的事，以后我走到哪儿都会跟着发生。我尽力走正道——天知道我尽了。可这帮多管闲事的人不给我安宁。您还记得舞会那晚吗，您指给我看一个在城里做工、冒充白人的姑娘？”

罗杰斯太太承认记得，却不知道那同米米有什么相干。

“相干的就是这个，”她得到的回答是这样的。“我从没想过要离开自己的族人。如今我也不想离开他们，是他们把我赶走的——把我赶到这个地步：要么彻底隐没，不再被人追猎，要么我就干出可怕的事来。那姑娘既然冒充得了，我想我也冒充得了。我姓法国姓，我会说法语——至少骗过一切不是法国生人的人绰绰有余——我会缝纫，人家只会当我是法国人，绝不会想到别的。我当然会来看您，可我要离开哈莱姆，永远离开有色人。我要过我自己的生活，挣比这儿多得多的钱，更早地把小让接到身边——唉，此外没有别的路了。……”

## XVII / 十七
===Original===

Again Mimi had the feeling she was closing a book in her life and opening a new one. Just like a novel by Rolland, she thought. Or, better, her life to her was like one of those Harlem apartments of seemingly interminable length, with no hall and with each room opening into the next one. Railroad flats, she had heard them called. She felt she was always opening the door of another room, passing through it, then opening and closing behind her, never to be reopened, the door of the next cubicle. Those years of childhood in New Orleans had been the first one, happy and carefree years when she was more content than ever afterwards. The next had been Atlanta, then Philadelphia, then Harlem. Four separate lives, and here was a new one opening before her. She wondered how many more through which she must pass before the final exit. Sometimes now she wished she could skip all the rest and make that last step, wherever it might lead. In her moments of greatest depression she thought of suicide but these morbid thoughts did not remain with her long. Petit Jean and thoughts of his future would have driven them away even if she had not felt a deep aversion to death by her own hand.

From a newspaper advertisement she was able to secure a small room with a private family, living in the lower Nineties, respectable but commonplace. In the matter of linear feet and rods and miles she was not far distant from Harlem but in other ways she might as well have been halfway round the world from the scenes she had once known. Here the towering apartment houses, most of them five or six stories in height and of cream brick and with terra-cotta or stone trimmings, housed countless numbers of families, belching them forth from seven to nine in the morning as small business men and stenographers and manicurists and clerks dashed madly for the nearest subway or elevated station. At the little newsstands they would snatch, according to tastes, copies of the *World* or *Times*, though, more than any other purveyor of the news, they bought issues of “the paper for people who think.” Sleek and well fed, powdered and rouged and lipsticked with expert hand, they were swallowed up by the yawning and insatiable pits and were whirled to downtown jobs by the rushing, roaring subways.

===Chinese===

米米又一次感到，她正合上生平的一本书，翻开新的一本。就像罗曼·罗兰的一部小说，她想。或者不如说，她的生平在她就像哈莱姆那种一眼望不到头的公寓，没有走廊，一间屋子通着下一间。她听人管那叫“火车厢套间”。她觉得自己总在开一扇又一扇房门，穿过一间，再开了下一间小屋的门、随手关上，永不再开。新奥尔良度过的童年是第一间，那是一生中后来再没有过的快活无忧的岁月。第二间是亚特兰大，然后费城，然后哈莱姆。四段各自分立的人生，此刻又有一段新的在她面前打开。她不知道自己还得穿过几间，才到最后的出口。有时她但愿能跳过其余所有的，一步跨到最后那一步，不管它通向哪里。最沮丧的时刻她想到过自杀，可这些病态的念头在她心里待不长。即便她不曾对亲手结束生命怀有深深的厌恶，小让和对他的前途的念头也自会把它们赶走。

凭报纸上一则广告，她在一户人家那里租到一个小间，那家住在九十街下段，体面，却平常。论英尺、杆、英里，她离哈莱姆不远，可从别的方面说，她同自己一度熟悉的那些地方，还不如隔着半个地球。这一带的公寓高楼，多数五六层，奶油色砖墙，配着陶瓦或石头镶边，装着数不清的人家，一到早晨七点到九点，便把他们喷吐出来：小生意人、速记员、修指甲师、店员，疯狂扑向最近的地铁或高架车站。在小报摊上，他们按各人口味抢一份《世界报》或《时报》，不过买得最多的，还是那份“给会思考的人看的报纸”。他们油光水滑、营养充足，粉打得、胭脂抹得、口红涂得一手好功夫，随后便被那些打哈欠的、填不满的深坑吞下，被飞奔轰鸣的地铁卷进城里的差事里去。

===Original===

Later in the day swarms of children poured forth from the same apartments, in winter bound schoolward, in summer to the nearest park or the street, chaperoned by quarrelsome mothers or older sisters. Yet later came elaborately coiffured and gowned young women, invariably pretty, who toiled not nor spun. Evening came and the Gargantuan beehives reversed the process and swallowed again the hordes they had spewed forth in the morning hours. Phonographs blared raucously, voices were raised in babels of song or quarrelling or loud laughter. Midnight, and the din died down. Sleep, engulfing and obliterating as a candle-snuffer, brought peace, broken only when a late and noisy party was quieted momentarily by sleepy, irritated voices shouting: “Hey, cut out the noise down there!”

It was in one of these huge rabbit warrens that Mimi found shelter. The house she lived in looked down upon most of the others in the block﻿—it boasted in the entrance hallway rows of dusty but imposing palms, set in tubs painted a bilious green, and between these tropic relics stood uncomfortable-looking chairs of imitation needlepoint. No one seemed aware of the purpose of these chairs, which always reminded Mimi of the photographs she had seen of the electric chair at Sing Sing, for not even the oldest inhabitant of the house, who had been living there now for a year and a half, had ever seen anyone sitting in them. But they added class, tone, éclat. And they likewise added a few dollars to the rent of each apartment in the house so adorned.

===Chinese===

过些时候，成群的孩子从同样的公寓里倾泻出来，冬天裹向学校，夏天涌向最近的公园或街头，由爱吵架的母亲或姐姐们押着。再晚些，出来的是发式精巧、长裙曳地的年轻女子，个个漂亮，却既不纺也不织。夜晚来临，这些巨人般的蜂巢倒转了程序，把清晨喷出的那群人重新吞回去。留声机刺耳地喧响，人声鼎沸，有歌有吵有大笑。午夜，喧嚣沉寂。睡眠像烛罩一样罩下来，吞没一切、抹去一切，带来安宁，只偶或被打破——哪个开得晚、闹得凶的派对，被睡意惺忪、火气冲冲的喊声暂且压一压：“喂，楼下别吵了！”

米米避难的，正是这样一个巨大的兔子窝中的一座。她住的那幢楼俯瞰着这一街区大多数别的楼——门厅里摆着几盆落满灰尘却气势逼人的棕榈，栽在漆成胆汁绿的木桶里，棕榈之间立着几把仿针绣面的椅子，一副坐上去不会舒服的样子。这些椅子的用途谁也说不上来，米米总觉得它们像她见过的新新监狱电椅的照片，因为连楼里住得最久的那位——住了一年半的老住户——也从没见人坐过。可它们添了派头，添了格调，添了气派。它们同样也给这幢楼里每套公寓的房租添了几块钱。

===Original===

She had not wanted to leave Harlem. Mrs. Rogers had argued, pleaded, almost wept. At times Mimi had been on the point of weakening, had almost thought seriously of trying to stick it out. Each time that she had almost convinced herself that this was the wisest and only course, some new evidence of the ordeal would come to her and make her vow anew that she would not attempt to live it down. Years before she had heard a story which Booker Washington had told in which he likened Negroes to a basket of crabs when one of them had with great energy climbed almost to the top of the basket and freedom, the others less progressive than he would reach up with their claws and pull him back to their own level. This had seemed to her then merely an effective story coined for oratorical purposes, but now its applicability was forced upon her with painful truth.

Though she had to a considerable degree recovered from the intense consciousness of race which her experiences in the riot at Atlanta had engendered in her, yet Mimi, despite the shortcomings which she saw in her own people, had a loyalty to and an affection for them that was almost an obsession. She never spoke of this feeling, for she felt that to those who understood, explanations were unnecessary, while to those who needed an explanation, whatever she might say would have been obscure and difficult of understanding. In other words, to her it was as useless for her to attempt the depiction of her loyalty as it would be for a man to maintain loudly and persistently the faithfulness of his wife. She had always felt an instinctive distrust of those Negroes who boasted of their loyalty and devotion to their race. It had seemed to her that if such faithfulness were genuine and unselfish it needed restatement no more than did the affirmation by a woman that she was respectable.

===Chinese===

她本不想离开哈莱姆。罗杰斯太太争过、求过，几乎哭过。有几次米米险些软了下来，当真认真想过咬牙撑过去。每一次就在她几乎说服自己这才是最明智、唯一的路时，那场磨难总有新的凭据送上门来，使她重新发誓决不去硬熬。多年以前，她听过布克·华盛顿讲的一个故事，他把黑人比作一篓子螃蟹：一只好不容易力气十足地爬到篓子顶上、眼看就要自由，其余那些不如它进取的，便伸出钳子把它拉回自己那一层。那时她只当这是为演讲编的一则妙喻，如今它的贴切却带着刺人的真实压到她身上。

亚特兰大暴乱的经历在她心里种下的强烈的种族意识，虽已褪去大半，可米米尽管看得分明自己族人的短处，对他们仍怀着一份近于执念的忠与爱。这份感情她从不出口，因为她觉得，懂得的人无须解释，需要解释的人，她说什么也是晦涩难懂。换句话说，在她看来，想描绘自己的忠诚，就像一个男人到处大声坚称自己老婆贞洁一样无用。对那些夸口忠于种族、热爱种族的黑人（Negro），她始终怀着一种本能的不信任。她一向觉得，这份忠心倘若是真的、无私的，就用不着反复申说，正如一个女人用不着申明自己正派。

===Original===

Thus her passing from the race seemed to Mimi persecution greater than any white people had ever visited upon coloured people﻿—the very intolerance of her own people had driven her from them. And in the deception she would have to practise as one ostensibly white, she felt she was doing a mean and dishonourable thing. She would do so, she determined, for there was no other course open to her. But in her new life she missed the spontaneity, the ready laughter, the naturalness of her own. She saw morose, worried faces. Here there was little of that softness of speech to which she was accustomed. Here there was an obsession with material things that crowded out the naturalness that made life for her tolerable. I’ve made my bed now, she reflected, and there’s nothing for me to do except lie in it.﻿ ﻿…

---

She did not experience the difficulty in securing employment that she had feared. There had been vague rumblings of discord and war in Europe, but all that seemed very far away, too distant ever to affect America. The mad rush that ushered in Easter and spring, the return like homing pigeons of the wealthy from Palm Beach, the momentary pause in New York before taking wing again for Europe or the mountains or the seashore, taxed to the limit the shops of the modistes and milliners, the makers of boots and gowns and habiliments of a thousand kinds for those vaguely classed as “society.”

Deft fingers flew, the needles of sewing-machines flashed in and out like pelting raindrops of silver in a summer shower. Yards and bolts and tons of filmy silks and crêpes de Chine and organdie turned overnight into dainty dresses. A long, nastily cold winter was gasping its last long breath and little wisps of softer breezes fanned the cheeks of the scurrying throngs, telling them spring was near.

===Chinese===

这样，她之冒充白人（passing）脱离种族，在米米看来，是比白人加给有色人的任何迫害都重的一场迫害——把她从族人中间赶出去的，正是族人的不容。而冒充白人要行的那些欺瞒，她觉得自己是在做一件卑鄙、不名誉的事。她会做的，她拿定了主意，因为再没有别的路向她敞开。可是在新生活里，她想念族人的率真、说来就来的笑、自然。她看见的是愁苦、忧烦的脸。这里少有她听惯的那份软语温言。这里有一种对物质的痴迷，把那份使生活还能忍受的自然，挤了出去。床是我自己铺的，她想，如今只好躺上去。……

---

她没有遇到她担过的那种找工作的难处。欧洲那时节已有隐隐的失和与战争的风雷，可那一切显得很远，远得永远波及不到美国。迎接复活节和春天的疯狂抢购，阔人们像归巢的鸽子从棕榈滩飞回来，在纽约作片刻停留、再振翅飞往欧洲或山间海滨——这一切把女装店、女帽店、靴子长裙和千百样行头的铺子，榨到了极限，那些主顾被含含糊糊地归作“上流社会”。

巧手如飞，缝纫机的针像夏日骤雨的银雨点进进出出地闪。成码成匹成吨的轻薄丝绸、双绉、蝉翼纱，一夜之间变成一件件精巧的裙子。一个又长又讨厌的寒冬咽下最后一口长气，一缕缕柔软的风拂着匆匆人潮的脸颊，告诉他们春天近了。

===Original===

The unforeseen demand for new and more clothes brought with it the need of workers. Mimi, having set her face on the new life she had chosen, determined she would start at the smartest places in seeking employment and work gradually downwards until she had gained what she wished. She knew the names and addresses of the most exclusive modistes, the shops where gowns and frocks were purveyed to the smartest-dressed women of New York and Oshkosh and Sioux City. To her surprised delight she was successful at the first place to which she applied﻿—at Francine’s﻿—known wherever the best women’s clothes were known.

Francine’s name was greeted with that tone of respectful homage granted only to the names of Paquin and Lucile and Worth and Bendel and Jean Patou and Frances. At Francine’s one entered into an atmosphere where milady’s attire, the choice of material and model, the selection of millinery, was raised to the plane of a religious ceremony. Soft-voiced priestesses respectfully assisted in the rites, making suggestions in tones which were not too respectful nor yet too haughty. Purchasers of models from Francine, even though they had bought there for many years, were never allowed to forget through subtle yet unmistakable means that they were after a fashion being shown especial favour when they were permitted to enter Francine’s door.

Madame Francine herself was the high priestess of the temple of feminine adornment. Only the most favoured customers saw her or were served by her after their first visit. If that customer were of great distinction either through family connections or wealth, she might be allowed the honour of Madame’s personal service. But if she were of less than the topmost bough of society or if the wealth she represented was expressed in less than seven figures, then she never saw Madame Francine after her initial entrance into the temple.

===Chinese===

对衣裳的这阵预料之外的需求带来了用工的需要。米米既已把脸转向自己选定的这新生活，便拿定主意从最时髦的铺子应聘起，一路往下，直到求得所要的。那些顶顶排外的女服店的名字地址她都知道，那些铺子把长裙短服卖给纽约——以及奥什科什、苏城——穿得最讲究的女人们。叫她又惊又喜的是，她头一家应聘就成了——在弗朗辛家，凡是认得最好的女装的地方，没有不认得这名字的。

弗朗辛这名字所到之处，人必以恭而敬之的声调相迎，那种声调只献给帕坎、露西尔、沃斯、本德尔、让·帕图、弗朗西丝这几个名字。进了弗朗辛家，便进入一种氛围：夫人的衣装、料子与款式的挑选、帽子的拣选，都被抬到了宗教仪式的高度。嗓音柔软的女祭司们恭恭敬敬地襄赞典礼，提着建议，那声调既不过于谦卑，也不过于倨傲。来弗朗辛家买款式的主顾，哪怕买了几十年，也永远不许忘记——通过种种微妙而明确无误的手段——她们被容许踏进弗朗辛的门，多少算是受了特别的恩典。

弗朗辛夫人本人，便是这座女性妆饰之殿的大祭司。只有最受优待的主顾，头一回去过之后才见得着她、由她亲自伺候。倘若那主顾门第显赫或财富惊人，或许能荣蒙夫人亲自效劳。可她若够不上社会这棵树的顶梢，或者她名下的财富不足七位数，那么初进殿门之后，她就再也没见过弗朗辛夫人。

===Original===

For Madame Francine knew the value of a stage setting much better than most of the theatrical producers whose lights flashed nightly a few blocks westward. She knew that the place for her temple was then to be only in the upper Thirties or lower Forties, just a step from the Avenue. She knew the supreme importance of keeping from the transaction of bartering every least suggestion of buying and selling. She knew the necessity of proper first impressions upon the purchaser, and of maintaining that impression.

One entered her place after a magnificently apparelled doorman had opened one’s carriage or motorcar door. Inside, one found oneself in what in reality was a salon, free of course from every tiniest suspicion of vulgarity, of trade. The carpet was of black and grey, the woodwork of a grey in which there was the faintest suggestion of tan, the draperies were of grey and mulberry. The shaded lights from the wall sconces and ceiling gave no harsh glare, the illumination furnished being only just enough to combine the needed air of respect for the wonders beyond and above and the light necessary for the rites of conversation in lowered tones. Chairs and couches of delicate build matched or purposely contrasted with the colours in rug and draperies. Soft-voiced, slim and perfectly gowned priestesses glided noiselessly forward to greet one and inquire one’s wants as though being welcomed into the home of the priestess﻿—welcomed, perhaps, not by the mistress of the home, but by a poorer relation of the mistress who lived there. On an onyx table there was perhaps a hat perched upon a stand. Or in a softly lighted built-in case there rested a rarely exquisite and beautiful purse. Or a bottle, richly carved, of perfume. Or a bit of gold or silver filigree, of amber and lapis from a dim little shop on the Ponte Vecchio. Or a miraculously carved comb for milady’s coiffure. In solitary splendour it reposed﻿—its grandeur not a whit less than that accorded a string of matchless pearls in one of the temples devoted to jewels in the Avenue nearby.

And when the visitor had made her wants known she was conducted, if her need was of gowns or tailored suits or hats, up the winding marble stairway to the miniature theatre on the floor above. She was aided in the ascent by the balustrade of metal covered with mull velvet.

===Chinese===

因为弗朗辛夫人深谙舞台布景的价值，比西区几个街区外夜夜闪着灯的多数戏剧制作人高明得多。她知道她的殿宇那时只能设在三十几街上段或四十几下段，离大道只一步。她知道顶顶要紧的是让每一桩交易里嗅不出半点买卖的气味。她知道给主顾留下合宜的第一印象是何等必要，又知道怎样维持这个印象。

先是一位服饰华丽的司阍为你拉开马车或汽车的车门，你才进得了她的店。进得门来，你发觉自己置身之处实际上是一间沙龙，当然，浑身上下没有一丝一毫俗气、一丝一毫市侩。地毯是黑灰二色，木作是一种灰色，灰里隐隐透一点棕，帷幔是灰与桑葚色。壁灯和顶灯的光不刺眼，那照明刚刚够——既给楼上楼里的奇珍维持必要的敬意，又够压低嗓门谈话的典礼之用。纤细的椅子和躺椅，同地毯帷幔的颜色或相配，或有意相对。嗓音柔软、身条苗条、衣着无可挑剔的女祭司们悄然无声地滑上前来迎你，问你的来意，仿佛你被迎进的是女祭司的家——迎客的也许还不是这家的主妇，而是寄居在这里的主妇的一位穷亲戚。一张玛瑙桌上，也许端放着一顶帽子，套在架上。或是在灯光柔柔的壁柜里，安卧着一只精美绝伦的手袋。或是一瓶雕工繁丽的香水。或是一点金银细丝，一点琥珀和青金石，来自老桥上一家幽暗的小铺。或是一把雕得如有神助的梳子，梳夫人的发髻。它们孤零零地安卧着，光华不让左近大道上那些珠宝殿堂里一串举世无双的明珠。

来客说明来意之后，若要的是长裙、套装或帽子，便被引上盘旋的大理石楼梯，到楼上那座小小的剧院去。攀梯时，有包着绒面呢的金属扶栏搭一把手。

===Original===

Madame Francine’s sense of the appropriate and impressive setting rose to its heights in the room she entered at the top of these stairs. Here the rugs were of grey, the woodwork and walls and draperies blending unobtrusively in the colours of the showroom below. On a marble mantel to the left stood in solemn dignity a marvellously fashioned clock, so beautifully made, one felt it almost a profanation that it should measure unimportant things like minutes and hours. To each side of it there stood a delicately carved candlestick of shiny brass, their brightness matching the framework of the long table with marble top on which rested sketchbooks of designs and models. The tiny stage fitted with footlights and drops, its flooring of squares of black and white marble, glowed with impressive and anticipatory dignity. Daintily clad and perfumed and manicured ladies lolled at ease upon divans or in straight-backed chairs, according to tastes and sizes, while mannequins pirouetted and posed and paraded around and across the stage. Every detail had been minutely scrutinized, every bit of unobtrusive allure had been utilized to induce the dawdling audience to the point of conviction and purchase. Instinctively one lowered one’s voice﻿—the temple must not be profaned.
